on behalf of the s&d group. – mr president, commissioner, colleagues, i don’t know whether there are many people or there were many people in this room who would have believed that it was possible to build an aircraft that can fly around the world in one go solely on solar panels. well, science companies, together with bertrand piccard, have done that. and how did they do it? they tried to get energy on the aeroplane as low as possible and, as it were, threw away everything that was not necessary. so you had a very energy-efficient aeroplane and then it could fly on solar. and that is the philosophy which we need to apply when we vote on wednesday. make sure that we have a very high level of energy efficiency, and then it is possible to achieve a high level of renewables, of which in the long run – though not too long – we will need 100%. that is why i am very proud of my political group with two excellent rapporteurs on energy efficiency and renewables. we fought for the 40%, and i still hope that we can achieve a majority in parliament in favour because this not only goes together with our achievements and our commitment to the paris agreement, but it is also something that can boost our economy, create jobs and ensure that energy poverty is tackled – which is also something very important to my political group. finally, the transition to a zero-carbon economy must avoid false solutions. we cannot accept solutions which are not contributing to real greenhouse gas reductions or, by doing a sort of collateral damage, have a negative effect on our natural environment and our biodiversity. so it is very important that we all agree on palm oil – ok – but we must also be agreed on forests and on the fact that we cannot use stamps and round wood as a source of green energy; i hope we can find a majority on that very important issue as well. in writing. the adoption of the regulation on technical measures for the common fisheries policy is an opportunity to ban electric fishing in the european union. this technique currently benefits from a derogation which, since 2007, allows fishing with electric trawls on an experimental basis within a maximum of 5% of the perch trawler fleet of each european union country operating in the north sea. the original experimental motive, now largely obsolete, is fallacious because almost the entire beam trawler fleet has since been converted to electricity. this technique, which is banned in most of the world (many countries including the united states, brazil, uruguay and china have banned it after serious environmental consequences were observed), is dangerous for the ecosystem, a disgrace to europe and discredits us on the international stage. more needs to be done to develop, support and encourage sustainable activities rather than to fuel a controversial practice. thank you.
member of the commission. – madam president, i apologise to the honourable members at the beginning, but there were so many questions and i will take a bit longer, because i do not like to be criticised as we are always very open. first i would like to thank parliament for giving me this opportunity to talk about these specific measures for greece which we adopted together, as we said, in october 2015. i am very grateful to the committee on regional development and in particular to the chair, mrs mihaylova, and to mr arimont for their continuous support in promoting these measures. i would also like to thank the committee on budgetary control (cont) – they are not here – for their constant interest, in particular in implementing the priority project list, as we said, which was launched in 2011 by my predecessor, commissioner hahn. as you know, we have together adopted extraordinary measures for extraordinary times, as you said, madam president. the impact of these specific measures in favour of greece shows to what extent cohesion policy has played a decisive role in relaunching the greek economy. they are also a vivid expression of the solidarity of cohesion policy, and i hereby confirm that the commission stands by the greek authorities to assist them in boosting jobs and growth. i think it is important to recall, first of all, the extraordinary nature of these specific measures. as you well know, in 2015 the commission proposed to amend the common provision regulation because greece was desperately in need of liquidity. the purpose of this revision was to frontload existing eu funds for investment in growth-enhancing projects with two objectives in mind: to help implement the 2007-2013 programmes and to kick-start investments under the new programming period. in total, approximately eur 2 billion were paid to greece in 2015 and 2016. this included eur 1 billion for the 2007-2013 period, linked to two measures: first, the increase of the eu cofinancing rate to 100%, which meant that no national contribution was needed; second, the early release of the last 5% of remaining eu payments, normally released only at the closure of the programmes. to maximise the impact of these additional amounts, greece undertook the commitment to channel them via a ring—fenced account. the other eur 1 billion was made available by increasing the rate of initial pre-financing for 2014—2020 programmes. measuring the impact of the specific measures on the completion of individual projects is not an easy task, as cohesion policy assistance is provided at programme level. but what is certain is that without these measures there would have been many more uncompleted projects and greece would have had serious trouble financing their completion. the report provided by the greek authorities indeed shows that, thanks to these specific measures, the number of uncompleted projects was drastically reduced for 2007-2013, and only 79 projects are yet to be completed out of more than 50 000. one of the most striking examples is the completion of the five highway concessions. the liquidity provided for the specific measures was a prime contributor to their finalisation. regarding the priority project list, i believe that the outcome of the exercise has been satisfactory. it includes over 180 projects in a wide range of sectors. to date, close to 120 projects have been completed, while 18 projects are being completed with national funds. 24 projects are being continued in the current period. the whole mechanism has worked as a trigger for the greek government and local and regional authorities to take ownership and action when projects were not moving ahead as they should. overall these specific measures have brought greece back on track for the benefit of the greek citizens. the high amount of liquidity injected in a short period of time relaunched investments in the greek real economy. according to the 2017-2018 edition of the global competitiveness index, greece scores particularly well on infrastructure and ranks 38th out of 137. as regards the increased competitiveness of greece, when we compare the 2017-2018 edition of the global competitiveness index with the 2012—2013 one, greece has improved notably in own infrastructures, technological readiness and innovation. these are typically sectors for which structural funds have provided extensive support in the past decades. these measures were extraordinary. i think they are the epitome of what cohesion policy should be: solidarity in action and not only in words. it is a policy that delivers, adapts to circumstances, helps concretely and has a tangible impact on the daily lives of millions of europeans. i would like to conclude by underlining that the commission will continue to provide all the information to parliament. i am ready to hear your point of view and to respond.
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe group. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat fraud. in summary, this is about the two billion euros that greece received in 2015 to be able to invest in projects faster and easier. we did so at the time because it was a special case and we also took a special measure - only on the condition that parliament could also exercise a special control, a control over whether the money was used efficiently and whether the projects contributed to the economic recovery of greece. i have been waiting for this report from the greek government since march 2017. i have written to the commission on several occasions, to the greek government, and it was only on 4 december that i received a version of this report, in which i must make an assessment, in greek. i don't speak that language and i expected to be told in a language i understand and can evaluate. i have been waiting for this until today and i am glad that we are doing this interpellation today, because today oh miracle i finally received this text in german. i regret this, firstly because the citizens of europe have a right to know whether this money has been spent correctly, and secondly because i would also like to use this report to prove and demonstrate that cohesion policy is important, that it represents a european added value and that it should continue beyond 2020. answer to a question on the 'blue card' procedure. i can only answer that question: firstly, the absorption rate in greece seems to be high, and if i use the word 'seems', it is because i did not actually receive this report, or in a language i do not know. and i cannot, at this stage, give a final assessment of whether the absorption rate is really that high, whether the money has gone into sensible projects, whether it has contributed to an economic upswing. i have heard the commissioner's comments. she told us that was all right. i would like to read it once, with figures, facts and arguments from the greek government. until i have that, or until i have this report in the language i understand, i cannot give a concrete answer to that question.

mr president, commissioner, while the reflection paper presented by the commission for the chapter on sustainable development was supposed to be a neutral document, the option you have clearly chosen is to continue with an ineffective model, which you certainly want to improve, but which is ineffective nonetheless. a combination of the two approaches, namely the strengthening of dialogue accompanied by sanctions, is a perfectly compatible and, above all, desirable proposal. furthermore, the discussions we have had in the framework of ceta, in which you have committed yourself to reviewing the chapter 'sustainable development', clearly show that this chapter, as provided for in the current trade agreements, does not meet our expectations in terms of respect for social and environmental rights. the european parliament has repeatedly asked the commission to do so, i.e. to provide for sanctioning mechanisms. in its opinion on singapore, the european court of justice also stated that the 'sustainable development' chapter is intended to govern trade between the union and its partners by making the liberalisation of trade conditional on the parties respecting their international obligations in the fields of social protection, workers' protection and the environment. it also added that a party is entitled to terminate or suspend the liberalisation of such trade in the event of a breach by one of the parties of provisions on social, labour and environmental protection. the credibility of the european commission, which is seriously committed to this policy of sustainable development, is therefore at stake. if we want to convince people of our responsibility in this matter ... (the president withdrew the floor)
in writing. the european union is obliged to respect human rights, workers' rights and environmental standards in its trade policy. the inclusion of sustainable development chapters in its trade agreements is therefore a very good development. however, the enforceability of these chapters leaves much to be desired. these cannot be hardened at present, as there is no system of sanctioning in case of non-compliance. the great danger is that it will be reduced to fine words and that good intentions regarding working conditions, respect for environmental standards and human rights will remain dead letters. there is no strong incentive for the eu's trading partners to comply with these provisions. i call on the european commission to adopt binding provisions on human rights, workers' rights and environmental standards. binding provisions, with sanctions if our trading partner violates the provisions. this also requires close monitoring of effective compliance. in this way trade with countries where workers' rights and environmental concerns are being questioned will no longer be tolerated. this chapter will then no longer exist merely formally, but will also be an effective way of strengthening more sustainable trade.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, taoiseach, i think that our european union can learn a lot from ireland. when i say that, i am not talking about the weather forecast, naturally. i am talking about what i call the irish capacity to transform and to reinvent itself, as has happened over the last decades. i think that is exactly what we need in europe today: this capacity to transform ourselves and to reinvent ourselves – and not to destroy ourselves, as some of our colleagues want. thirty years ago, ireland was a very different country. i think only 7% were born abroad. today, that figure is more than 70%. i think that ireland today has become one of the most diverse nations of the european union. the irish economy has successfully integrated migrants from all over the world – from poland, from nigeria. ireland has also become the home country for a number of big companies, such as international it giants. today, ireland is no longer a country of emigration; it has become a destination country and also a country with many pro-europeans – sometimes very critical but always constructive, and that is absolutely key. what i hope to see in this debate is that ireland can show the same european leadership as happened in the past. let me remind you that in 1985/1986 it was garret fitzgerald, your predecessor, who gave the final support to the single european act. that support was crucial, because it was the first time that ireland broke away from the british position and made it possible to push forward the european agenda. it is the single european act that later resulted in the single market, and that is now seen as the biggest european achievement. even margaret thatcher found it the biggest achievement of european politics. the same thing happened in 2004 and 2007. after the enlargement of the european union, it was ireland who championed, in fact, the freedom of movement of all people in the european union, including the polish plumber and the romanian doctor. i hope that ireland continues to have such a commitment to reform the european union now. your voice is critical in this debate. i see four things to do. the first is the new governance for the eurozone. the second is the european defence union. the third is the new european asylum and migration policy. we have to get rid of dublin – not of joyce’s city, naturally, but the dublin regulation on migration. we need to have something completely different, where italy and greece do not bear all the burden. finally, i think we also need a deep democratic reform, with a transnational democracy. i thank you for your support on that important reform, which i hope we can achieve in the coming weeks. lastly – and the president of the commission is right – we need a budget that goes in parallel with this. it is impossible to say to the citizens: ‘look, citizens, you’re right: we need more this and we need more that; we need more european action’ but, at the same time, we do not give the necessary instruments and the necessary budget. finally, president, i think there is a new generation of politicians in europe who are standing up. you need to know one thing. it is the last thing i am going to say, and it is on brexit. whatever stance you take, taoiseach, europe will always be behind you. we know what is at stake in these brexit negotiations. we know how existential it is for you, but know that we will always be behind you. in these negotiations, we are all irish, and you have to know that.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, it is a real pleasure to welcome the prime minister. taoiseach, you have demonstrated again in your speech the depth of your country’s and your own commitment to the european project. we all got familiar with your name on the occasion of the brexit debate, and ireland is obviously the member state that will be most affected by it – not just for economic reasons but because this is about peace on the island. let me just say this: we are at your side on this. letting violence erupt again in ireland will not just spell disaster for the communities concerned, but it would it would be an arrow through europe’s heart because the european union is, and remains, a peace project. brexit is a challenge we could have avoided, but it should not detract us from those challenges that are existential to the future of europe. allow me, taoiseach, to mention two of them that i find particularly relevant to ireland. first, all societies need to find ways to live within the biophysical constraints that nature sets us. there is simply no way around it. europe has a choice here – either to become leaders in the ecological transition of all societies, thereby becoming solution providers to the rest of the world – or to stick with the past, thereby becoming solution takers from the rest of the world. from a young, entrepreneurial prime minister such as you, i would expect the first option. yet, taoiseach, your government has been sorely lacking ambition on tackling climate change. you said when you became prime minister that climate action would be your first priority. yet it doesn’t really show. this year ireland was ranked as europe’s worst performer in tackling climate change. it is one of only two countries in the eu that will miss its 2020 emission reduction targets. and now your government seeks special deals and exemptions from the quite modest 2030 targets. on a similar line, prime minister, your government opposes the waste reduction bill proposed by the irish greens, which only aims at bringing your country on a par with best practices across europe on the way to a more circular economy. come on, taoiseach, with its abundant resources for renewable energy, ireland has the potential to be a pioneer in green transformation. your country and europe’s prosperity depend on it. you will hardly be surprised by the second topic that i want to raise which is, of course, taxation. if we want our single market and single currency to thrive and to deliver benefits to all and not just the richest, we need to stop this race to the bottom on taxation and on social protection. and you know that ireland often comes to the fore as a tax haven for corporates. of course, you would argue – and i would agree with you – that several member states offer real taxation rates to multinationals that are way lower than the irish nominal 12.5%. and you would say that ireland should be allowed to find ways to make up for geographical disadvantage. i agree with all of this, but surely the alternative cannot be between a one—size—fits—all taxation system that would work for the central big and core european countries to the detriment of everyone else and the no—holds—barred tax competition that we have right now. instead of playing hold—out, instead of fighting european commission decisions in court, i would expect ireland to be at the core of efforts to better cooperate on tax matters in order to give tax justice a chance in europe. it is not just our excessive ecological footprint, but also ballooning inequalities that threaten the very existence of all civilised societies. taoiseach, the european project is about establishing a lasting peace on our continent through shared prosperity on the one hand and, on the other, through anchoring our societies in the values of freedom, democracy and human rights. i am very glad that your government wants to ensure the right of women to control their own bodies. i hope that the upcoming referendum will allow your country to make progress in that direction at a time where we see progress in the other direction in too many parts of europe. taoiseach, if you can deploy the same leadership on ecological transition and on tax justice as the one that you have deployed here, you will have done a lot for the european union. i am sure you can do it. the president, the commissioner, the members of the commission, we all know. it is the young, my generation, who have paid the highest price for the crisis. it was and rightly is a top priority of this legislature. europe has really tried to make the youth guarantee and the youth employment initiative a success and it has worked. in my country, too. youth unemployment in brussels fell for the 55th consecutive year. but still, youth unemployment at 24% remains far too high. why is that? the economy is going to go into a tipping point. employers are looking for the right talent and my government is also juggling figures on the amazing job creation. the problem, however, is the mismatch. however good our intentions, the real target group - the unemployed young people who are not in education - is still not reached. that is why i support the call for more data and more results-oriented work from the member states. show us where the money goes and deliver quality too. every 24-year-old deserves the chance to have a real, decent job and i am glad that the commission is going to look at that in the future. ladies and gentlemen, the debate on the multiannual budget for the period after 2020 is already about savings. well i tell you plainly: let's not let young people be a thrift store. on the contrary, more resources will be needed, for example for youth employment, erasmus+ and other programmes. the european social pillar is a compass. let us use that compass well towards the future, towards youth. thank you, sir.
i should like to thank the rapporteur for this balanced report. however, it is not a good idea to make the youth employment initiative a permanent eu funding instrument. the causes of youth unemployment vary widely from one member state to another. a uniform approach is therefore impossible. it is more sensible to encourage the member states and the regions to adopt structural policies tailored to their own labour markets. but this initiative is doing just the opposite. it takes away the incentive for the regions concerned to change course. the large-scale eu funding masks the structural problems underlying youth unemployment. i see a clear example of this in belgium. wallonia and the brussels region receive funds from the youth employment initiative. these young people then disappear in training courses and from the unemployment statistics and can bring a good news show to these regions. i regret that these regions are not thus obliged to implement the much needed structural reforms. the youth initiative is just a cheap cover for the bleeding. what a waste of money!
on behalf of the s&d group, when frans timmermans is there, i always take the opportunity to speak in my own language. that's a little annoying for the other commissioner, but he can listen to the interpretation. let me start by saying "thank you, big congratulations". it's about time, but you're doing it. you come up with a strategy around plastic, and i think we're the only major region in the world today that's taking that responsibility. and by doing so, we set the standard for the rest of the world. that's very important! we're going to support you in that. we are also looking particularly forward to the legislative proposals, because soft legislative strategies and awareness-raising are all well and good, but we also need legislation. i am thinking of the working party on single-use plastics, but also of the legislation on port reception facilities. both are very important and parliament will certainly support them. there's also going to be an investigation into a tax on single-use plastics. that is a very important item in many of our member states. our group wants to support that. i think that's a good way to tax plastic. what we do not agree with is the use of this tax as a kind of own revenue for the union, because this is a tax that you do not want to collect, which must go to zero. and when we talk about own income - and we are very much in favour of that - we want to have a stable basis for that. one last point. it is very important that the commission proposes to ensure that all plastic packaging is recyclable by 2030. look at that alongside the wonderful work that our rapporteur simona bonafè and the others have done on the waste package and the circular economy. that's a good foundation. but we're also going to have to make sure that we create a market, that the manufacturers are going to use those recyclable packaging as well. as long as cheap oil is more readily available than recyclable materials, we have a problem. this is why strong initiatives are needed, such as ensuring that products of the future contain a minimum of recyclable materials. but thank you for this initiative!
the strategy is undoubtedly another step towards the circular economy we all want, in which recycling more and better plastics is an important and necessary link. i thank you and congratulate you on the ambition that the commission is showing here. we need to take a firm stand on china's ban on plastic imports to invest more in our own recycling capacity. but of course, just recycling more isn't enough. we also need to invest in waste prevention, in thoughtful product design, in new business models. i also welcome the commission's nuanced position on biodegradable plastics. clear agreements are needed, but we also need to realize that even that kind of plastic is not a salvage solution for marine litter, quite the contrary. the actions announced on microplastics also sound promising. european solutions must, will and can make a real difference here. finally, i call on the commission to speed up the legislative work, but also to take account of tailor-made products and not to limit its activities to consumer packaging. there is certainly room for innovation in the business-to-business market too!
the european parliament is today taking the final step in the process of ratifying the marrakesh treaty. people who are blind, visually impaired or have a reading disability still have very difficult or no access to books and other printed material. the need to make more works and other protected material available in accessible forms such as braille, audio books and large print is great. the text on the table introduces a new compulsory exception to copyright in eu law. this will allow beneficiary persons and organisations to make and distribute accessible format copies of works in the eu and third countries party to the convention. thanks to the marrakesh treaty, more than 30 million people in the eu with visual impairments are finally getting equal access to the printed word. a small step for humanity, a giant leap forward for blind and partially sighted people. thank you, sir.
the evaluation of the trafficking in human beings directive, published in 2016 by the commission, shows that sexual exploitation remains the most widespread purpose of trafficking in europe, followed by labour exploitation. women and girls are the main victims of this and the visit of the socialist and democratic group to sicily in the field of agriculture has given us a glaring example of this modern slavery. of course, sicily is not the only place where this slavery takes place. romanian women are not the only victims in europe either. the networks of nigerian girls in prostitution in brussels are another example. we often talk about sex exploitation, but there is also exploitation by companies, simply because they have an interest in making a short-term profit by paying their workers as little as possible. so what i am asking is that if we are working on the issue of sex exploitation - which is important, of course, in human trafficking - we also need to empower european companies that are exploiting these people today, whether in tomato growing, apple picking or other activities, simply because they want to sell products at lower prices in supermarkets, without any scruples. therefore, commissioner, i think it is important - and it is the commission's responsibility - to ask the member states to enforce this directive, which exists and is not being respected by some of them, but we must also give the member states the means, such as social inspectorates, for example, to monitor labour laws, because it is through labour laws, through labour protection that we will also protect these people, and it is important that companies respect labour protection.

the n-va is concerned about the human rights situation in china. under president xi jinping, china is turning to the world, but it is sliding ever further into dictatorship. the crackdown on peaceful protest and the persecution of hundreds of courageous protesters are proof of this. the nva delegation regrets this and therefore supports the resolution before it, which strongly condemns the sentences imposed on activists wu gan and lee ming-che, human rights lawyer xie jang and defenders of tibetan identity tashi wangchuk and monk choekyi. economic prosperity must go hand in hand with democratic reforms. we therefore urge xi jinping and his government to release all innocent political prisoners, to respect international human rights conventions and to respect the cultural identity of all peoples. finally, we call on the european institutions and the member states to continue to emphasise human rights in their relations with china and to put pressure on the regime to implement the necessary reforms.
for the ecr group on january 19, 2015, exactly three years ago tomorrow, thousands of congolese students began their courageous protest against the postponement of the presidential elections. president kabila is still on the throne, and unfortunately blood is still flowing in the streets of so many congolese cities, through all sorts of delaying manoeuvres. in late 2017, this bloodshed reached an absolute low point, when a demonstration led by the congolese church was brutally suppressed. particularly tragic, especially since the church has always played a reconciling and upbuilding role. the european condemnation of the violence was appropriate, but it simply did not go far enough. after three tumultuous years in which countless opponents lost their lives, we as the european union cannot look the other way. the fact that european companies and european member states are still signing contracts with kabila for energy supply, infrastructure and military cooperation is cynical and contributes to the political crisis in the congo. i call on the eu to further expand targeted sanctions against the corrupt members of the congolese regime and i urge member states to review their links with kabila.
madam president, the democratic republic of the congo is a country in central africa which should play a vital role: a political role, an economic role, a development role. unfortunately, this giant with clay feet has been a factor of instability in the region for decades. the wars that have been fought there, and which have never really stopped, have caused millions of deaths, millions of displacements, rape, and so on. and all of this for the benefit, quite simply, of natural resource exploiters and kleptocrats. today, the situation is once again very worrying, first and foremost for the congolese people. as you know, president kabila's term expired in 2016 under the constitution. since then, the authorities have repeatedly taken measures to postpone the holding of elections, despite the new year's eve agreement signed in december 2017. it is true that the decision has been made to hold elections on 23 december 2018, that an enrolment is underway and that a number of things, means are now in place. however, the doubt remains, and with good reason: the repression of the recent peaceful protests is unacceptable; the un and fidh reports on the atrocities in kasai call for a response and investigations. the death threats against human rights activists today are reprehensible. the detention of political prisoners, by the hundreds, is intolerable and i can go on and on about what is unacceptable in the democratic republic of congo. the authorities, if they wish to be credible, both to the congolese people and on the international stage, must provide the means for an independent ceni, release political prisoners, bring the perpetrators of violence to justice and guarantee the organisation of free and inclusive elections.
the question is "blue cardboard". i completely agree with you on the issue of redirecting aid and maybe moving towards more humanitarian aid, but you know, the corruption issues in the democratic republic of congo are not done on the back of development aid. the corruption problems we have in congo are produced by international corporations that exploit human resources and line the pockets of congolese kleptocrats. i therefore ask the epp to allow the adoption and support of measures that will force european companies not to be corrupt. i therefore ask you, mr president, what actions is the epp prepared to support in this area?
member of the commission. – madam president, parliament is demonstrating, with this urgency resolution, its keen commitment to addressing the situation in the democratic republic of congo (drc). we have been regularly discussing the drc here, every six months since mid-2016, and you have passed resolutions expressing grave concerns, which have unfortunately been growing with time. the international community has been worriedly watching, over the past couple of years, one of the largest countries in africa edging towards collapse due to a compounded political, security, humanitarian and socioeconomic crisis. the council has also been paying attention to the drc. on 11 december 2017, foreign ministers adopted eu conclusions that assess, without any indulgence, the situation in the country and clearly set the strategic course for the months to come. in the meantime, the commission, through its humanitarian aid department echo, is responding to the growing needs of the 13 million congolese affected by the humanitarian crisis, including 4.35 million internally displaced persons and 1.9 million undernourished children. the eu is very aware of the importance of monitoring and reporting on the human rights situation in a country in crisis. it has repeatedly emphasised that human rights monitoring and reporting is a crucial part of the mandate of united nations organisation stabilisation mission in the democratic republic of the congo (monusco) and especially the un’s joint human rights office in kinshasa. the eu promotes regular coverage of drc issues at the un human rights council. we were at the forefront in pushing, at the 35th session of the council in june 2017, for a resolution on the investigation into the events in kasai. the eu has also internalised in eu law the eu—un sanctions regime, targeting perpetrators of human rights abuses in the great lakes region. development aid is carried out through projects which are constantly evaluated and in which funds are set aside for specific purposes. they are currently focusing on food security, health and infrastructure development. eu aid is indeed already focused on supporting the population of the drc. it should be noted that the eu does not provide budget support to the congolese government. having recourse to article 96 of the cotonou agreement would create huge transaction costs for alternative delivery mechanisms. national systems delivering basic social services cannot function on ngo support alone. the impact of eu support to the health system and to rural development benefits the poor, ordinary people of the drc, who should not be victimised. as you know, there are complex and entrenched causes for the chronic fragility in the congo, not least linked to the governance of strategic natural resources. there is, however, international consensus around one issue. elections are needed to end the current political situation, whereby president kabila has stayed one full year beyond the end of his mandate. in that context, the european union has chosen to work closely with the united nations, the african union and other essential partners so as to speak and act as one in this direction. there is now a calendar, with elections planned for 23 december 2018; there is a budget; voter registration has made progress; and there is a wealth of technical support provided to the congo. yet many possible hurdles could still emerge, representing as many occasions to divert the country from this key objective. security threats have recently resurfaced in kasai and with the unprecedented massacre of 15 tanzanian un peace-makers in the east on 7 december. we have to be careful and ensure that, while monusco is empowered to respond and neutralise negative forces, there is no manipulation and no distraction. for elections to be credible, full observance of human rights and fundamental freedoms is also needed, as well as clear confidence—building measures on the part of the congolese authorities. the incidents of 31 december 2017, with the disproportionate use of force by security forces, constitute a sombre development that you rightly stigmatised in the resolution tabled, as did the international community, including the african union. in conclusion, i reiterate the eu’s commitment to contribute to the stabilisation of this crucial country in africa. in order to do so, we shall be ready to use not only dialogue and constructive support but also the sanctions instrument, if and when that proves to be the only effective way to create incentives for an environment conducive to elections.
the migratory pressure in africa will only increase in the coming years and decades. africa's population is growing spectacularly, but economic growth is not keeping pace, and the situation in many regions is fragile and conflictual. many of those affected will also continue to try to get to europe. the democratic republic of congo has the highest number of newly displaced people in the world. last year alone, more than 1.9 million congolese were forced to flee their homes due to escalating violence in kasai and repression by the regime. the additional €28 million in european emergency aid released last year is important for improving living conditions in the camps and saving lives, but it is clearly far from enough. it is clear that the eu must simultaneously take on a greater role in tackling the roots of all this misery: not only in political terms, but also in economic terms. there must be an ambitious aid programme for the most vulnerable areas in africa. we need a real marshall plan for africa. an economic recovery plan, building on the trust fund for africa and creating opportunities and prospects for the affected population.

prime minister of croatia. – mr president, i have a few remarks on this first round of catch-the-eye. first of all, on the issue of defence, which charles tannock mentioned, i believe that strengthening european defence is very important, given the wider context of the challenges we have when it comes to security. having seen what we have discussed over the past couple of years, one of the first things i did as prime minister was to put through the new national security strategy, a new system of homeland security, and to increase our defence budget. we are in the process of acquiring new jet fighters. i want to be a credible country with capabilities that can increase and be inter-operational with other allies within nato and with partners in the european union. that is why we are participating, with almost 450 croatian military personnel, in various operations across the world, be it with the un, nato or the european union. on the uk’s exit, i think the critical thing is to find what type of future contractual relationship we will forge between the united kingdom and the eu. i think it was walter hallstein who said that association can be membership minus one percent, or cooperation plus one percent: that is a very large span for what we can put into the new contractual relationship. this is a choice. it is a political choice, in a strange situation, when someone is actually about to leave. in my view, that will be critical in the next phase of the negotiations with london. regarding the spitzenkandidat and transnational lists, i have a practical concern. the budget in my country currently for the whole list for the campaign is eur 200 000. if a colleague wants to campaign he has eur 200 000 for the whole length of the campaign. as a candidate, how do you think he will campaign in turku, in braga, in palermo or somewhere on the shores of bourgas in bulgaria? he cannot do it. this is the problem. we should invest a little bit more practical thinking in this: probably increasing the limits of financing, finding ways to ensure that the new system not only underpins the person who is a candidate for the future presidency of the european commission but also gives equal opportunities to someone from what i would call a ‘notorious’ political family or someone from a big country with a strong media presence, or someone from a smaller country. this is my concern and i do not see the answer in the debate so far. as i said, we need to have equal chances in this exercise. in my view, the way it is now being contemplated is not entirely fair. that is why i have very clear reservations at this stage. i am not saying we will not find a solution. when it comes to the multiannual financial framework, you mentioned that i said we needed to find smart approaches. the smartest way is to know what you want, what your political priorities are, and then attach the financial means to those priorities. for a couple of years, i have been following the work of the group of experts led by mr monti – so often eloquently represented by alain lamassoure – on the reform of the own resources system. this is absolutely key if we want to avoid the debates that mr verhofstadt mentioned when we asked if it is the council which decides on income and parliament which decides on expenditure. this is essential. we always debate in the nitty-gritty negotiations and trilogues in a short time in order to achieve a political goal. therefore, finding a balance between research, innovation, the digital economy, youth, jobs, and traditional policy areas, such as regional policy development or agriculture or defence, is always a challenge. i believe that if we understand what the challenges will be in the next decade we can find consensus, especially now when – after the 2016 blues, after the brexit referendum and with the political developments in 2017, especially the enthusiastic approach of president macron of france, and the likely formation of another german government led by chancellor merkel – we have gone, in football terms, from a 0-1 to a 2-1 situation. that gives me hope and optimism for the strength of the european project, including the debate on the financial framework.

in writing. female genital mutilation (fgm), which involves the deliberate injury of the female genital organs without medical reasons, is motivated by cultural, religious or social motives and occurs in young girls up to the age of 15. there are no health benefits. on the contrary, it can cause serious problems. short-term infections, but also sexual problems in later life. according to unicef, some 200 million girls and women worldwide are victims of female genital mutilation. half of these women live in three countries: egypt, ethiopia and indonesia. today is the international day against female genital mutilation. the commission's request for zero tolerance is therefore justified and i fully support the resolution which accompanies it. the resolution calls on the eu and the member states that have not yet signed the istanbul convention on combating gender-based violence to do so. that convention contains grounds for criminalizing vgv. it also calls for the explicit criminalization of the medicalisation of vdv and for the highest standards of protection for asylum seekers applying for asylum on vdv-related grounds, in the context of the reform of the common european asylum system and the revision of the asylum directives, and through the new eu agency for asylum.
i am very pleased that we are today presenting a genuine balkan strategy, that we are offering the countries a perspective, albeit with conditions. we ask them to increase pressure to implement the necessary reforms. and that's a good thing. we are asking for these reforms not only for the stability of our region, but above all for the citizens on the ground. because let's face it, they've been through enough war violence and they deserve a democratic country. but i've heard a lot here today. i heard it here: it's a perspective, it's not really a date. i would like to add an element to the debate. we in europe must also be ready when we go into the next phase of enlargement. we must have brexit done by then. but we also need to reform the union. we should go for a smaller commission. we must move towards abolishing the unanimity rule. so let us have a balkan strategy with a view to possible accession, but let us reform europe to be ready for that.
a new presidency, a new list of priorities. the european perspective that bulgaria wants to offer the western balkans can promote stability, prosperity and security in that region. that in itself is a good thing, provided that all the conditions are meticulously observed. reforms that guarantee democracy, the rule of law and the protection of universal human rights are therefore essential. membership should be the final stage of the road that candidate countries take. and that road is still long, very long. but moreover, is a union which itself has problems with respect for democratic principles ready for further enlargement? if the old europe prefers democratically elected leaders behind bars to in parliament, then the legitimacy to take in new members is lost. and yet that is exactly what is happening in catalonia today. the eu is not really an example of democracy and unconditional respect for fundamental rights and freedoms at the moment. so let's dig deeper before we dream of further expansion.

on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, first of all i want to thank the rapporteurs for their work, for their wisdom and for their patience in this long discussion. you may remember the former president of the european people’s party who was my predecessor as prime minister of belgium and who was, i can say quite easily, a very dedicated european, wilfried martens. wilfried martens was a true believer in and defender of transnational lists and he understood what was at stake: he understood that without a strong link between the citizens and europe, eurosceptics and populists would in the end always win the game. and he understood that transnational lists will not alienate the citizen: quite the contrary. why? because, in the proposal that you have before you, people will have two votes. one vote is for a national candidate, for a national list, to keep the link between the citizen and his or her mep; and a second vote is for transnational candidates, to strengthen the link between the citizen and the european union. in my view, this is the only way to create what i call a european demos – which cannot arrive by accident, or spontaneously of itself – and i think it is also needed to make the spitzenkandidaten process really democratic. today the spitzenkandidaten process is still undemocratic and non-transparent, a backroom deal within political parties or between political parties. what spitzenkandidaten alone will not deliver, and what transnational lists will deliver because they complement the spitzenkandidaten process, it is that it becomes a democratic process. it will be the people, the citizens, who will decide on the spitzenkandidaten and the president of the commission. i have not heard any serious arguments against it. people say federal states don’t have it: wrong! federal states want it. my country, belgium, is a federal state and many want a federal constituency. it is separatists who make war against it. a second example is the united states of america: a federal state. it’s because there is no single constituency that it is mr trump who is president and not hilary clinton, who received three million votes more than donald trump. secondly, another argument i hear is that transnational lists are good for populists. i have to tell you that is also wrong. you could argue that democracy is only good for populists, so let’s abolish democracy and let’s abolish elections. i can tell you one thing: that’s nonsense. you have to beat the populists, not to fear them, and to do that you need transnational lists. the third argument, that transnational lists are only good for big member states, is also wrong. no political party can afford to participate in elections without putting all the countries on the transnational list. what counts is not size, mr rangel, what counts is the vision, the passion, the commitment of all of us. that is what counts. and let us be honest about big member states. our colleagues from spain, poland, france, italy and germany today need at least double the number of votes received by us representatives of small and medium-sized member states, but i nevertheless tabled, together with pascal, an amendment to ensure that small and medium-sized member states will be represented on the list. i am concluding. i know you’re a very tough president, mr wieland. let’s not waste this historic opportunity. brexit will never come back. never again will we see half of the member states in favour of this european constituency. and finally, mr president, altiero spinelli, the father of this european parliament, would turn in his grave if we missed this unique opportunity. for the greens/efa group mr president, ladies and gentlemen, we are charged with defending the general european interest. however, each of us is elected in his national or even regional constituency, which may lead us to put national or regional interests ahead of the general interest. to take a concrete example, our common currency, the euro, needs strong financial solidarity mechanisms between states to be viable, which obviously cannot be achieved without responsible management of public affairs and public expenditure. it is very easy today for a german candidate to defend responsibility in the german constituency, just as it is very easy for a greek candidate to defend solidarity in the greek constituency. the reverse is much more difficult, but this is what candidates who will be running at european level will have to do. they will have to formulate and defend a project that can speak and convince not only in one country of the union, but throughout the union. this is the condition for a genuine democratic debate in europe. the departure of the british elected representatives is an opportunity to give the debate this truly european dimension without removing seats at national level, since transnational seats are in addition to national seats and do not replace them. ladies and gentlemen, five years ago we also introduced the idea of 'leading candidates', the spitzenkandidaten. let us dare to innovate, once again, with this transnational constituency. these two swallows will not make the european democratic spring, but i am convinced that they will make a major contribution to it.

in writing. in the run-up to the 2019 european elections, i support this proposal to create transnational european lists which, in practice, should allow citizens to vote, alongside meps elected on national lists, for the same meps across europe. in my vision of europe, which combines federalism and realism, i believe that the establishment of these single constituencies will help to create a stronger link with the european citizen and, in this sense, will promote more european democracy. these transnational lists will make it possible to better 'export' 'europe', to have more debate and, i hope, to strengthen the sense of european belonging. finally, they will make it possible to give the european elections a genuinely european character. the problem with european elections beyond the low turnout rates is that they are the sum of national electoral laws. having transnational lists that transcend geographical divisions will give these elections a true european dimension, beyond the borders of the member states. the creation of the single constituencies must, however, be part of a much wider project and be followed by other measures that will help to revive the european project, which, more than ever, needs a new breath.

and this is not a product of chance or some law of nature, it is the product of political choices. the need to make labour markets more flexible, to put public services and social protection under pressure, to relax any legislation that would weaken or handicap our businesses, even when it comes to protecting health or the environment, is fashionable, with the aim of improving public finances and achieving greater economic efficiency. retirement is delayed, and young people are having trouble finding work. we need to reduce unemployment benefits, whereas in some parts of the european union, for a job offer, you have dozens and dozens of applicants. rest assured, good people, growth is back and jobs with it. but the problem is that this job is precarious. he is getting less and less well paid, while the capitalists are getting a bigger and bigger piece of the pie. we are led to believe that making the rich richer and the multinationals more powerful is good for everyone. well, ladies and gentlemen, trickle-down economics exists, and it defies the laws of gravity, because money goes from poor to rich. all this has very concrete consequences, of course, because in our rich european union, a quarter of our citizens, including millions who have jobs, are at risk of poverty and social exclusion, to the point where some of them have to choose between eating and taking care of themselves, to the point of dying on the streets of our cities. mrs thyssen, ladies and gentlemen, i am glad that the european pillar of social rights has come into being. however, if we are to reverse the curve of inequality and make this pillar something more than a moral alibi, we must not only make it as binding as the european budgetary rules, but also definitively change the direction of our economic policies so that they are put at the service of the greatest number of our citizens.
madam president, madam commissioner, i would like to start by quoting the work of richard wilkinson, a british researcher who has done groundbreaking work on inequality. in fact, it can be summed up as follows: societies with deep inequality are societies that are very difficult to live in. conversely, a society with low inequality and high equality is a society where everyone is happy. everyone is very important. it's not just about the poor. everyone in society is better off when there is less inequality. what's perhaps important to stress: the european union is a democratic country, and it is a democratic country. that actually makes sense. people who all work together to build a society and all contribute to it in their own way but are very unfairly rewarded for it feel that it is very unfair. a democracy - we all know that - is much more than this parliament or the rule of law. it's a means to an end. in other words, to ensure that everyone can live together freely and happily, and that can be done in a society with less inequality. that is essential. the commission has been very active lately in the field of the rule of law, with our full support. think of what we're doing in poland. think about what we're doing in hungary. that's what's important. that part of democracy is being defended. the question is: will we please defend that other part of democracy with equal fire? my colleagues have already spoken about the social pillar and fair taxation. but let's be very clear. it's about a challenge that is much broader than tackling poverty, as important as that is. it is about an approach that ensures that our european democracy has a future.

deputising for the rapporteur. – mr president, firstly, mr dalton apologises that he is unable to be here to speak on this report himself. this regulation is an important step to close loopholes and improve cooperation between member states in the fight against criminality. the efficient exchange of information on previous criminal convictions in the eu member states is vital, in order to ensure trust, confidence and mutual recognition in the area of judicial cooperation. it is also only right that there is equality in the exchange of information on convictions of eu nationals and third-country nationals. today, under the current european criminal records information system (ecris), we identify previous criminal convictions in the eu for eu nationals but not for third-country nationals. our report closes this gap. the report had wide support in the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs (libe) precisely because it provides a fair balance between protecting the rights of individuals and ensuring that past criminal convictions are correctly attributed. it is about ensuring that judicial authorities and law enforcement agencies have a complete picture in order to carry out their work. as an instrument it offers clear safeguards and protection. it is only for convictions handed out by eu member states, and it can be accessed only under certain very specific circumstances. a robust and secure system for sharing conviction information with clear safeguards is needed, and this is what the libe committee has proposed.

on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, first of all we have to thank mr kamall for all the recommendations he is giving for the next budget, in which britain will no longer be involved. thank you mr kamall. secondly, i think we have to pay you, mr president, a double salary in february because you have taken over the role of mr tusk today and that is significant because you, mr timmermans, spoke of the idea of ‘one hat’ in the european union, and we have proved that it is possible for the commission to double-hat for the council. the only other thing, maybe, is that you can go over to the other side once, and then come back. but i will not go over the whole discussion that you had in that informal council about spitzenkandidaten and transnational lists. i have already said what i think about that, namely that you are making an enormous strategic mistake. you are endangering the spitzenkandidat concept by not embedding it in a democratic process in a transnational list. the reality is that the spitzenkandidat continues to be a toy of political parties and again between the council on the one hand and parliament on the other, but there is no legitimacy on the democratic front. transnational lists provide precisely that – putting the spitzenkandidat concept into a democratic process so it is our people who decide who is elected, and then the european council could no longer object to the outcome of such an election. but okay: rome was not built, as you know mr president, in a day, and the same is true of transnational democracy. we will come back to this in 2024. on the issue of the budget, i have been involved three times in the adoption of the multiannual financial framework (mff), twice as a member of the council and once as a member of the european parliament, and my fear is very simple, mr timmermans: that it will be business as usual. it will be a budget for seven years in which the next parliament will have no say – no say in five years! secondly, i fear it will be again a list of 50-60 rebates, exceptions, earmarkings, and exceptional monies to buy the support of all the member states, and 80% of it will be money paid by the member states going back to the member states, and not going to the new priorities that we have – border management, innovation, migration, defence and things like that. so my appeal to all the political groups represented here is: let’s break the cycle of this european budget. let’s make an agreement, together with the commission, on the priorities. secondly, on conditionality: will we continue to hear, for example, that mr orban is misusing european money to build a football stadium in his town, or to make his friends and family multimillionaires on the basis of european money? no! let us establish conditionality: if you don’t accept the values, you don’t need our money. that must be the principle. and thirdly, on the issue of our responsibility. this is the only parliament, colleagues, which has no say about income: the only one worldwide which has nothing to say about revenue. that has to change, and that means article 311 of the treaty, once there is a change to the treaty, will need to incorporate co-decision by the european parliament on income. my plea to you – and it is more important for me than the period of five years or of seven years – is that, for once, we have can cohesion in this parliament, unity in this parliament, on our refusal ever to accept an mff without co-decision by parliament on the issue of revenue. that said, if we do not decide before the next election, it will not be the fault of this parliament. i’m pretty sure that the european council will not be capable of reaching agreement on the next mff before the elections.
mr president, commissioner, with an annual trade surplus of around eur 10 billion, or 8% of total european agri-food exports, the spirit drinks sector is one of the most important sectors in this field and generates around 1 million jobs. until now, regulation (ec) no 110/2008 established a regulatory framework for these spirit drinks which has generally satisfied the professionals in the sector. the overall modernisation of this legislation is therefore at the heart of our concerns. i welcome the fact that article 43 (2) of the treaty on the functioning of the european union has been introduced as a legal basis, thus providing an agricultural anchorage for this legislation. indeed, spirits represent an important outlet for agricultural production in the european union, both in qualitative and quantitative terms. in addition, the alcohol used in the production of spirit drinks must be of agricultural origin. i conclude by saying that it is crucial that consumers can be legitimately sure, with confidence, that within the union, any beverage labelled as vodka, whiskey or rum is the product they expect.

in writing. bees play a vital role in the pollination of trees and plants. but now their colonies are threatened. action is needed to reduce bee mortality, including by banning pesticides that are harmful to bee health. developing a european action plan to protect bees, which would improve the resilience of colonies to mites and other sources of destruction, is a minimum. the commission has already proposed a ban on the most harmful pesticides, including neonicotinoids. europe must do everything possible to avoid the environmental disaster that could result from the disappearance of bees. i therefore invite you to support the amendments calling on the commission and the member states to put in place measures to strengthen the legal protection and financial support for honeybee ecotypes throughout the european union, including through the establishment of legally protected conservation areas for endemic honeybees, and calling on the commission to urgently propose legislation to ban the production, sale and use of all neonicotinoid pesticides throughout the union, without any possible derogation, in order to protect honeybee populations.
- i'm the reporter. thank you, mr president. it's early in the morning. i'm a good sleeper, and i'm lucky that way. i sleep reasonably well, but there are people who sleep less well, who worry at night because they feel insecure, including about their future, and who wonder if their savings are safe. it is our job to take those concerns seriously, to address them, and to remedy them where necessary. the people have not forgotten the deep wounds caused by the euro crisis, the danger to their savings. we all said at the time: this can never happen again. that is the question we are dealing with in this report today. did we keep our word? this will never happen again? have we developed rules that have made our banking system more robust, made it more resilient when banks fail? let's start with the honest answer: yes, we did. the system has become more robust and more robust because during and after that financial crisis we also made the honest and correct analysis that there were too few rules, allowing bankers to build up too much risk. we've dealt with that. we've put more rules on the table and we've implemented them. second, we also reversed the logic. it used to be that big shareholders, big bondholders of banks, would walk away with the profits when things were going well, but the losses would trickle down to the taxpayer when things went wrong. we have reversed that logic. what makes this report special is that we have not only seen the rules, but we have also seen the application of the rules, for the first time in the european union and in a number of different countries, with varying degrees of success. we have seen what has happened in latvia this weekend, but last year we saw the cases in spain and italy, where the spirit of the rules has been respected a little more in one case than in the other. i think that in italy, for example, we have seen a more creative interpretation of the banking rules. i think that is the european disease, ladies and gentlemen. we have better rules, but we don't always follow them. we're making sure that extra measures are taken, but if there are backdoors, they're not completely closed. i think we should start with that, with finally doing what we agreed to. that we would also effectively apply the rules on the table. but no, you see that here in the european debate there is a kind of idea circulating that by definition the easiest way to solve problems is to start to unite them, to start to disperse them. i'm a father of three young children. when our daughter suzanne does something wrong, i'll tell her, and then she'll have to pay for it. if i were to raise my children not in flemish but in european terms, i would have to address not only her but also her brothers, tuur and cas, who have done nothing wrong. that's kind of the logic that you also see in the banking system, that we have to be in solidarity, that member states that haven't done anything wrong are by definition asked to be in solidarity with member states that are doing wrong. i don't think that's right. i think we should first ask those member states to take responsibility for putting their accounts in order before we start to consolidate. that is why, in all honesty, i sometimes get a little nervous when i see dossiers on the european table, for example the european deposit and guarantee fund. today there are national funds which are empowered to take action when people's savings of up to 100 000 euros could be jeopardised. europe, the european commission in particular, is now saying that these national pools are too small, that we should replace them with one big european pot, which would make banks and savers here in northern europe guarantors for savers in southern europe. i think we should be careful with this. i think it is a good system if we pool risks, but only if the member states first limit their own risks. there is still a lot of work to be done in this area, particularly in southern europe.
- i'm the reporter. thank you, mr president. i would like to thank my colleagues, especially the shadow rapporteurs, for the excellent cooperation and the interesting debate. i think it's interesting that pretty much everyone we've heard in this room says, yes, there's been a lot of work done, good work, but there's also a lot of work left to do. so we have some business to attend to. in this respect, it has also become a rather critical report, with some suggestions for future discussions. if you ask me personally what the focus should be on now, i see four points myself. one: i think we should tighten up our state aid rules. we learned that from the italian bank bailouts. two , you 're not . we need to work more on the supervisory side. we have seen recently - and this debate has repeatedly referred to this - what has happened in latvia. three , please . we need more transparency. there is still a great deal of work to be done in this area too. and finally, four, and that's the most important thing: the rules must be tightened up and not relaxed. in this context, i am looking with more than any interest at what is happening in italy, for example. there are elections this weekend and almost every prominent italian politician is calling for a loosening of banking rules. that is not the way we should go. the direction we must take is to choose responsibility first and solidarity second. i also hear the commissioner talking about risk sharing and risk reduction, risk sharing and risk mitigation. that's right, but it's also wrong. it should be the other way around. in this parliament we should first talk about risk reduction and then about risk sharing. first we talk about responsibility, then we talk about solidarity.
on behalf of the ppe group thank you, mr president. good morning, colleagues. how are you? leaks like the panama and paradise papers keep public outrage burning. and we need to do that, because with public pressure, we can bring about political action. i think this directive is a really good example of that. the paradise papers in particular have shown the importance of intermediaries in tax evasion. it is these specialists who sell the most optimal tax loopholes to their clients, so to speak. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the taxation of the value of goods and services is not distorted. an sme in west flanders simply cannot afford the services of such a specialised consultant. this is beyond competition, this is just unfair. that is why this report is very balanced, and i think that is important. a good example of this is the reporting requirement we require for new and old structures with a possible odour. but at the same time, we can't let this become a manhunt. the exception of excluding old structures which are no longer actively used from this obligation to notify is therefore justifiable. in this parliament, ladies and gentlemen, we have made the clear choice for more fiscal justice and more transparency some time ago. this report fits perfectly into that.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of taxation. tax evasion is a multifaceted problem, but the source of it is usually a lack of information. today we vote on the dac 6. it is the latest in a series of proposals to tackle tax evasion and should close the last loopholes in the net. what makes this proposal so different is that it is preventive. instead of preventing tax avoidance, we give member states the opportunity to anticipate it and to protect their revenue from aggressive tax structures. it also ensures that the information they collect will be shared automatically. this cross-border approach will make it much more difficult to abuse the different systems between countries, because it would be naive to think that this problem can be solved entirely at national level. i believe that this proposal will achieve its intended purpose.

in writing. tackling and preventing terrorist financing is crucial in the fight against terrorism. once the money supply runs dry, it becomes a lot harder for terrorists to carry out their activities. that is why we need to get a better view of these money flows as soon as possible, and to intercept and punish shady transactions. but this can only be achieved if cooperation at european level is also optimized. in concrete terms, the creation of a european intelligence platform on terrorist financing should further improve the exchange of information between national intelligence services. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environmentally friendly use of energy. the new anti-money laundering directive agreed by the eu at the end of last year is already a major step forward in the fight against terrorist financing. the eu is also working to improve the way in which the eu's citizens are treated in the eu. but intelligence services also need to exchange that information efficiently with each other. belgium is already taking the lead in this area, inter alia by banning anonymous prepaid cards. let us share such good practices with other member states to develop an effective and proactive approach at european level.
