on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, dear prime minister may, this is neither the first nor the last time, i am afraid, that i will address you in this hemicycle, where you remain welcome at any time. ever since you became prime minister, i have carefully listened to you on brexit. i do acknowledge your wish to make the best out of the inconvenient situation the referendum has put your country in, but i am still perplexed by the inner contradictions of your government’s positions. indeed, arguing (a) that the united kingdom will leave the single market and customs union; (b) that the united kingdom government will stand by its commitment to the good friday agreement; and (c) that there will be no new regulatory barriers within the united kingdom, is a trilemma that cannot be solved. this has nothing to do with the alleged stubbornness of the eu-27; it has everything to do with the stubbornness of facts. if regulatory divergence is real, it creates a border, and no magical thinking can ever erase it. therefore, one of the three elements has to give, and i cannot imagine the british government giving up on, or taking any risk with, peace in northern ireland. therefore, it has to be (a) or (c). in our view, the only practical solution would be for the uk, or at least northern ireland, to remain fully within the single market and customs union. taking into account your long relationship with us, and the fact that, of course, the uk would then have to apply eu law without being a part of the decision process, it would be fair to establish a privileged channel for your country to express concerns and suggestions. and i agree with you that we have to tailor the transition period to the practical imperatives, rather than align it with any pre-set dates. the end of that transition should also be the cut-off date of any change in the status of citizens, and we do welcome, as guy just did, the very significant progress made over the last few months in that respect. a few issues remain to be tackled, however: the non-reversibility of rights, the rights of european residents with disabilities, the status of future spouses, the rights of third-country nationals, as well as voting rights. our key concern is that no one ends up losing rights. if we want to be creative, we should consider basing citizens’ rights on residence rather than on nationality, which may provide at the same time certainty and simplicity. prime minister, one recognises a stateswoman by her ability not so much by being strong facing the other camp, but by confronting her adversaries in a round camp. now is the time to be bold.
mr president, it is clear that every step we take in these negotiations raises more questions, and on both sides of the channel. this is certainly the case for a group of people who have been underexposed until now, and wrongly so: our fishermen. the fishermen in flanders are having a hard time. they want to make new investments, but they are not getting any support from the union because of european rules. and right now, one of their biggest factors for business security is being called into question. many of them fish on the basis of historical rights and agreements in british waters. and they do so with respect for the environment and with respect for the rules in force. whether we like it or not, fisheries are inevitably part of these negotiations. it is therefore crucial that clear agreements are made in our new relationship with the united kingdom in the field of fisheries. first and foremost, it must be about the conservation of fish stocks. fishermen, even after brexit, will not stop swimming at the border between british and european waters. but the most important point is this. it is important for the safety of the flemish fishermen, as well as the british, that access to each other's waters as it exists today can continue to be guaranteed. and there must be a clear link between future fisheries agreements and the new relationship with the united kingdom, as it will be established through negotiations. if we do not, ladies and gentlemen, - and i apologize for the metaphor - we risk being caught in treacherous waters, and we cannot and must not do that to our fishermen.
on behalf of the ecr group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, mr juncker, thank you for coming today, because we missed you very much yesterday in the interesting debate on the appointment of your chief of staff, mr selmayr, as secretary-general of the european commission. i now want to talk about the future of europe, which must be social. we must work towards upward convergence and make the eu a good place to live for everyone. we do this by tackling fraud and social dumping, among other things. this is rightly a priority for the juncker commission, although i do have some critical questions about the way in which the commission intends to achieve this social objective. for example, commissioner thyssen will be presenting her proposal for a european labour authority this afternoon. the new european agency should be the missing piece in the fight against social fraud, according to thyssen. i would stress, however, that we must consider carefully the added value of new organs. do we really need that authority? does it really add something that the member states themselves cannot? why, for example, do we abolish the recently established platform against black labor and replace it with authority? the creation of expensive european institutions should not be an end in itself. nor should the authority seek to take the place of the member states. even if this is not the stated ambition today, i wonder how this european labour authority will continue to grow in its role. its name and its permanent character at least indicate further plans for the future. i think we must be very careful not to allow a surreptitious expansion of powers, whereby the union is gradually eroding the powers of the member states. if today a smokescreen of good intentions is being erected, but the real objective is to set up a european social inspectorate, then i regret this manoeuvre. i repeat that the future of europe must be social, but i add that europe has no future without respect for and for its member states.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, i want to urge the council to add two specific items to the normal agenda (the western balkans, which president juncker has spoken about). the first is that we need from the council a common reaction from the eu, together with our friends in the uk, on the new aggression by the kremlin on what is still european soil – in britain – and i am referring to the poisoning case of sergei skripal and his daughter. mrs may has said that it is an attack against britain as a country, and i think that a common reaction in the next european council is absolutely needed and counter—measures need to be decided by the eu. my second point, naturally, is that, regarding the type of solidarity that we need still to have with the uk, that we hope also for it to be returned. solidarity from the uk, for example, in our policies towards mr trump: it should absolutely be avoided that, behind the eu’s back, they send somebody to the us to find a special deal on these trade measures. if mr trump finds that there are horrific tariffs from our side, there are also horrific tariffs from their side. he is talking about the 10% on the import of us cars in europe, and we paid only 2.5%. but there are other examples: tariffs on a train carriage, for example, are 14% in the us, or for pickups – and there are a lot of pickups in the us – we have to pay 25% in tariffs. i think what the european council should do now is say that there are two options in this starting of a trade war. either we start a trade war and a war on tariffs by increasing them from both sides, or we do what should be the thing to do: to lower the tariffs in both cases, the tariffs on the european side and the tariffs on the us side, which both sides find so horrific. i hope that that can be the common position. we have no interest in starting a trade war. what we have to offer instead is a trade deal from the european side. in fact, there are many people who were against ttip but now see that if we had got ttip we could have avoided what is happening today. i hope that this measure will be taken – no trade war, but a new trade deal.
the rapporteur. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, it is time to put an end to the long drift in the financing of the union's budget. the treaty is clear: article 311 states that 'the budget of the union shall be financed entirely from own resources, without prejudice to other revenue'. we're a long way from that. today, contributions from national budgets account for almost 70% of the union's revenue. worse, since margaret thatcher's "i want my money back", the drift of the system has produced a monster of opacity. the british rebate gave rise to rebate on rebate, to which over the years have been added multiple and heterogeneous corrections, which it is totally impossible to explain to the citizens. the truth is that the union's budget is no longer the instrument of a common project. it has become a box, heavy to feed for some, interesting to empty for others. the fair return is becoming the alpha and omega of the european budget. this has already been strongly denounced. our colleague, alain lamassoure, has made a brilliant case for this. our colleague, janusz lewandowski, tried to correct it when he was commissioner, and he lost both times. more recently, at the insistence of our assembly, the three institutions have installed the monti group, which has done a remarkable job. our report is in line with the conclusions of this group, which have been insistently endorsed by the representatives appointed by the three institutions. in doing so, we are pursuing a threefold objective: to respect - as i said - the requirements of article 311 of the treaty and to break the back of the logic of just return; the aim is to offset the negative effect of brexit on the budget. it is also a question of giving the union the means to meet the major new challenges we face. we therefore call for the gradual introduction of a new own resources package to protect the budget from the vagaries and fluctuations of a single resource. we advocate the selection in this package of new resources which are directly linked to the creation of european added value in the union's strategic policies. we are calling for the abolition of rebates, of all rebates, of rebates on rebates, of all existing or invented corrections. we are calling for a corresponding reduction in the budgetary contributions of the member states, which are likely to become residual in the coming years. three key points to conclude: by voting for these two reports, you will be affirming the determination of our parliament when it announces that it will not approve the future multiannual financial framework unless it involves the implementation of new own resources to guarantee its financing. we also ask you to approve our request to amend article 311 of the treaty, where it regulates the decisionmaking procedure in the field of own resources. the european parliament, as the budgetary authority alongside the council, cannot be reduced to a mere advisory role in the introduction of own resources. this disregard for parliamentary democracy is unacceptable. finally, parliamentary democracy is also not comfortable with the situation in which the parliament elected after the 2019 elections has no say in the financial framework that will organise, for seven years, the use of the budgetary appropriations it provides. in conclusion, i would like to thank my co-rapporteur, janusz lewandowski, and the cfp rapporteurs, jan olbrycht and isabelle thomas, my colleagues and friends, eider gardiazabal and josé manuel fernandes, and my colleague, helga trüpel, my budget assistant, the secretariat of the committee on budgets, our political advisers and my friend, pierre verkaeren, for their valuable assistance.
president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i think we can say that europeans rightly expect the european union to work on security, social protection, defence and, of course, climate action. the people expect the european union to protect them and all of us in all these respects and to invest in european projects with a very clear european added value. this will require additional resources. the commission has already proposed that the commission should be able to take a decision on the matter. that is not to say, ladies and gentlemen, that we cannot save wisely. of course we can and of course we must, but we must not fool the people. saving on, for example, the cohesion funds means in our region, in flanders, 250 million less on an annual basis. we can tell people that, too. we can also say that we are the publishers of fake news those who say that europe has its priorities out of order and that we are spending too much money that we should sue them and that the masks can finally fall.
the rapporteur. madam president, i am very pleased with this debate and the general tone of the speeches by a large majority of colleagues, particularly with regard to the need for new own resources. yes, the budget of the european union needs new own resources. yes, we are right to link the acceptance of the multiannual financial framework to the incorporation of new own resources. i am pleased to note, commissioner, that you are in this line. if i remember correctly, you have indicated that the commission was already working on four or five possibilities for new own resources. i am not saying that we should not be concerned about the future of the community. you are right, and if you remain right, you will have the massive support of our parliament. i also want to respond to some of the speeches by colleagues who have strongly warned against an increase in taxes paid by citizens when new own resources are introduced. own resources, ladies and gentlemen, do not necessarily mean additional taxes for citizens. if we reach an agreement tomorrow on the taxation of the big digital companies, which are currently playing with our feet and pay almost no taxes, while they make billions in profits, we will not tax the citizens any more. if we find a way tomorrow to withdraw a few cents from speculative financial transactions, we will not tax the citizens, on the contrary. if the profits of the european central bank are distributed differently tomorrow, the citizens will not be taxed. we have to stop with the fantasies. we need new own resources and we need these new own resources, because we need europe more than ever.
on behalf of the alde group. mr president, i would first like to congratulate our colleague mr viotti on his investment, his creativity, his italian style and his sense of compromise. and i want to say to him, "mr. viotti, you are blessed by the gods". i have just heard the commissioner say, if the translation is correct, 'mr viotti, you have put your accents, they are ours' and then i have heard the commissioner say, when he examined the council's proposals, 'they are practically yours'. but this is remarkable, mr. viotti. i will not therefore use this opportunity to repeat what my colleagues and the commissioner have said on the matter of priorities, but i would simply like to stress two things which i think are important at this stage. firstly, young people, all young people, must experience in their own lives that the european union is for them, that it invests in them, that it counts on them. we all agree that the resources available under erasmus+ must be considerably increased and extended to young people from the working class by apprentices. measures to support youth employment need to be assessed and the best performing programmes strengthened. the european solidarity corps must be implemented rapidly to strengthen the values of mutual aid and solidarity among young people. secondly, the popular classes, who have been gradually won over by euroscepticism and populism, must be reconciled with the european idea. europe - and this is a member of the alde group who tells you this - cannot be the affair of the ruling circles, the entrepreneurs, the academic circles and the intellectual middle class alone. the three institutions have recently solemnly proclaimed the european pillar of fundamental rights. to proclaim is good, but to put it into practice is better. we must all look together for ways of adding european value in the social field.
president, mrs mogherini, you yourself said that the situation in syria is dramatic. seven years, four hundred thousand dead. in the last three months alone, more than three thousand children have been killed and 13 million people are in need of humanitarian aid. in addition, after much insistence, we finally adopted the un resolution, but it was not implemented. what's worse, the relief convoys are not getting to the victims. mr schaake has already said it, hospitals are being bombed, ambulances are being bombed. how inhuman can inhumanity really be? sometimes i feel like we're actually getting a little used to seeing those images. children with foam on their lips, mustard gas, bombings, mass destruction on such a horrific scale. and we look at it and we talk. i think it is good that you have said that we must ensure that the ceasefire is respected. we need to make sure there's a no-fly zone. we need to get help to the victims. that's priority number one right now. but i regret to say that i have not heard much talk about the fact that we will have to talk to iran, turkey and russia. if europe is really taking its task seriously, that is what i am asking you to do. take that roll! you are a strong woman! you can do it! and the second point is: we'll have to execute the perpetrators before any tribunal.
the rapporteur. mr president, madam commissioner, i would first like to thank all those who negotiated this text and helped to bring it to fruition. thank you to tom, bernd, caroline, miguel and bas. as you know, the report on the european semester which we are voting on tomorrow is a step forward in the economic and budgetary policy to be pursued and offers solutions to reduce the growth of socioeconomic inequalities within the same country and to limit the growth of the phenomenon of the working poor or to move towards greater convergence between the member states. i think we can rejoice: the economic situation is improving and a new direction is beginning to emerge. this is obviously what is being asked for in this report and i thank the commission, which has taken up some of our remarks in its country-by-country recommendations. it decided to place greater emphasis on integrating the social rights and environmental pillar into the guidelines of its recommendations. as we know, the european citizen is looking for a stable and healthy socioeconomic climate, the main objective of which must be full employment. europe remains the richest continent in the world, with still considerable development potential, for example in the field of energy transition, but it is essential that our socioeconomic instruments serve the cause of citizens, their jobs and their well-being. workers are confronted daily with the impact of the rapidly changing economy and, as politicians, we must be able to react to these changes, accompany them to avoid them being the first victims. that is why we have included a whole series of points in this report, but i would like to highlight five points which i think are important. the first focuses on the importance and need for increased private and public investment to invest in the real economy. private investment is only at the level of 2008, public investment is still 40% below the level of 2008. we also asked the commission to assess the obstacles to the implementation and depreciation over time of large investment projects, which are often public-private partnerships. without public investment, for example in infrastructure or health care, there is unfortunately no private investment, and if you add these two factors together, there can be no job creation. the second point concerns responsible public finances. we insist that the budgetary guidelines, at national and euro-area level, must ensure the long-term sustainability of public finances and their sound management. this good management must go hand in hand with a rise in wages. this is a proposal that is also supported by ecb president mario draghi, in order to allow workers to have higher purchasing power and thus to revive the economy as a whole, perhaps more effectively than current measures. we then discussed the issue of structural reforms, particularly to strengthen the youth guarantee, the importance of education policies, aid to micro-enterprises and the guarantee of the sustainability of social security systems. in short, everything that makes our continent more valuable to the rest of the world. let us not hide, this added value is in danger and we must not only preserve it, but also intensify it so that europe can once again be a symbol of hope for these millions of young people. the debate is obviously about european tax harmonization, all the work that has already been done by the european parliament and the need to go further towards a common system of fair corporate taxation, and finally the last chapter is about greater integration in the negotiations on the six-month period of the different actors, such as the european parliament, but also the national and/or regional parliaments and the social partners. i really think that ownership of the semester by these different actors is a prerequisite for better implementation of the country-specific recommendations. this is obviously only the first step. other measures are still needed, such as reforming the stability and growth pact to allow states to reinvest in services for citizens and to boost employment. but i think we are on the right track for real economic and social change in europe and i hope that this report will have a large majority tomorrow.
president-in-office of the council. – mr president, thank you for the opportunity to discuss today the european semester, a topic of particular relevance in these times. the council attaches great importance to the semester, and i am pleased to exchange views with parliament on this subject. if we are to make europe relevant to peoples’ daily lives, we need to achieve good results on growth and jobs, high-quality education, social protection and inclusion, as well as environmental sustainability. we should stress here that it is question of ownership. implementation of structural reforms under the european semester is key for strengthening the resilience and potential growth of member states and the european union as a whole. an agreement on common objectives is therefore important. your reports show that we see these common objectives, and i am glad that we have this opportunity to discuss them with you today and that you have done so recently with national parliaments. to start on a positive note, evidence suggests that europe’s economy is strengthening, employment is growing, investment is recovering and public finances are improving. yet, clearly, many challenges remain. you have identified a number of them in your reports. while the recovery is turning into expansion and is becoming more broad-based, some member states still face major structural challenges. high debt levels and high youth unemployment rates remain a source of concern. also, investment activity has not yet reached the levels we aspire to. boosting investment remains a priority in order to promote a more robust recovery and to secure healthy growth rates in the long run. the extension of the european fund for strategic investment in terms of financial capacity is an important step towards achieving this goal. let us continue to work together to stimulate social investment and human capital, to lift the remaining barriers, and to foster a truly favourable investment climate. furthermore, we need to support growth with smart fiscal policies while paying close attention to the long-term sustainability of our budgets and debts. we also need to implement the necessary structural reforms on our product and labour markets in order to prepare our economies for the challenges ahead. we must provide our young citizens with high-quality education and invest in their skills today. the single market is a great eu asset, and we should join forces for its further development. during the presidency, we plan on making progress on the initiatives of the digital single market strategy, as we think digitalisation is crucial for the competitiveness of the industry in the european union. in view of these challenges, the bulgarian presidency has outlined the digital economy and skills for the future as one of our four priorities. we are committed to contributing to the process of completing the digital single market as a source of growth and competitiveness, while also focusing on the development of skills and competences of the 21st century. as we are now leaving crisis times behind us, the legacy of the crisis remains a concern. some citizens are trapped in a cycle of poverty and social exclusion from which they cannot easily escape. this is an important issue for the presidency. our policy efforts need to be geared toward those with the most disadvantages. they also need to be geared towards those regions and sectors which are undergoing the most difficulties. we need to think of new solutions to new problems whilst seizing the opportunities ahead of us. the collaborative economy represents a huge potential, but it cannot be a reason to backtrack on employment and social rights. employment and social policies need to adapt to the changes in the labour markets, but at the same time workers’ rights should remain protected. environmental policy, together with the greening economy and greening the european semester can contribute in a very significant manner to the broad policy objectives of stimulating sustainable growth and creating jobs. the environmental implementation review initiative will be again on the council’s agenda next year. what is key is to focus on the full implementation of the reforms and to carefully monitor the progress in each member state. we should share our experiences and use best practices examples to learn from each other. only by closely working together to constantly push for reforms, and to continuously support inclusive growth and job creation, will we be successful in creating tangible benefits for every citizen and in rebuilding trust in a strong and prosperous european union.
member of the commission. – mr president, it is a pleasure to talk this house today, and a special pleasure because it is nice to be able to bring some good news. this year’s semester takes place amid steady economic expansion. europe is seeing the highest growth rate in a decade, investment is picking up, employment is at record levels and the unemployment rate is almost where it was before the crisis. the share of people at risk of poverty or social exclusion fell to the pre-crisis level. public finances are improving and the banking sector is well capitalised. our strategy of investment, structural reforms and responsible policies is working. still, in some member states the legacy of the crisis is visible in the high levels of debt and non-performing loans, and in the unemployment rates. indeed, also, the risk of poverty and social exclusion remain high in some countries and 18 million people are seeking a job. we need to use the current good times to strengthen our economic foundations, improve resilience and support convergence. i take from the two reports on the annual growth survey which we are discussing today, that we share views on the challenges and where the european union and member states should focus their efforts. we must make sure that today’s growth is inclusive and sustainable and that our economies and societies are prepared to harness the opportunities and address the challenges that technology and globalisation bring. so let me thank the two rapporteurs, mr bayet and mr hetman, and the rapporteur for the committee on budgets for these excellent reports. raising productivity is the only way to achieve higher living standards and greater prosperity. for this we need investment, not just in infrastructure but also in human capital, research and innovation, and this requires ensuring an investment-friendly environment including by tackling corruption and ensuring respect for the rule of law. beyond that, only an educated, skilled labour force can seize new opportunities when the labour market changes, and only with proper social security can people make the necessary transitions in the new world of work. human beings is indeed what europe is about. in terms of fiscal policy, we stress the need for the right balance between supporting growth and ensuring sustainable public finances. good times should be used to build buffers and reduce high debts. fair, growth-friendly taxation and well-targeted spending remain crucial. the european semester has proven instrumental for the coordination of policies in the union. we run it in a spirit of cooperation and partnership. we engage in discussions with the national authorities and stakeholders at national and at european level, including the national parliaments and social partners. last week, the commission issued its assessment of the economic and social challenges for 27 member states, including the relevant detailed analysis of macroeconomic imbalances. we see that the imbalances are receding in the context of a favourable economic environment. we de—escalated the macroeconomic imbalances procedures for four countries. slovenia has successfully corrected its imbalances and left the procedure. france and bulgaria were de—escalated from excessive imbalances to imbalances. so was portugal, which still needs to address the high levels of public, private and external debt. we also see that the implementation of country specific recommendations has slightly improved as compared with last may. since 2011, member states have made at least some progress on more than two-thirds of their recommendations. when implementing reforms, member states can count on the support of our structural and investment funds as well as our structural reform support service. last week we adopted the annual work programme for the structural reform support programme to support 24 member states, with more than 140 projects. maybe the most important message of this semester is that economic growth and social progress go hand in hand. this year’s semester is a landmark. our country reports have never been so social. you know that we proclaimed the european pillar of social rights at the social summit a few months ago, and this is our joint political commitment by the commission, by the european parliament and by the council to move forward with the stronger social europe. but the success of the pillar depends on the extent to which we implement it at european level, but especially at national level. the semester is our key instrument to make the principles and rights included in the pillar a reality on the ground. following the proclamation, for the first time the principles of the pillar are now embedded in the semester. to help with monitoring and implementation we have developed, together with the member states, a social scoreboard with 14 headline indicators that cover the three dimensions of the pillar: equal opportunities and access to the labour market, to education also, dynamic labour markets and fair working conditions and public support, social protection and inclusion. ladies and gentlemen, we need to use the good times to build a resilient economy – one that is less vulnerable to economic shocks and more able to respond to them. for this, we need reforms that combine the goals of stability and efficiency with a strong focus on fairness and inclusiveness. that is why we are now starting the second phase of the 2018 european semester by taking the dialogue to the member states on the basis of the country reports, and such strengthened dialogue will be very valuable in the preparation for the 2018 set of country specific recommendations in may.
i would like to thank mr bayet for his good cooperation and his hard work on this report, which he will no doubt deliver up to and including the vote tomorrow. we have found a good balance, i think, in this report around three main points: first of all, the report calls for a good balance between public and private investment. investment can stimulate demand in the short term, which is crucial in the current economic context. secondly, sound fiscal policy is essential for sustainable economic growth. if we want to keep the public finances under control, we really need to start preparing for the elephant in the room: the increasing ageing. if we do not take responsibility now and make affordability of pensions and care a priority, then this is a culpable omission towards every young person in europe. thirdly: the commission has already taken a number of measures. productivity-enhancing structural reforms will not only attract investment but also increase real incomes. digitalisation and technological progress are fundamentally reshaping our labour market. we must move towards dynamic labour markets, supported by accessible social security systems. the commission will publish its country-specific recommendations on 23 may. i can only urge that all national parliaments and the social partners be more closely involved in this process.
the rapporteur. mr president, commissioner, minister, thank you for these exchanges. in conclusion, i would like to say that while economic growth is stronger in 2018, inequalities between our citizens are still far too present and therefore in the context of this coordination tool that is the semester and with this clear economic improvement, i think that we cannot go wrong. i believe that we really need strong action which is - as you said, madam commissioner - balanced and inclusive, and there is still a lot to be done in this respect, but i am particularly pleased - and it is partly thanks to you, mrs thyssen - with the full integration of the social pillar and its 14 indicators into the european semester because, contrary to what some people think, social is not just an expense but can also be a driver of the economy. there have been many studies showing that investing 2% of european gdp in the social sector would create almost 20 million jobs in europe, including women's jobs. it is therefore high time, if we are to be truly effective, that we analysed our economic policies from the social and environmental standpoints, and i am of course very pleased about this. finally, i hope that the commission and the council will follow us in taking strong measures for the benefit of the europeans. i believe that europe's survival is at stake.
mr president, let's be honest with each other. america is increasingly taking sides for itself, for america. they increasingly feel that their old allies should be left to fend for themselves. we see this in the middle east, where europe is expected to extract the hot coals from the fire itself. we see that in the debate on defence and nato, where america thinks we should start investing more ourselves. we see this in the free trade across the atlantic, which is not getting any easier. ttip is off the table. and today we are talking about import tariffs on aluminium and steel. let's not get fooled into thinking that either. this is not a new evolution. we've seen this before under obama, we're seeing it again under trump. i would therefore like to call for: let us look at ourselves first, let us not fall into the trap, let us not overreact, on the contrary. let's put on the glove, proud, with confidence, self-confidence. we are choosing not to have a trade war. we are against protectionism. we are in favour of fair and correct free trade because it is good for our businesses, our jobs and our consumers. a trade war doesn't make america and europe great again, it makes us all small. not great again.
mr president, i have just three questions about the debate we have had. the first question, commissioner, is whether you really believe that the wto today has the capacity to enforce the rules at international level and, if so, how do you intend to pursue this policy within the wto? we have all said that we are in favour of multilateralism, but we are seeing today that it is lacking in respect for the rules. secondly, as regards support for industry: we are not in favour of protectionism, of course, but free trade puts our industries under enormous pressure today in relation to competitors who do not respect a number of rules. how can we support our industries if we do not address this issue clearly? thirdly, we will have some spillover effects from the closure of the american markets. we talked about the chinese overproduction. how are we going to apply the new trade defence instruments, but also the significant distortion? are you prepared, commissioner, to activate the tools we have just voted for here in this house?
the us president is trying to pit eu member states against each other. the british tried that in the brexit negotiations: they came home from a cold fair. the eu has become increasingly aware that we are much stronger as a bloc of solidarity and unity in such conflict cases. if trump continues with his puberal measures, against the advice of almost all of his economic advisers, the eu has no choice but to respond. in the meantime, we must find out how to accelerate the roll-out of the concept of a cap on co2: the idea is embedded in the agreement on the reform of the ets system which we recently concluded. it would amount to paying a levy at european borders on co2-intensive products sold in the eu. products that are not produced using the cleanest production technologies are subject to additional costs, as in the european ets, the eu's emissions trading system. the commission has also proposed a number of measures to combat the use of these substances. but in particular, the heavily polluting imports from china could be tackled. in the light of the planned american recovery of the heavily polluting coal industry, this is a more than responsible exercise.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, last week, prime minister, it was in china, president xi jinping crowned himself emperor for life of that enormous continent. also last week, it was donald we will only count on ourselves in this new world order. in such a new world order, the key question is: are we ready now with the european union? my answer to this is: no! we are not ready for the moment, and i give always the example of how the americans reacted to the financial crisis, prime minister, and how we reacted to the financial crisis in 2008. they, in nine months, democrats and republicans, decided a three-stage rocket: cleaning up the banks – 400 billion tarp; an investment programme three times bigger than ours – 900 billion (they are the capitalists and we are the so-called keynesians, but the opposite is true); and at the same time, a quantitative easing programme of 1.2 trillion. and we, after nine years, are still discussing the banking union inside the european union. and we have still not cleaned up all our banks in europe. and that’s not the only example; the example is also valuable for other fields. the real problem we have is that we are still not a real union as our founding fathers wanted. we are still a confederation of nation states that is based on the unanimity rule. so what we need as reform – and you have indicated in a lot of your interventions – are three things. first of all, a european defence community. we are alone in the world of tomorrow, so we need to do that homework. we are spending 40% of american military expenditure and we are only capable of doing 10% of the operations of the american army. so there is a field where we need absolutely to create that community as fast as possible. my second point – you have indicated it and i agree completely with you: the new euro governance, a euro budget, a fiscal capacity. it is completely ridiculous to have a single currency and not behind that single currency a fiscal capacity and a full authority. it is not only with the stability and growth pact that we can do the business. finally, and that’s my third point (and here i’m in disagreement a little bit with you and with the tone of your intervention): we need also institutional reform, a renewed european democracy. we need, for example, a european parliament that is not only responsible for the expenditure but also for the income, as in any normal european democracy. we need also, in my opinion (as i said already to president juncker) not a commission of 28. let’s bring it back to a real european government of 12 or 15 people. and we need to get rid of the unanimity rule. let’s be honest, also in matters of foreign policy. i give the example now of what is happening in britain: this attack on european, on british soil. we have still no opinion of the european union. ms mogherini is completely silent about it. why? because she needs, for example, 28 agreements before she can take a position on such a crucial issue. i think it’s time that we did this reform now. why, mr prime minister? we have a window of opportunity of two years to do so. after brexit there was no nexit, no frexit, no dexit, fortunately. but it’s not won. there could be a frexit, a dexit and a nexit if, in the coming two years, we don’t succeed. and that’s your responsibility, and that’s our responsibility, and that’s in fact our common responsibility. so let’s do it now, in the coming two years.

author. mr president, the internet has become almost indispensable in almost every aspect of our lives. we send a whatsapp message to a loved one, plan a route through google maps, watch cat videos on kinds of cybercrime. unfortunately, this sometimes involves foreign governments. we're more vulnerable than we think. this is why national and european authorities must work together to ensure our online security today and tomorrow. we need to invest much more in prevention, detection and response. we have already strengthened our legal framework. the new data protection regulation will raise the standards of data protection. the commission proposal on eu certification for secure ict products also has a number of important implications. we prefer to pursue a high and international standard of security throughout the line. we shall not succeed by legislation alone, however, because it cannot always keep pace with the rapid technological developments. europol, the european police cooperation agency, will soon have a decryption expertise centre. that's good news. this should support smaller member states in cracking encrypted messages. this allows them to more quickly track and tackle cybercriminals who try to remain anonymous. the european commission is providing them with an additional 5 million for this purpose. the new europol director, catherine de bolle, has already stated that the fight against crime and terrorism cannot be separated from the development and use of new technology. national governments also need to realize that old-fashioned policing must go hand in hand with embracing technological innovation. that concern also applies to the judiciary. while criminals use the latest gadgets, police and law enforcement often rely on chases with outdated equipment. but we also have to look at the civilians. governments need to make them more aware of the risks of the internet, so that they are more alert to various forms of cybercrime. they should also be encouraged to always report if they are the victim of cyberbullying, phishing or hacking, for example. it's not happening enough, but without that report, the police can't act. reporting is therefore needed to get a better view of cybercrime so that we can tackle it more targeted. finally, i would point out that data is a valuable asset, not only for the private user who wishes to protect it, but also for the police services. our police and intelligence services need access to data to detect crimes and to detect terrorist plots in time. a too dogmatic view of privacy prevents the police and the judiciary from doing their job. that is what i have heard during my work as co-rapporteur in the special committee on terrorism. colleagues, in the interest of our citizens, we must strike a balance between privacy and security. it is not an "either/or" discussion, but an "and" discussion. both privacy and security are important. police services need to be able to do their job, which is to protect us from terrorists and criminals both offline and online.

on behalf of the s&d group. madam president, i would first like to thank paul and alain for this important report, which was awaited by civil society, because, of course, all our fellow citizens have suffered from the various tax scandals luxleaks, panama papers, and so on. all these scandals have one thing in common: large multinationals pay almost no tax by playing on the 28 tax systems in the union. whether we are citizens, smes or multinationals, it is normal, fair and ethical for everyone to contribute to society in proportion to their income, because it is tax money that funds social security, health care, schools, public transport, infrastructure and the development of our economy. this was obviously and scandalously forgotten by many multinationals. the result is that each year european states lose 1 000 billion in tax revenue. this is obviously unbearable! the aim of the two directives we are discussing today is to arrive at an identical definition of taxable profit in european countries, in order to put an end to the game of multinationals, which seek to take advantage of the differences between our tax systems in the union. in concrete terms, this accis project proposes that there should be only one place of taxation, one contact with a tax administration for all multinationals operating in the union. at first, only large companies with a turnover of more than 500 million will be affected, but parliament has strengthened the directive proposed by the commission, extending the scope of this directive to all companies within seven years. i think that's a good thing. under the new law, companies operating across borders will have to comply with a single european system for determining taxable income, rather than the different national regimes under which the activity is carried out. we have also introduced measures to tax digital companies, which must also contribute their fair share, given the enormous profits they have made. these two directives should therefore, in part, address current concerns on corporate taxation. a drawback, unfortunately, was that we could not agree on a minimum tax rate. this tax rate would, however, allow for real tax harmonisation at european level and should reduce intra-european competition, which is currently too strong. we are counting on you, commissioner, for this important matter. the answer is 'blue card'. madam president, i will be very brief, because i think my colleague has not yet understood a lot of things about tax evasion. we've been getting the same stuff for years. no, it is impossible for a multinational to deprive itself of one of the world's largest markets - 500 million consumers. when we all agree to impose higher tax rates, they will stay at home because they cannot do without our european market. and i'm not at the disposal of the multinationals.
i would like to see every european country attracting businesses and creating jobs. i want the small member states to have a chance too and not all of it to go to the large member states. i want countries to retain their powers in taxation and therefore we should emphasize that the european union is a platform for cooperation and not a country, and that it is therefore not for the european union to impose taxes. for all these reasons, i am against these proposals for the cctb and for a ccctb. i know that these terms, cctb and so on, sound rather dry and technical and like accounting jargon, but they are not. this goes way beyond that. this is much more fundamental. this is about our fiscal policy and the end of it. this is about a transfer from small to large member states, because that is what companies will do. they will shift their businesses, their profits and their jobs from smaller, innovative economies to the larger economies in the european union. france and germany, because there are more factories, because there is a bigger market. we are opposed to that. fortunately, we're not alone. there are a number of other delegations doing this as well. i am surprised that the socialist party is supporting this proposal, that it seems to prefer jobs in germany and france to jobs in flanders, wallonia and brussels. i know where our priorities are. it's with our people and our economy.
president. - mr president, we have been advocating for years for a level playing field and fair taxation. the ccctb is a very important step in this direction. i would therefore ask you today to join me in supporting this report. finally, the tax base will be the same throughout the european union, with one objective in mind: tax value where it is created. but we do not want to eliminate competition between member states. on the contrary, by creating a level playing field, there will be competition in three main areas: the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve the situation in the community. this will strengthen the competitiveness of our member states and, ultimately, of the european union as a whole. as a corollary, our companies will have to rely on one system instead of 27 different ones, which will reduce the administrative burden considerably. this one system is also a strong basis for taxing the digital giants like facebook, google and amazon. it also creates a framework to arm us against attacks on our european tax revenues by third countries. in summary, the ccctb stands for more revenue borne by a wider base and a union that is stronger in the world. this is a historic opportunity. i hope therefore that the council will take our advice to heart and will come up with results quickly. president, asylum and migration are important issues. our citizens are concerned about the relentless influx of migrants. they are concerned about our porous external borders and the socioeconomic challenges and security risks associated with them. i still believe that we in the union must move towards a system where recognised refugees can only be transferred by resettlement, so that we know who is entering and can enforce a maximum limit, but also so that families no longer risk their lives in shabby boats because they know that illegal entry is no longer rewarded with legal residence. the dialogue is also taking place at international level. i am pleased that agreements are being made on such a resettlement policy for europe, but also worldwide. i would call on the negotiators to show the necessary ambition and realism and also to pay sufficient attention to the return of illegal economic migrants, so that the support of the population increases. make a clear distinction between vulnerable refugees who have found no safe haven abroad and those seeking happiness. i do find it worrying that parliament is calling for the decriminalisation of humanitarian aid, pointing to italy, which seized an ngo boat on suspicion of aiding human smugglers. despite orders from the italian rescue and coordination centre and the eu-trained libyan coast guard, the ngo took the migrants to a european hotspot. humanitarian organizations are not above the law or the agreements made.
the european parliament has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the future of the european union. it is not a binding framework, but it is a framework in which we will encourage international cooperation to tackle this phenomenon. we are about to start the debate here and everyone is talking about the negative effects of migration. i would like to say briefly that, fortunately, migration is positive in many cases. people who have the freedom to live, work and live and move within the world. but here today we are talking specifically about migration that people are concerned about, about illegal migration, about refugees and migrants. and i want to make a plea here for one specific group, namely the children in migration. can i ask you to close your eyes for 30 seconds, or let's say 10 because i don't have that much time to talk, and think back to your own childhood? we can happily look back on happy childhoods, on playing in freedom. children on the run, they can't do that. children on the run, fleeing chemical weapons, bombings, are put on a dingy boat by human traffickers at risk of drowning. commissioner, i want to make a statement today to focus on children in migration. europol has also said that ten thousand unaccompanied minor asylum seekers are missing in europe. let's make a real plan of action. let us make sure that the global pacts focus on the children. no more child custody. we must make a commitment to registration, to family reunification, to the humane care of the most vulnerable. a child in migration is first and foremost a child and deserves our protection.
i would like to point out, mr president, that there is a further persistent confusion about migration. the idea that we should respect the human rights of migrants on european soil has been replaced by an idea that was not intended at all, namely that migration is a human right. the hungarian government has understood this very well, and in a document of barely two pages, the 'proposals by hungary to the un's global compact on migration', paragraph 3 states: "migration is not a basic human right". and they are. and that is why we in the enf are in favour of the idea that the geneva convention should be revised as soon as possible. it's from a very different time. she was meant for something else entirely. and it hinders a normal and modern view of migration, which is adapted to what the population of europe is feeling at the moment about the migration that has happened to them.
in writing. if we are to reverse the growing reluctance of parts of our societies towards migrants, we need to show citizens that migration policy can be changed. we must offer european citizens and migrants an alternative to the images of dilapidated boats in the mediterranean, the jungles of calais or the misery of overcrowded asylum centres and refugee camps. the global compacts are therefore an important opportunity which we must pursue with ambition. i am proud that the european parliament can put forward a strong text today. in many parts of the world, migration is both a challenge for society and an opportunity for the economy. to avoid falling short of the challenge and to reap the rewards of this opportunity, we must work together. this means financial solidarity for vulnerable regions and the common organisation of legal migration channels. good immigration policy strengthens people rather than making them more vulnerable. i am therefore pleased that the text also pays particular attention to children, who already make up a significant proportion of the number of migrants, but will do so even more in the coming years. parliament reaffirms today that human rights are the key to, and not the obstacle to, sound policy.

the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to cover the protection of personal data. moreover, such data is often sold without the knowledge of the individual. the right to privacy and the protection of the digital space are therefore more important than ever. as legislators, we have a duty to protect our citizens from abuse of privacy, but we also have a duty to ensure the safety of our citizens. the right to privacy cannot be an absolute right. today, we see very clearly that terrorists too often slip through the cracks of the net by abusing the right to privacy. investigators complain that they often run after the facts. for example, they can't access stored data like whatsapp messages in which terrorists discuss attacks. the right to privacy allows these terrorists to continue their criminal activities anonymously and with impunity. this is an unintended and undesirable side effect of the right to privacy. so we need to work on a more balanced balance between privacy and security. it is not the intention to protect one to destroy the other. privacy and security should be able to coexist, not be separated. we must therefore prevent the misuse of digital space, whether by tech giants or by criminals and terrorists.
mr president, the scandal we are talking about today with cambridge analytica and facebook reminds me in some ways of the other scandal we have already had to deal with in this legislation: the dieselgate scandal. the comparisons are a large multinational which is shockingly damaging the confidence of europeans and other people in the world. that we are dealing with powerful multinationals is clear. it's about millions of data points that are also going to make billions. you can see it very clearly: in 2006, the five most important, largest and most powerful companies were all energy and oil companies. and today -- i don't know if that's a good evolution, probably a little bit -- are the big five, the big data and internet companies. that's kind of the point i want to make in addition to the justified plea for e-privacy and the justified pride we may feel in our own regulation, but we also need to look at it more broadly. it's about something that has a very big and deep impact on our society, it's about companies that today pay little or no tax, and that also needs to be part of the solution when we look at this scandal.
president. - in the weeks and months before the paris attacks, the terrorist salah abdeslam used an anonymous prepaid credit card from bpost to travel around europe. this is just one example of how terrorists and other criminals abuse the eu's internal borders. using anonymous credit cards, virtual currencies, and shady companies of all kinds, criminals can conduct financial transactions undetected. that is why i welcome the measures adopted today. stricter rules on the anonymity of prepaid credit cards and virtual currencies such as bitcoin, more transparency on key figures behind bank accounts and companies, and better data exchange between authorities within and between member states, as timely access to the right information is essential in an investigation. so, steps in the right direction, but more is needed. let us therefore speed up the implementation of additional measures in the fight against terrorism.
i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr dalton, for all his good work. i think the dieselgate has exposed a harsh reality, namely that the current rules have failed. i am therefore very pleased with these new, more efficient rules which send a strong political signal to close the loopholes in the legislative net. that way we can make it clear that this can and should never happen again. it is only through good cooperation between the member states and europe that we will be able to protect our public health, our environment and, in short, the european citizen. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's energy policy is not undermined by the fact that the community is not able to meet its obligations under the treaty.
mr president, it is now more than two years since we heard that one of the giants of the car industry had tampered with the emission measurements of their cars. as a result, a committee of inquiry was set up, and we worked together to find out what caused it. but more importantly, we had to find a way to prevent such consumer fraud in the future. we then made a number of recommendations based on our findings. when i look at the proposal before us today, i am pleased to see that many of these have been taken up. a proposal for lower emissions and cleaner air for our children and grandchildren is therefore a proposal i support.
the promotion and support of organic farming is a legitimate political priority for the european union. because organic is a double win: the commission has already proposed a number of measures to improve the quality of the products. despite strong growth in recent decades, the share of organic farming in the european agricultural area is only 6%. my region of flanders is even lagging behind. only a good 1% of our land is organic. it's got to be much better than that, because we're also seeing exponential demand. europe cannot even cope with the demand for organic food, and so organic food is increasingly being imported. the question now is: how do we make sure our farmers choose organic farming? how can we strengthen them and at the same time safeguard their competitive position? so, how do we make bioproduct interesting? the report contains a number of interesting points and also a groundbreaking innovation: finally, farmers will be able to produce in a mixed way. provided that the activities are clearly separated, traditional farming may be combined with organic farming. this is a way for farmers to make the transition gradually. the two or three years of transition are over, and that is a good thing. at least as important: the eu has a long history of providing financial support to non-eu countries. i think that is very important for fair competition. this legislation has cost blood, sweat and tears, but after 20 months of negotiations and 18 trialogue meetings, i think europe can be proud. it's me, all right.
on behalf of the group of the european right, i would like to thank mr ramon, rapporteur, and my dear friend, for your hard and excellent work here in parliament. commissioner, it is always a pleasure to see your passion and enthusiasm for european competition policy. you're right, of course. unity in diversity is the slogan of the european union, but some people put more emphasis on unity. we all have to do the same. member states must start to transcend their own individuality. diversity is seen as a weakness. we must ensure that there is no competition, and certainly not between countries. the opposite is true. the opposite is true, our european civilization is different from others precisely because we have seen this rivalry, this competition throughout our history. it's what makes us who we are. the competition between kingdoms, between principalities, between city states has ensured that we still live through the european miracle and that the european miracle has been made possible. we'll see in the results. we have cities that are flourishing, we have trade networks, we have schools, we have universities. we see the flowering of philosophy and of science, our literature, our culture. all of this has been made possible by competition, including our unprecedented economic progress. not in spite of our political fragmentation, but because of the very fact that we are different and we compete with each other. our diversity is a strength. it's not a weakness, and we have to cherish it. so today i ask that we celebrate that competition. let us celebrate the report of our good friend ramon tremosa. let us not succumb to any imposed urge of absolute equality. unity, yes, but in diversity.
member of the commission. – madam president, i have listened very carefully. let me start with one remark. i vaccinate myself every year against influenza. i did it many years ago against hepatitis and yellow fever also. as a cardio-surgeon, i had an obligation to vaccinate myself. why? because i was responsible. because it was dangerous for my patients if there was any possibility of my transmitting infection from me to my patients. in response to arne gericke, a very simple message: let’s do it together and let’s save children’s health. when we are talking about children’s rights, about the convention on children’s rights, children have the right to as high as possible a level of health protection. it does not matter that sometimes parents do not understand it, but who is responsible for saving children’s lives? only the parents? no. we are all responsible. the state has a responsibility to guarantee conventional requirements, to implement. of course some countries have mandatory vaccination, but more than half of european union countries have mandatory vaccinations and calendars. that is ok. some have only voluntary. ok. some have a mixture. but we know very well that awareness, information and possibilities and our personal willingness to raise awareness and to be together and extend our health immunity is our own obligation also. i am a medical doctor. for me it is very difficult to listen to today’s issues. ms rivasi raised some very important questions. you know that pharmacolovigilance is in place. you know that all vaccines go through very thorough scientific assessment, through who checks, through ema checks. no, no, it is true. and we are always blaming conflicts of interest and showing up a bad industry. once again there are fears in this house. please present the evidence. that is the question. and of course we know very well that conflicts of interest should be addressed and we have a lot of tools to do this. speaking about general food law, we know very well we will also discuss issues about conflicts of interest. efsa, ema or ecdc it does not matter which. but look, that is not the main problem. the main problem is how to really encourage people and restore trust in science, and be together. you and i, we are both responsible for that. we can go on addressing issues and disseminating messages on pandemics saying these are because of industry or experts. i am sorry. what about rabies? in writing. in one year, the number of europeans infected with measles has quadrupled to over 20 000. of those, 35 did not survive the disease. this trend confirms that the declining vaccination rate poses a huge risk to public health across the eu. more and more parents and patients are delaying or even refusing to vaccinate their children. diseases that were almost eradicated in europe, such as measles, are reappearing with disastrous consequences. the positive effect of vaccination is undeniable. every vaccination counts, because the better the population is vaccinated, the harder it is for a germ to spread. it is a matter of solidarity, so that people with reduced immunity can also be protected. it is also unacceptable that years of medical progress should be undermined by misleading information. it's not possible because of the political frenzy of fake news about vaccines for electoral gain. medical staff have a critical role to play in providing patients with clear information about vaccines so that everyone has a fair chance of protection.

and i must say that i have been able to quote some startling figures for belgium. in 2017, 124 missing children were reported to child focus in belgium. and do you know how many at the custody service? countries. and then there is the big cliff, the future of the eurozone. the big debate for the next few months. i am pleased to note that belgium is also increasingly taking the side of the realists. committed to the european project, not for a romantic, happy europe, disconnected from reality, but for a europe which is really focused on an ambition for results. results for the fundamental issues. we are convinced that europe can and must become more and more a source of value for our fellow citizens, more jobs and more purchasing power. we are convinced that we must work together more closely national rules. we have no lessons to learn on the subject and we assume the real subject. we do not think we will escape a debate on legal, orderly migration channels. we do believe that this debate should take place and we are prepared to conduct it with a high degree of commitment. finally, a few belgian questions on nuclear safety. i would like to briefly mention the belgian initiative on nuclear safety. we have made a clear choice: get out of nuclear energy in 2025. is that easy? no, that's not easy. is it an ambition? yes, that is an ambition, but we want to do everything we can to achieve results. we have in recent weeks within the council of ministers, with the support of the states, confirmed commitments in this regard. as far as safety is concerned, we are in constant contact with our neighbouring countries, and also with international monitoring in this respect, for example with germany, as well as with other countries. i notice that some people are systematically trying to cause anxiety, panic and worry. we are assuming our responsibilities by entrusting the nuclear control to independent bodies. mr lamberts, the government you don't like - and i understand that - is the government that has developed interconnections with neighbouring countries, investments, and an offshore wind farm, which is growing more and more. belgium is the second country after france to have introduced green bonds: €5 billion mobilised from belgium in support of the energy transition. finally, i would like to touch on a number of other subjects which have been raised, in particular the european budget. the european budget is of course a crucial moment and we must certainly make a clear choice, a choice of arbitration. i don't think anyone underestimates how important it is to make timely decisions. i have clearly said and i repeat: this is a useful proposal from the european commission. that's a starting point. but at the same time we also think from belgium that we must do everything to spend better resources. that makes sense. but we are also realistic. we also know that we must, if need be, consolidate our classical policies, our classical initiatives from the european union. that is also crucial. the new policies open you up, strengthen you and make you possible. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the there is currently conflicting case law in belgium on this subject, i.e. the rule of law, the independence of the judiciary. the government is looking at the legal possibilities of a subject which i feel is important to a number of people who have been in our country for a long time. these are some of the elements i wanted to mention. you got it. i thank you for this lively, intense debate. be convinced, however, with all the nuance but also with all the force of commitment, that belgium has the ambition to remain in the cockpit of a project in which we believe with great fervour because, as guy verhofstadt has rightly said, history has shown that the path of cooperation, peace, dialogue and the pooling of forces is the path to guarantee freedom, democracy and the rule of law, which, i repeat, we hold as the apple of our eye.

vice-president of the commission. – mr president, first i really would like to thank you for the positive reaction and what i feel was overall support. you are right, there are three of us here to demonstrate how important the mobility issues and all the packages we presented to you are for the commission, and you are also right that it was a collective effort and the result of very good energy union team spirit. i would like to assure you that we are ready to use this positive spirit to work with you, day and night, and if you are talking about trilogies, you’re speaking mostly about the night sessions. but we are ready to work with you to make sure that we use every single day. we still have almost a one year ahead of us to make sure that all the proposals we put to you for the area of modernising mobility will be approved. so we can really start the next decade to be the new framework and with all the efforts which are necessary to make sure the citizens are safer, our roads are better and our industry is much more competitive. if you allow, mr president, i know that we are running out of time, so i just wanted to save some time for my colleagues, for miguel and violeta, also to contribute to the debate with their responses.
member of the commission. – mr president, i would like to thank all of you for your contributions in the debate on your support for the commission proposals. following the intervention of ms dalli regarding the achievement of our target of reducing greenhouse gas emissions by at least 40% compared with 1990 by 2030, let me start with some clarifications and explain how the transport sector will contribute. for sure, the commission is putting forward a different set of proposals in order to achieve those targets, but in the new effort sharing regulation, we set out binding national greenhouse gas emission targets for the period 2021-2030 for member states. these targets cover all the sectors of the economy that fall outside the scope of the european union emissions trading system, including road transport. no sectoral targets are set under the effort sharing regulation. nevertheless, the analytical work underpinning the effort sharing regulation proposals shows the cost—effectiveness of a european union-wide emission reduction of 25% compared with 2005 for road transport by 2030. under the baseline, emission reductions are around 70%, so there is an eight percentage point difference. with our 2017 proposals for post-2020 emissions standards for cars and vans, this difference gets significantly smaller – it shrinks to four percentage points, and the gap could be progressively closed by one percentage point with the implementation of the proposed co2 standards for lorries, which have to reduce their emissions by 30% compared with 2019 by 2030. the remaining gap will be addressed through other additional policies with impact on road transport greenhouse gas emissions. these concern, for example, the minimum share of renewable fuels in transport, which is proposed by the commission in the revision of the renewable energy directive as well as the proposals for the eurovignette, clean vehicles and combined transport directives. with all this legislation, we will make sure that transport also contributes to achieving our 40% minimum reduced emissions target by 2030. to conclude, let me stress that the commission has tabled ambitious and realistic proposals which we think strike the right balance between environmental, consumer and employment objectives. that is why the commission would like to call on our colleagues later and all the stakeholders to work closely together to ensure the swift adoption and implementation of the proposal for co2 emissions standards for lorries – so that the benefits for european union industry, businesses and citizens can be maximised and generated as soon as possible.
member of the commission. – mr president, honourable members, thank you very much for all your comments and the real support for the mobility package. i take on board your criticism that we might have introduced this package a little bit late into our mandate but, believe me, it was done in a very systematic and comprehensive way, so i do believe that together we can really deliver on it, with the confidence that we will also deliver on the results, and we are all really looking forward to those results. i am very much in favour of removing silent killers from european society, and transport is contributing a few of them, so please give your full support to three very clear vision zeros. zero fatalities on european roads, zero pollution from european transport and zero red tape in mobility solutions by 2050. i know it’s ambitious, but it is achievable if we join our forces and we are really serious about delivering on this objective. for that reason, i’m very much looking forward to engaging immediately with you and seeing how far we can get under our mandate, and i'm sure that the commission and the commissioner that will come after me will be equally devoted to these goals and we will deliver on those so badly needed objectives by 2050.
with the third mobility package proposed by the european commission on 17 may, we are taking an important step towards drastically reducing the number of road traffic fatalities. in 2017, 25 300 people were killed in traffic accidents in the european union. that's 25,300 too many. the measures announced will equip cars, trucks and buses in the coming years, starting from the end of 2021, with, inter alia, an emergency braking system to protect vulnerable road users, a black box in each car, an intelligent speed assistant and a reversing camera. the mapping of dangerous traffic points and additional investments in infrastructure should also drastically reduce the number of victims. we must do everything we can to reduce the number of road deaths to zero as quickly as possible.

we can travel freely within the schengen area without passport controls. but this mutual trust between the countries cannot be achieved without strong common protection of the eu's external borders. unfortunately, these external borders are still porous, as european courts prohibit pushbacks. boats are still being transported to europe for rescue instead of to the last safe country of transit. i therefore think it is perfectly justifiable for member states to be able to temporarily reintroduce internal border controls. this report, however, condemns this approach. the commission is also critical of the systematic checks on eu citizens coming from outside the eu, including returning syrian fighters, at zaventem airport, for example. we think that criticism is very inappropriate from a security point of view. the reintroduction of border controls within the eu does indeed entail economic costs. but it is precisely because the eu is doing too little to strengthen these external borders that member states want to know who is going in and out. border controls, like targeted police action, are sometimes necessary.
