increasingly being constructed through and thanks to the european union. we also share your vision of a free, strong and open european union and your desire to speed up rather than slow down its construction. a few days after the entry into force of the famous general regulation on data protection, i would like to remind everyone that it was under the luxembourg presidency that agreement was reached on this pioneering text, which was also put on the rails by the former commissioner viviane reding, also from luxembourg. and you will allow me to claim the pride that it was a green minister from your government, félix braz, who concluded the negotiation with the green rapporteur of this parliament, our colleague jan philipp albrecht. we are also pleased to note that, like your irish colleague who in january inaugurated this round of debates, your government has advanced the values of equality and, in particular, women's rights. this is all the more remarkable as within the european union some member states want to go backwards in this area. i am also pleased that your government has given the ecological transition a greater place in public policy. i would like to emphasize the positive role played by carole dieschbourg at the paris climate conference. it is quite logical that on the climate front your government supports the european parliament's position, which is much more ambitious than that of the council and the commission. in this area, you are committing luxembourg to renewable energy, but also to a spatial planning policy that respects our common assets: soil, air and water. similarly, while the grand duchy was the unholy protagonist of the luxleaks saga, i am pleased that it is now supporting the draft european directive on the protection of whistleblowers, who are so essential to our democracy. and you see where i am going with this, because while we as european environmentalists are generally proud of the european and national achievements of the government in which our luxembourg friends work, there is still a big black spot between us: taxation. since the 2013 offshore leaks revelations, a litany of scandals have revealed how the world's largest corporations and the world's richest people evade and cheat on their taxes. the grand duchy is certainly not the only actor in this senseless tax competition between the member states and between europe and the rest of the world. but it is hard to deny that your country shares responsibility, especially alongside its benelux neighbours. the european commission was right to criticise luxembourg for its aggressive tax practices. our wealthy belgian families know something about this, who have invested no less than 48 billion euros in your country by setting up letterbox companies. and many of our red devils, our footballers, did not hesitate to transfer a large part of their advertising revenue to luxembourg in order to benefit from your far too lax intellectual property protection regime. the european action of the grand duchy in the tax field is also largely disappointing. when your finance minister does not play the clock to delay the introduction of a tax on the digital giants, he is blocking without hesitation initiatives that are in the interests of the european taxpayer - those taxpayers, as manfred said, who do not have the leisure to play one state against another - in particular the introduction of public country-by-country reporting for multinationals or the implementation of a common and consolidated corporate tax base. in short, your opposition in principle to any form of tax harmonization, on the grounds that it would mean a reduction in taxes, at a time when multinationals pay almost nothing. there is talk of an increase in taxes, indeed, and it is necessary and justified. i would say that from the country with the highest per capita income in the european union, i think europeans could expect a hand to be extended rather than a fist to be clenched. mr prime minister, i would like to conclude by dedicating my speech to camille gira, your secretary of state for sustainable development, who died on 16 may. he was a pioneer of political ecology in your country, notably by turning his village of beckerich into a showcase for ecology, with 100% renewable energy, but above all with unprecedented citizen participation. its model of citizen-led ecological transition has inspired many green proxies across europe. ecology will miss him and we will miss him. he was one of ours.

mr president, with the golden visas we are really facing a mechanism that does not smell good, i would even say smells bad. it is a shame that european citizenship can be bought, even with dirty money. moreover, in many cases the purpose of the residence permit is not to actually reside in the country that issued it, but to move with impunity within the european union and to do business there. it is a private pension obtained by the sale of a european common good. thirdly, and perhaps most dangerously, there is more control in the european system for obtaining a short-stay visa than for obtaining a long-stay visa, which will allow you to do business indefinitely in the territory of the union. in the face of this, the council and the commission are heard saying: 'we are going to remind the member states of the principle of sincere cooperation'. do you think the member states are listening to you? you can see they're not listening to you. so there is only one thing to do: we must make long-stay visas a collective responsibility, as is the case with short-stay visas.
president, eleven member states in the european union have programmes that grant residence permits to foreign investors. let me be clear that i do not necessarily think that is a bad thing. on the contrary, such programmes can be a way of attracting valuable investment and encouraging entrepreneurship. but granting citizenship is not about double standards, and that's why we're here this afternoon. if the issuing of these visas is not accompanied by adequate integrity checks, we risk giving free rein to criminals and money laundering rings. then it's purely selling to the highest bidder. i am fully aware that the granting of citizenship is an exclusive matter for a member state, but europe has the right to speak out on this. what one member state decides has consequences for all of us. citizenship automatically gives access to the internal market and allows us to enjoy our freedoms. of course, this is not by chance the ultimate selling point for such constructions. and there goes the shoe. it is absolutely absurd that we have no idea who is granted european citizenship and how. so let me be clear. there is nothing wrong with a golden visa programme in principle, but member states must take their responsibility in this. criminals with money in their pockets are given a free pass to launder their funds through these programmes, and that is simply not possible. we must thoroughly investigate these programmes within the committee on financial crimes, tax evasion and tax avoidance. we need to know who is getting these visas, what the criteria are and how the preliminary examination is going. we owe it to the citizens of europe.
i am very pleased to see that the commission has taken the initiative to put forward a proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment in the event of a natural disaster. the european second-hand car market continues to be plagued by odometer fraud, with an annual economic cost of as much as €8.77 billion. you cannot solve this alone, because the manipulation of odometers is not just a problem of european transport: it also concerns the internal market and consumer rights. yet your colleagues will be absent today as well. yet the solution is obvious. together with your colleagues, you could propose a realistic framework, with mileage being recorded and information shared with member states and consumers. the references to belgium and the netherlands are correct. in our country, the introduction of the simple car pass has reduced fraud by 97%. you hear it, there is unanimity in parliament on the urgency and the solution. now we have unanimity in the commission, where the various commissioners continue to push the envelope. i listened to you carefully and you opened the umbrella a little by referring to the existing instruments, such as the directive on driving tests. i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in this. more frequent registrations are needed and the consumer must also have access to the data. this is not so much a technical dossier as it is primarily about consumers and citizens. the good news is: we can solve the problem without interference, regulatory overreach or complicated procedures.
the rapporteur. madam president, europe has entered a new dynamic, that of the circular economy, that of the preservation of our raw materials, which have already become so scarce. eco-design, which we will be discussing this evening, is a key pillar of this. this means taking into account all environmental impacts on biodiversity, water, soil, air, noise and, of course, energy consumption during product design. i would say that this is logical, since this phase of product manufacture is responsible for 80% of environmental pollution and 90% of the costs for producers. in 2005 i was already rapporteur on the first framework directive on the subject , the objective at the time was limited and aimed solely at reducing greenhouse gas emissions by improving energy efficiency. today, we can no longer focus solely on this energy efficiency issue. europe must change its paradigm and act on better use of resources and on the recyclability of products, an ambition that was very widely shared by the members of the environment committee, who adopted, by near unanimity, on 25 april, my report on the implementation of the 2009 directive. i would also like to thank all the members of the committee, and especially the non-existent rapporteurs, with whom i had a very good working relationship: francesc gambús for the epp, massimo paolucci for the s&d, nikolay brekov for ecr, kateřina konečná for the gue/ngl, margrete auken for the greens, eleonora evi for the efdd and sylvie goddyn for enf. i would remind you that this is a report on the situation and on implementation. eco-design requirements for a range of energy-intensive products have borne fruit: i am thinking of air heaters, boilers, computers, lamps, televisions and household appliances in general. the numbers speak for themselves. the eco-design policy, coupled also, it must be said, with the energy labelling legislation, is generating savings that are greater than italy's annual energy consumption, nothing less, or 175 million tonnes of oil equivalent. if we take the analysis to a level closer to us, that is to say to consumers, this represents a saving of 490 euros per household per year on energy bills. several studies by the uk authorities confirm this success, including one published in 2015, which concluded that every pound invested in eco-design was worth 3.8 times more to the uk economy and therefore a cost-effective policy. however, this blue sky that i'm drawing here, is still dotted with a few clouds. there is some disagreement, particularly unanimous criticism of the persistent delays in the decision-making process. the commission must therefore, commissioner, remedy this situation, and we have made this clear in paragraphs 7 to 14 of our report. european manufacturers must be able to rely on clear deadlines for implementing measures. this is important for their strategy and to maintain fair competition with non-european companies. but on the whole, the reasons for satisfaction are there and lead us, of course, to look further and to want, firstly, to extend eco-design measures to energy-related products, which have the greatest potential for saving, such as solar panels, refrigerated containers and hand dryers. secondly, we intend to rely on other criteria, different, additional criteria, to set our requirements. this could be the durability of our dishwashers, our refrigerators, the repairability - we have put a lot of emphasis on this - and the availability of spare parts, for example for tvs and so-called all-in-one printers. we believe that it is essential that the commission should find a solution in its work plan for the selection of criteria. this has been an essential part of our discussions on mobile phones. i know this is not easy given the life span of mobile phones, on average from 6 months to 2 years of use depending on whether they simply die or whether they go out of style. their environmental record also needs to be improved. i am convinced - i am speaking here as a user - that smartphones should be made with removable components, particularly the battery, and should be accompanied by clear information on their possibilities for reuse and recycling. market surveillance also needs to be improved. the commission estimates that between 10 and 25% of the products covered by the directive do not comply with the ecodesign requirements. the responsibility for this lies with the member states. at european union level, ... in writing. ecodesign and energy labelling are often portrayed by eurosceptics as absurd, pointless european interference. europe should not care which vacuum we buy! but with these two regulations we can meet half our energy saving target by 2020, reduce co2 emissions by 320 million tonnes a year and the bills of every european family by 500 euros, generate 55 billion euros for our industry and create 800 000 new jobs. it is therefore clear that europe must do something about this. and i want it to be a priority. ecological design must include more products and more criteria. the focus is now on energy consumption alone. we want to extend this to include sustainability aspects such as recyclability, use of recycled materials, recoverability, easy dismantling, availability of spare parts and regulation of rare or harmful raw materials. the minimum product life and guarantee periods should also be included. but the first step is to apply the rules correctly, and there the shoe is twisting again. non-compliance is currently estimated at between 10 and 25% of products on the market. this would result in a loss of 10% of the potential savings. this corresponds to the total energy production of the belgian nuclear power station. we can't let this go on any longer.

on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, after the financial crisis, when the european market infrastructure regulation (emir) was put in place, it resulted – and we welcomed that – not only in more transparency on the derivatives market, but also in more central clearing, which is a welcome development. there is still progress to be made, and the first positive aspect of the text that we are going to adopt is that we insist that central clearing should go up; the share of the market that should be centrally cleared cannot go down, it has to go up. second, this idea of having more conservative methods to decide whether small financial counterparties are below the clearing threshold is also welcome. on these pension funds: i remember the study carried out by the european securities and markets authority (esma) showing how badly pension funds were funded. when pension funds have to resort not only to derivatives, but to over-the-counter derivatives to run their business, then something is probably badly wrong. they made promises that they cannot possibly hold. i do not think that this is a reason to exclude them from the obligation of central clearing. the two—plus—one is welcome, and they cannot complain about that; they have to run their businesses in a sustainable way. we are going to vote on this report, but i would finish by saying this: warren buffett mentioned these derivatives as weapons of mass financial destruction. this was not by chance. these products are used in such volumes that they dwarf the real economy. 30 or 40 years ago, we had a working economy and we did not have this mass of derivatives. there are probably a lot of these derivatives that are unneeded and dangerous, and this is still a work in progress. secondly, central clearing houses are becoming ‘too big to fail’ institution. they are systemic, and we should better regulate on how we deal with them in case of crisis.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's air transport policy is not disrupted by the introduction of new technologies. summer vacation is just around the corner. many vacationers will soon be flying back to their favorite destination, and they want to do so in a safe and comfortable way. perhaps others will buy their first drone this summer or a european entrepreneur will bring the next innovation to the industry. more and more people are getting involved in aviation, and the figures show that. in 2035, 14.4 million passenger flights will be operated in europe. that's 50% more than in 2012. we are also seeing this steep rise in the drone sector, which will create 100 000 jobs over the next 20 years. this is good for the economy, employment and competitiveness of the european union. but let us not forget the safety of passengers and the impact on man and the environment. today we are taking an important step in the right direction. we embrace promising innovations, but also provide the necessary safety framework. proportionality, less red tape and a sense of reality are key. in this way, we facilitate innovative ideas and save the recreational aviation industry a lot of headaches. and we also have specific rules for drones. a vital step in safely integrating these unmanned aircraft into our airspace. as with other devices, manufacturers and operators will have to pay attention to security, privacy and protection of personal data in the future. a measure that benefits both the users of drones and the people on the ground. finally, i would ask the european aviation safety agency to continue its good work and to carry out its new set of tasks with diligence.

i thank you, madam president. a year and a half is a long time in european politics. a year and a half ago, mr. verhofstadt was negotiating with mr. beppe grillo. he wanted the italian five star movement in his alde group. meanwhile, love seems to have cooled a bit. a year and a half ago, all that was talked about was spreading, spreading, spreading migrants, as if that was the solution to the asylum and migration crisis. in the meantime, we are also talking about something else, we are talking about stronger european borders, we are talking about pushbacks, we are talking about more and better reception in the region. little by little, the insights come, the common sense comes, one realizes that this is the only way to control mass migration, to crack down on human traffickers, and to finally stop those drownings in the mediterranean. that is the understanding that exists, at least in the member states, but unfortunately in this european parliament it is still far too little. that's why the next six months are going to be so crucial. austria will then chair the european council. i wish mr kurz all the best in pushing this realistic agenda forward in the future.
on behalf of the group of the european right thank you, mr president. today we are debating the creation of an emf, a european monetary fund, the european variant of the imf. the fund could help member states with bad paperwork. an interesting idea in itself, but it's not clear what exactly that emf means, how it's going to be filled in. will it be an emf in the strict version of mr schäuble, the former german finance minister, or will it be the more lax interpretation of the european commission? if i look at the origins of that idea of the emf, you end up with a 2010 opinion piece in the economist written by daniel gross and thomas meyer. the european commission then started working on this idea of an emf. what's interesting is that gross and meyer wrote an update of their paper six months ago, in december 2017, assessing and analyzing the european commission's proposals. and what is their conclusion? they wanted a european monetary fund that would ensure more budgetary discipline and stronger debt management. i look at the commission's proposals and it seems that it is more inclined to encourage yet another solidarity mechanism, when we know that transfers encourage irresponsible behaviour rather than greater responsibility. gross and meyer also saw the emf as a non-political institution that could judge in a neutral and economic way. they also want to talk about an exit scenario from the euro, that it is not taboo. both proposals are not supported by the european commission. in short: the commission is threatening to encourage irresponsibility. and then suddenly she is surprised that the northern member states do not want to play along, that the northern member states want to keep the reins in their hands and want an intergovernmental solution. it's clear what needs to be done. it is time for the commission to change its mind.
for the greens/efa group mr president, it will soon be 150 years since germany was united and 30 years since it was reunified. this political union was preceded by a customs and monetary union and since then germany has not been economically homogeneous. but no one doubts its durability. so what holds germany together? on the political level, of course, a common language and institutions; in economic terms, it is a single market governed by broadly common tax and social rules. this federal taxation feeds a federal budget of more than 10% of gdp which, together with a federal social security, constitute extremely robust mechanisms of economic solidarity. in addition, there is a system of direct budgetary transfers between the regions, so that today three regions contribute more to the federation than they receive, and the other thirteen do the opposite. i take germany as an example because it is both the member state which benefits most from economic and monetary union and one of those where resistance to any change in the structure of the euro is strongest. the history of germany shows us above all how the economic and monetary union should evolve if it is to avoid disintegration. we have built a single market and then a single currency backed by a federal central bank, but in this market and in relation to the rest of the world, member states are engaged in a tax, social and commercial competition that is barely regulated and the only mechanism of economic redistribution at our disposal is the very modest budget of the european union, which represents 1% of gdp. is it any wonder that this whole thing is producing divergence rather than convergence? to believe that the euro has a future in this context is to deny reality. if we want to give it a future, the solutions are known: banking union, including common deposit guarantee; a much more robust common budget with its own revenues, typically a fraction of the corporate tax or the price of co2; finally, in the absence of social and fiscal unification, we must at least abandon competition in favour of cooperation. otherwise, a return to national currencies will be inevitable. some, including those in this room, want to do so, either because they think that the political will to achieve the necessary reforms will never exist, or because they think that the nation-state is the only space in which democratic sovereignty can be exercised. it's not our choice. we are convinced that the end of the single currency would lead the european union down the spiral of decomposition, a decomposition which would leave the member states as pawns in the face of the multinationals, in a situation where the trumps, the putins or the xi of this world would take pleasure in turning against each other to our common detriment. we europeans will soon be five per cent of the world's population. whether we like it or not, our unity is the condition for our sovereignty, political and fiscal sovereignty, social and economic sovereignty. so, i ask you: is donald trump going to be the catalyst for this awareness? we hope, but we don't see the signs yet. on the contrary, we still see too many member states presenting the issue in a moral light: the virtuous states of the north should not subsidize the fishermen states of the south. i would remind them that germany, where this moralizing discourse is ubiquitous, mr president, is also the home of deutsche bank, one of the most corrupt banks in the world, and of an automobile industry which has knowingly deceived the public for decades. no one, no country, has a monopoly on virtue or vice! the survival of the euro is a matter of common interest, and let us hope that those who will have to decide will realise this in time.
mr president, 16 years ago the euro was introduced in 12 countries of the european union. the french franc, the mark, the peseta, the guilder. one currency, one monetary policy, one exchange rate policy. it was and it is a historic project. but it was and is also a project that is not yet finished. monetary union can only work in conjunction with a budgetary and a political union. the economic policies of the member states must be coordinated. everyone must control their public debt. we knew that, and yet we looked the other way for years, until the financial crisis of 2008. the construction of the eurozone, which was and is still in fact not fully completed, is a very important part of the process. and the sun is shining, ladies and gentlemen. that's why today we must repair our house. the european union has a knack for carrying out repairs only in bad weather. but anyone with a shred of common sense knows that we must act now. because now the sun is shining. there is economic growth, unemployment is falling and investment is rising again. but apparently good weather isn't enough to fix the roof. we should really take the ladder, climb up on the roof and get to work. that's why we're calling you, colleagues. the economic conditions are there, but we must show political courage. let's take advantage of the good weather, because sooner or later the storm will come again. we must all go home and explain why it is so important to complete the banking union, why the eurozone is necessary and why the budgetary rules are not a piece of paper but are crucial to the success of this project.
thank you, mr president. we are all concerned about the increasing threat to human rights, the rule of law and democratic values. and the eu is right to promote the universality of human rights and fundamental freedoms in its external policy. but we have a problem, commissioner. we have a problem with our credibility and our clout if we don't apply internally what we propagate externally. i have witnessed firsthand how in catalonia police violence was used in a brutal and excessive manner against peaceful voters. politicians and civil society leaders are being held far from home, some for seven months, as political prisoners. the eu is a safe haven for those persecuted on the other side of the world, and that is a good thing. but for catalan and european citizens, whose democratic rights are being repressed, the eu has little solidarity. and that undermines our credibility. and it's one of the reasons why trust in this european story is under serious pressure.
nato and the european union remain the two cornerstones of the european security architecture. we fully support closer cooperation between the eu and nato. by coordinating our defence efforts more closely at international level, we are developing a more efficient and effective defence. the current hybrid threats, cyberattacks, terrorism and so on, are just some of the challenges that are calling for this. my delegation has also won in favour of the building of a european defence community. specific partnerships between certain eu member states are a first step. belgium and the netherlands, for example, cooperate closely in joint purchases and maintenance of frigates and minehunters, or in air surveillance missions. the permanent structured cooperation in the field of defence (pesco) should play a central role in this. but we are happy to leave the further interpretation to the member states, who have exclusive competence in this area.
it was like the procession of echternach, but after two years of resistance, ukraine finally gets an anti-corruption court. this is the necessary final step in the fight against the endemic scourge of corruption and a necessary condition for new european macrofinancial assistance, which we therefore fully support. the ukrainian public and civil society support the fight against corruption. the ukrainian people made very heavy sacrifices for this during the euromaidan revolution. that is why i am continually surprised that kiev, with its current leaders at the helm, so often presses the accelerator and the handbrake at the same time. in this way, reforms all too often fail, are watered down and have so far failed to convince the fight against corruption. the creation of the anti-corruption court, with independent judges, is a step in the right direction. we must monitor the concrete implementation as well as the entire reform agenda. it will also have to lead, for example, to a more modern and democratic electoral law and an operational rule of law.
the european parliament has already expressed its views on the commission's proposal for a regulation on the european union's external relations. any tool that protects women a little more from violence is of great importance. i would stress that we are dealing here with the most heinous crimes against women, such as genital mutilation, forced abortion and forced sterilization, child marriages, rape and honour killings. people can and should have religious beliefs, but this should never stand in the way of protecting women. i cannot understand and accept that culture, custom, religion, tradition or so-called 'honour' can be an excuse to oppress and abuse women. i ask you all: what's honorable about violence against women? and how can fighting violence be inconsistent with traditional values? i am deeply sorry that it is proving so difficult to send a strong signal against violence in the eu. violence against women, and by extension against all genders, has no place in our european society. we are so happy to beat ourselves up for our european values and fundamental rights, but we have a hard time condemning the most flagrant form of discrimination. i think this is unworthy of the european union.

for the greens/efa group mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the european union is first and foremost a union of values, a community of values based on the equal dignity of every human person. to protect these values as best we can, we have chosen a political regime, democracy. a democracy now being challenged at the very heart of the union. because democracy is not just about universal suffrage. the electoral system must also allow for a fair representation of society in all its diversity. the citizens must also be able to obtain information freely from an independent press. but the wall of separation of powers must still stand against all abuses. it is precisely on this front that the polish government that emerged from the 2015 elections openly challenges our common values. he has taken political control of the judiciary and is now attacking the supreme court, the last remaining independent court. we welcome the action taken by the commission, which did not hesitate to initiate the rule of law procedure against poland. but we must admit that the results to date are meagre and that the bulk of the contested measures remain in place. the polish government also knows that it can count on its allies in the council, in particular mr orbán's hungary. and, madam panayotova, bulgaria's absolute timidity raises the question: is this an embarrassment or a tacit complicity? still, time is of the essence. if nothing is done, on 3 july a large number of supreme court judges will be dismissed, paving the way for the government to appoint a majority of judges by hand. we have written to you together with our polish friends, mr timmermans, asking you to use all the means at your disposal to prevent this from happening. on behalf of all poles who share our values, on behalf of all democrats, i repeat this appeal to you today: urgently refer the matter to the court of justice of the european union. beyond the polish case, the essence of the judiciary is under threat in several eu states. it is not only in poland that attempts are made to subordinate him to the executive, and a subordinate judiciary opens the way to all abuses of power. in other countries, budget cuts, whether brutal or deliberate, paralyse the judiciary in its primary mission of delivering justice. is there still any question of justice when years pass between a crime or a misdemeanour and its punishment? is there still any question of justice when citizens no longer turn to the courts for financial reasons? finally, mr timmermans, in the name of the fight against terrorism, it has become common practice to exclude the judiciary from the game. while no bombing has ever been committed because a judge failed to issue a warrant in time, here again the door is open to all abuses. i would ask you, mr president, to exercise your role as guardian of the treaties and of our fundamental values without weakness or favouritism. no government - and i insist on this - is a priori suspect - i conclude, mr president - but no government can be a priori spared your surveillance. what is at stake here is a fundamental element of our democracies and, as you yourself have said, democracy is never an irreversible acquis.
for the greens/efa group mr president, ladies and gentlemen, nothing, nothing can justify a country closing its ports to a ship laden with people in distress. mr salvini has used human lives to engage in political blackmail and, incidentally, electoral manipulation. this is beyond description. however, we understand and share the anger of the citizens of italy, malta and greece, these countries on the front line of the reception of migrants, who are too often left to their own devices by the other member states. from salvini's italy to kurz's austria, from de wever's belgium to macron's france, orbán's hungary and kaczynski's poland, fortress europe seems to have become the alpha and omega for a majority of european leaders. under the guise of firmness and humanity, they are trying to suppress and contain at a distance, even if they have to pay dictators or armed gangs to turn their country into an open-air prison. but let us face the reality, the challenge of migration - as you said, commissioner - will not disappear. between wars and persecution, hunger, climate change and economic exploitation, the causes that drive human beings on the path to exile remain burning. if the heads of state and government - who often boast of being leaders - are stumbling upon responses that are costly, inhumane and doomed to failure, the european parliament has taken a different direction, and like you, mr president, i am proud to belong to an assembly that has managed to find a large majority to put an end to the dublin system and replace it with a collective, responsible and solidary approach. but the politicians sitting here are from the same countries and belong to the same parties as the heads of state and government and their ministers, so tell me why what is possible for us here is impossible for them behind the closed doors of the council. if mr salvini is really seeking the solidarity of europeans, he knows what he has to do: support parliament's position in the council. one thing is certain: by dehumanizing migrants to justify fortress europe, we are trampling on our core values. nobody, nobody is asking the european union to take in the 65 million people who are displaced within or outside their own countries! but europe is far from taking its fair share of the responsibility for welcoming refugees. we can, we must do more, and better. it is a question of justice, solidarity and responsibility. it is more a question of ethics and humanity.
in writing. the aquarius' odyssey is more than a wake-up call. this case shows us the importance of clear european agreements. let this be the ultimate argument to finally force the european breakthrough, which will allow the council of ministers to unblock the reform of asylum policy, and sign an agreement for a rapid, uniform processing of asylum applications and an agreement on the further development of an effective european border and coast guard, with 10 000 border guards (7 times more than today). not a policy of pushbacks, but a policy that prevents people from getting into old boats, and effectively, but correctly, returns expelled migrants. without holding them back or pushing them back onto old boats. an agreement that allows migrants to apply to hotspots in africa, but provides protection for war refugees, and especially the most vulnerable among them. i sincerely hope that the aquarius will also provide the impetus for the launch of a far-reaching marshall plan for africa. we have a strong interest in investing in the region, primarily in renewable energy and sustainable agriculture. the existing trust fund for africa urgently needs to be expanded into a comprehensive investment pact, to generate opportunities and prospects in the region.
in writing. mr president, i would like to say that what we have seen in the last few days is shameful, unseen and unworthy, but reality commands me to say that there is really nothing new under the sun. since the entry into force of the dublin regulation, we have seen that migrants are the plaything of european governments and administrations. people are not registered and not picked up, so they quietly disappear under the radar. when migrants apply for asylum, the uncertainty of the dublin transfer hangs over their heads. and now the ports are closing to vulnerable people. dublin is creating a zero-sum game, and the democrats are the losers. because make no mistake, salvini does not profit from the fact that italy has to take in 600 migrants. the advantage is that he can score on the hood of vulnerable people. that is why i am now addressing every democrat in the council: you must act now. the game must end. reform dublin now, because with every ounce of dignity we take from vulnerable people, we take a little bit of our own dignity. you must act now because you are responsible for what the eu will become in the next elections.
member of the commission. – mr president, i’m really happy to be here today for this debate on the proportionality test directive ahead of tomorrow’s final vote. with this proposal, we have jointly delivered on the first part of our services package of january 2017. i really welcome your cooperation and your support. what do we want to achieve with this directive? you know that negotiations were thorough, they were intense, but we reached an agreement in just over a year, so relatively shortly. let me briefly recall the goal of the directive. we want to improve the quality of the future regulation of professional services. as your rapporteur said, they are of utmost importance for our economies. business services, including professional services such as accountants, lawyers, engineers and architects account for almost 13% of eu value added. regulated professions all together make up nearly 22% of the european labour force, so almost 47 million people. and research shows that services inputs, including professional services, account for about 40% of the value added in manufacturing industries. so it’s extremely important for our economies. we also note that poorly-designed regulation creates obstacles, especially for young graduates trying to access these professions and for professionals when carrying out their professions. so we need to ensure that across the eu, the new regulation is really proportionate in order to protect public interests and that it does not create needless obstacles. this directive will help member states with devising their regulations. it will mean that we can minimise any negative effects on the single market, on competitiveness or on the mobility of professionals. it does not prevent member states from adopting new requirements, but it does prevent unjustified ones. we are not losing sight of existing requirements either. we adopted reform recommendations on the existing regulation of professional services as part of the services package. you discussed these recommendations recently – i’m really grateful for that, and i want to thank mr danti for a report that really encourages us to pursue this important work together with the member states, and we are doing exactly that. the proportionality test directive complements this by helping member state authorities to fulfil their obligations under existing legislation, and of course, court judgments. it sets out very clear rules on how to determine whether planned legislation is really fit for purpose and makes sure that this is determined in advance of legislation being adopted. we have insisted many times that the commission should make enforcement of existing rules a priority, and we are doing exactly that. but we have to recognise that ex-post enforcement actions are definitely not enough. the new directive will help us provide clarity on the general obligation of the principle of proportionality. as i said, i’m really grateful for the way that we have worked together on this proposal. the compromise, i think, strikes the right balance. the outcome of the negotiations maintains the overall objective of the proposed directive. it maintains legal certainty and coherence, while at the same time making sure that important public interests such as patient safety – which was raised many times – and our human health are really clearly acknowledged. there were many moments where some doubted that this directive should cover all of the professions, but clearly, the principle of proportionality applies to all professions, so i am grateful that this house – you, honourable members – has managed to find a good compromise. i also welcome the enhanced transparency and additional focus on the principle of non-discrimination. transparency has to be ensured before and after a new regulation is adopted in the member states. relevant stakeholders shall now be involved before member states adopt the new regulation on professional services. the reason why the regulation was found to be necessary will be available to all after its adoption. you also have drawn attention to the obstacles that may hit professionals when they want to provide temporary or occasional services, so i welcome the addition that proportionality of requirements related specifically to this temporary or occasional provision of services must be assessed. the qualifications directive already says that professionals must be able to provide their services in other member states without unnecessary administrative hurdles. but of course, too often member states demand plenty of information from professionals instead of just using direct cooperation with other member states via our well-established internal market information tool, imi. let me be clear that this does not affect the posting of workers or the application of local working conditions. i think that the adoption of this directive will not be the end of our work in this field; rather, it is the beginning. we will look closely into the implementation of this directive and will assist member states. we will place special emphasis on the transparency that you introduced for the regulation and its rationale via our regulated professions database. this database has really become a reference tool for professional regulations in the eu. i would especially like to thank the rapporteur, the shadow rapporteurs, the competent ministers and, of course, the presidency for your cooperation.
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe group. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. over 5500 professions are currently regulated in the eu. and there are still significant problems with the mutual recognition of professional qualifications between member states. we still receive a great many complaints, for example, when one member state imposes absurd conditions on the exercise of one's profession in another member state. this can't be. that is why this proportionality test, as it is now being enacted here in this act, is an important step. it will help to simplify the recognition of professions across borders. this is particularly important for people who live on a border, as i represent them. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. another component of the so-called service package was the so-called electronic service card, and i shall come back to it very shortly. i have made it quite clear in committee that i am against it and i have even contributed to its rejection by my voting behaviour. why was that important? this service card would have brought up all the problems that we were trying to combat with the posting of workers directive. it would have made effective controls more difficult and would have opened a new door to social dumping. we must fight social dumping more effectively than we simplify it. of course, it remains important for companies to be able to offer their services across borders. that is what our internal market is all about. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks arising from exposure to carcinogens at work.
rules on regulated professions should be proportionate but also targeted. if they're not, this test will filter them out. it is therefore a smart regulation tool with which we are giving a strong push to the development of the european services market. i read in this legislation above all that the safety of the patient is paramount at all times and also that my own region, flanders, is granted a great deal of freedom in the organisation of its health professions. i therefore believe that compulsory dutch language tests for doctors starting work in flanders are in line with the principles so clearly set out in this new directive: quality, accessible and efficient health care.

mr president, the partnership agreement between the european union and the african, caribbean and pacific group is extremely important, and today we must reposition ourselves in the framework of a european, african, caribbean and pacific group agreement. it is true that today, if you ask european citizens about cotonou, the post-cotonou, i think there will be few citizens who know exactly what we are talking about. the same applies to citizens of africa or the caribbean or the pacific who, if questioned, will not know exactly what we are talking about. the first challenge we must therefore face, as europeans and with our partners, is to ensure that everyone knows what is in these agreements. i join my predecessors who said that civil society should be involved in these agreements, in the discussions about these agreements, that there should be a process of ownership by civil society for the negotiation of these future agreements. it seems to me important that the people of europe and the people of africa, the caribbean and the pacific should be properly informed of what we are doing. secondly, the work we have to do at parliamentary level is also important. i would like to say that i am very pleased that the commission has taken the initiative to present this report to the european parliament, and that it is a very good initiative. environmental challenges, demographic and population challenges, resource use challenges, transparency and governance challenges are challenges we share with these different countries and with the countries of the european union. i am a little saddened today to see that when we address these countries, we do so only with the concern of migration, when this same migration is in fact a wealth that we must share with these countries. so i think that the future of cotonou is also to be able to project itself into a common future with all these countries and i hope that we will all succeed in this challenge together.
in 2016, i worked as a shadow rapporteur on the resolution on combating illegal wildlife trade. i was then advocating a european ban on ivory trade, without any back doors. because illegal ivory always finds its way into the legal circuit. i repeat my plea today. ivory trade is deadly to biodiversity and threatens to turn elephants and rhinos into 'living dinosaurs'. but the ivory trade has other disastrous and often deadly consequences. trade annually puts billions of euros in the hands of criminals, militias and terrorist organisations, creating insecurity and instability. moreover, trade in ivory is growing spectacularly and we, as the european union, are involved as a market and as a hub. a strong fight against these criminal activities and the closure of our ivory market are necessary to ensure the protection of the species and our safety. the european union can and must make a difference.
today in moscow, the world cup begins with the opening match between the russian and saudi teams. neither is a footballing giant, and neither is a democracy in the economist's democracy index. in the list of host cities for the world cup , i see some names that sound like: moscow, st. petersburg, sochi, but not labytnangi. i'm not sure where they are. and that's not surprising, because that's a small town just above the arctic circle. there, filmmaker oleg sentsov is fighting a lonely battle from his cell, a hunger strike that has now lasted a month. with this last remaining means, he is calling for attention to be paid to all ukrainian political prisoners in the country. he himself was convicted for his criticism of the illegal annexation of crimea, an excessive propaganda stunt by putin. colleagues, in a few weeks, the football circus will leave russia and the country will return to darkness. i call on all european leaders to condemn strongly during this world cup the imprisonment of oleg sentsov and all other ukrainian political prisoners. today i am already a supporter of oleg sentsov!

the report was drawn up by the rapporteur for an opinion for imco. mr president, i was rapporteur for an opinion in imco and i wanted to emphasize the importance of a defence industry for the internal market. we see too often that each member state is working on its own projects, whereas it would be much more efficient to work with other companies to produce new products from other member states. we see the successes in the research programmes, such as horizon 2020, the future horizon europe, where cooperation is at the heart, and that should also be possible in defence. i'll make sure the budget isn't cut from the civil investigation. this should continue to be the rule in the new programmes beyond 2020. i am pleased to see that the door is being opened to cooperation with companies from countries outside the european union, because we must also involve our foreign partners in these programmes when it comes to the strategic importance.
president, measuring is knowing. that is certainly true in agriculture. if we are to guide the common agricultural policy, we need accurate data. how else can we know exactly what is happening in our rural areas and what the impact of climate change, among other things, is, as well as the changing social climate in rural areas? through years of meticulous data collection, we can begin to better identify trends, but also to predict trends. one of the clear trends is, of course, the rapid decline in the number of farmers, which is a real concern to me. the new cap must take measures to provide a supportive solution to this. colleagues, although data collection in the agricultural sector has been going on since 1966, i do not need to tell you that in 2018 this data is needed even more than ever. the modernisation of the data collection system provided for in this legislation is therefore, in my view, a much-needed step. in conclusion, i would like to stress one more thing. in measuring various rural indicators, it has recently been noted that rural communities' internet connections are not always optimal. however, the presence of high-quality and fast internet is absolutely necessary, not only to allow people to have a social digital life, but also to be able to do, for example, precision agriculture. i hope to see real improvements here in the near future.

the mediterranean, which is 0.07% of entire migration flows worldwide: 68 million in all. so what are we talking about? in my opinion we are not talking about a sudden influx of people crossing the mediterranean. we are talking about a political decision, an opportunistic decision of one minister of the interior in italy, mr salvini, who created this problem that we now have on our plate. that is happening, and mr salvini has a very simple opinion: he says, ‘not in italy, put them in germany, put them in austria put them in hungary maybe’. and mr orbán, who is a friend of mr salvini’s, or eventually to hungary and maybe to your country, austria’ and you are saying – and have already – you don’t want them in austria. you want them to stay in italy or in germany or to send them to hungary. you see what the problem is, ms chancellor. the problem in europe is that the only consensus that the council and the prime ministers and you and your friends can agree today is: ‘not in my back yard’. that is the only consensus that you are capable of for the moment, and that is the scandal of europe. don’t talk about migrants. we are talking about a political crisis on the back of migrants, and what i am asking you is not only to be tough on migration or illegal migration. you have to be tough on illegal migration but when an asylum seeker is coming from syria when he enters the european union, he is illegal before he asks for asylum. what i am asking you is to play the european card, as austria has always done. and the european card is a real european border and coast guard. back the proposals of the commission before the end of the year with the council. secondly, make an agreement on dublin. the parliaments have a position. the council has no position for the moment. thirdly, create european reception centres where people can ask for asylum inside or outside europe, i don’t care, what is important is that they are not falling into the hands of the smugglers and the criminal gangs. finally, a legal system of economic migration, which we absolutely need. in conclusion, chancellor, you can count on the support of all this parliament. not for big speeches against migration but for european solutions on migration.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, the chancellor has said that the austrian presidency wants a europe that protects. we couldn’t agree more, mr chancellor. in an uncertain world, the first thing that most of our citizens want is protection. let’s take a look at what they say when we ask them and i have here the report commissioned by parliament seeking the opinion of all people in europe. so, what do they say? the first thing they want to be protected from is terrorist attacks. secondly, they want their jobs to be protected. thirdly, they want to be protected from poverty and social exclusion. even though the survey was conducted in the immediate aftermath of the biggest wave of arrivals in europe, being protected from uncontrolled migration only comes fourth, right before being protected from climate change. when it comes to prioritising european action, all citizens say first work on tackling poverty and social exclusion and then work on fighting terrorism. i am saying that because your presidency has decided to conflate security with migration and make this its top priority, as if every migrant or asylum seeker were a potential criminal or terrorist, which would then justify keeping him or her away from the eu’s territory. and you do that, when in actual terms, as guy verhofstadt just said, the arrivals on our shores have dramatically dropped. make no mistake about it though, taking into account the dire situation of so many people in the areas surrounding europe, the asylum and migration challenge will not fade away anytime soon. it is one that does not lend itself to simplistic solutions, but one that europe can meet while fully respecting its core values. so why is it, mr chancellor, that in chorus with too many other european heads of state and government, you choose to focus public attention on an issue that does not rank highest in our citizens’ minds and whose size makes it manageable, if only member states would show solidarity with one another? the simpler explanation, mr chancellor, is that you are seeking a diversion by riding on fears that you and your friends are fanning. you probably want to draw the attention of the people away from what you want to do at home – extending the working day to 12 hours, reducing social benefits to people based on nationality, or contradicting the banter of your coalition partner by ratifying the comprehensive economic and trade agreement (ceta), which is a very multinational-friendly free trade deal. so the picture becomes clear. you want a europe that protects, yes, but that protects those who wield economic power, not the citizens. this is evidenced by the report that we have launched which highlights how your country still allows wealthy austrian citizens to park their money away in liechtenstein, a country that sells passports to dubious individuals or shows little enthusiasm in fighting money laundering. after the financial crisis, mr chancellor, anger and anxiety grew in our society. we could, and we should, respond by changing our economic and social policies in order for them to serve the people. instead, you and your friends choose to double down on neo—liberal policies and diverted the anger and anxiety towards very convenient scapegoats – the migrants. so i’m asking you, mr kurz, and also you, mr weber, is this the new normal at the european people’s party? over the last few months we have seen your member parties increasingly adopting the ideas and rhetoric of the far-right, such as in austria, in bavaria or in france. going together to the polls, like in italy, governing with it, such as in austria and, to crown it all, your member in hungary is hailed by the entire far—right international as a hero. you claim to be doing all this to prevent the expansion of far—right parties. the reality is that by legitimising their ideas you accelerate their diffusion in society and worse, you are making them official policy. is this still the party of the fathers of europe, who decided to launch the european project to cure our continent forever of the plague of nationalism and hatred? i have never been – and these are my last words, mr president – i have never been scared of the extreme right, but when the ideas of the extreme right contaminate the parties that were at the heart of european democracy, we are all in danger.

i believe that this experience is precisely the europe we do not want. we need a europe of cooperation, we need a europe where we can discuss on equal terms, even if there are different nationalities and factions, and we need a europe which we want to move forward together. we, as the austrian presidency, would like to make our contribution here, if we can. i hope to have a good cooperation with you as the european parliament. mr president, what is the subject of this dossier? it is essentially a question of two things. it is about road safety and it is about tackling social dumping. these two things go hand in hand, of course. it is a good thing that we are introducing mandatory and accelerated introduction of smart tachographs in international transport and that we are doing the same for delivery vans to close the back doors. on the other hand, it is our duty to put an end - as mr christensen has just said - to the social ills in the transport sector. truck drivers also have rights and deserve our full respect for the work they do. they have the right to spend a weekend at home or in a decent hotel instead of in some cheap parking lot. today, this is already the case in belgium, germany and other member states. we really don't want that arrangement to be undermined. a six-by-six-foot cabin is not the ideal place to spend a weekend. these measures are essential from a social point of view and also from the point of view of road safety.
madam president, since 1976, the democratic republic of congo (drc) has experienced several outbreaks of ebola virus, which were more or less managed with peaks of infection in the 1970s, but much less so today. a total of 1,000 people died in the various waves of contamination over 40 years. on 17 may, after the case was identified in mbandaka, a city of one million people, the alert was raised again, and the world health organization (who) responded directly by launching an experimental vaccination campaign on high-risk populations, as well as information and isolation campaigns. today, who and the government in the drc acknowledge that the outbreak is contained. on that basis, i have asked that we extend our debate this evening to the general situation in the democratic republic of the congo. if the ebola virus has killed a thousand congolese since 1976, the war in that country since 1994 has killed more than six million congolese, displaced thousands of them who still live in camps, mutilated and raped young girls. while the drc is able to stop the ebola virus, it clearly lacks the will and ability to protect its people. that is why i have asked for a broader discussion on the situation in this country on the eve of an electoral process and where everything suggests that the mess is organized by the government. i refer to the speech by mr mugwebe, the sakharov prize winner of this assembly, who said that the elections would not be credible. he also said that the congolese people live in modern slavery, a government of blood and guts that has no plan or vision. living, working, eating to the full, being free are rights in a country as rich as the drc. yet, massacres, rapes, and massive habitat destruction are the daily lot of this population. all the institutions in the drc are now illegitimate, whether it is the provincial assembly, the national assembly, the senate, the presidency, and this does not seem to move anyone - even here in this assembly - because they will have to organize the elections so-called independently. so, yes, we are concerned about ebola, but i would say that the general situation in the congo should be of greater concern.

for the greens/efa group mr president, eight years: it has been eight years since greece was placed under financial assistance by the european union. officially, the country is now out of the aid package, growth is back, tourism is back, the trade balance is in balance, everything is fine, except that for us, these eight years seem like an outrageous waste. the greek socialist and conservative parties have for decades allowed public deficits to run, in particular by neglecting to collect taxes from their privileged clientele. nor did they try to modernise the country's economy. their responsibility for the disaster is overwhelming. but they were not alone, because if greece was able to borrow beyond reason, it was because banks, and not only greek banks, far from it, lent to greece beyond reason. and it is the people of the country who ultimately pay the price for this double irresponsibility. from the outset, the successive assistance plans had the sole objective of protecting the financial interests of the creditors, i.e. the banks. once again, european taxpayers have come to their rescue. worse, the remedy inflicted on the country is economic nonsense. greece is expected to have a substantial primary surplus in the coming decades, which even the international monetary fund considers to be totally impossible. this denial of reality by the european creditors has very direct consequences. the greek people are suffering, they are dying. the suicide rate has increased by 35% in the country since 2008, 20% of greeks are in extreme poverty and more than 400,000 greeks have left the country for lack of prospects. the desperation of the population is such that even the neo-nazi far right has returned to parliament. meanwhile, the country's endemic ills of underinvestment, patronage and tax laxity towards the richest remain. so what should we have done? first, to make the creditors share the responsibility by cancelling, as the imf wanted, a substantial part of their claims. secondly, mr moscovici, we must make european aid conditional from the outset, not after eight years, on a fair contribution from the richest and on the establishment of a tax administration worthy of the name. the european union should have put its investment tools at the service of the country's energy transition in such a way as to enable greece to be a net exporter of electricity from renewable sources. it could also have borne the costs of hosting refugees, of whom we know that greece is taking in more than its fair share. if nothing can undo the enormous human damage suffered by greece, it is not too late for the european union to change course and put justice and economic common sense back at the centre of the relationship that links it to the cradle of democracy.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0173/97). that fuse has now been removed thanks to tough, difficult reforms by greece. the european union is also committed to the development of a common foreign and security policy. that's a good thing. the fuse is out, but the powder keg is still there. that'll have to be the next step. the fuse is out, but now we have to get the powder out of that gunpowder barrel, so that the eurozone becomes more stable. because let's not get this wrong: greece, that was a manageable crisis. if a larger member state were to get into trouble, we would see things differently. that is why some say there should be a eurozone budget: to absorb shocks. we know where that leads: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. that's not the answer. let's go with the other solution. let us strengthen accountability, reintroduce and enforce the no-bailout clause, opt for clear budgetary rules that we finally make enforceable. - please, please.
the greek economy is growing, competitiveness has returned and exports are increasing. the ship avoids the collision course and actually has a little wind in its sails. the signals are positive, but we're not there yet. the greeks have weathered a huge storm. europe must continue to guide them to ensure that the growth rates it is now achieving are sustained. economic growth, ladies and gentlemen, and i want to stress this very seriously, must also be inclusive growth. the greeks have had to make huge cuts, including in their pensions and social security. although there were few other options from a macroeconomic point of view, we really have to admit that this has been a very difficult period for the average greek. the worst thing is that it is mainly the young people, the generation that has never been able to reap the benefits, who will have to bear the heaviest burdens, also in the future. the positive thing is that the greek government now seems to have its budget in order, including a primary surplus. this should allow the debt to evolve to a viable level in the long term. the control of the greek debt is, in my view, of paramount importance. debt restructuring should not be taboo. the debate must be held on this, even if it can never be a full pardon. but it is equally important that in the long term all the promises made are actually fulfilled. whoever is in charge in athens next year, the european finance ministers must continue to monitor the situation. the structural reforms agreed on must be implemented. i am thinking specifically of the energy sector, reforms of the administration and the fight against tax evasion. the situation in greece is so special that enhanced surveillance is the best option at the moment. i would also like to stress one last point. it is indeed possible for a country to emerge from a deep financial, economic crisis without massive devaluations of its currency. the greek example is a great blow to anyone who claims otherwise.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat fraud. madam president, commissioner, i would like to start by congratulating our colleague kinga gál on the outstanding work we have had the opportunity to do with her. the proposal we are discussing today is important because it concerns potentially 1.4 billion citizens from some 60 countries who are exempt from the visa requirement, and it actually concerns 30 to 40 million people who enter the territory of the european union every year. the proposed system is simple: any third country national who is exempt from the visa requirement will have to obtain prior authorisation before entering the union's borders. firstly, is such a measure necessary? we think it is. of the 30 to 40 million people who wish to enter the union each year, some may pose a real risk to internal security, a migratory risk or a risk to public health through the spread of epidemics. etias is therefore a plus for internal security, for border control, because it allows potential risks to be identified in time. it is also a plus for the travellers themselves. do we know that there are currently nearly 300 000 people who are turned back at the union's borders every year, even though they have already paid for their journey? etias will significantly reduce this number. secondly, is this measure bureaucratically untenable? obviously not. travellers can simply apply online by filling in a standard form and 95% of applicants will receive a positive response within minutes. thirdly, is this measure excessively costly for travellers? that is debatable, but 7 euros per person for a permit that will be valid for 3 years does not seem excessive to us. for comparison, the price of a visa is 60 euros. and finally - and the commissioner has stressed this - this measure respects fundamental rights. personal data are kept only for the duration of the authorisation. no sensitive questions are allowed. an ethics committee is set up to verify that risk indicators are necessary and proportionate and a right of appeal against a refusal decision is introduced. for all these reasons, my group will vote strongly in favour of the etias project.
on behalf of the group of the european right mr president, i am glad that tomorrow we are voting on the ethias dossier, after months of hard work. it was identified in a study, together with the entry/exit system, as an important missing link in european border control. this system will hopefully screen foreigners who do not need a visa to come to europe in advance from 2021. an automated search of european and international databases should provide the security services with a complete picture. the schengen information system will no longer allow anyone to travel to the schengen countries unnoticed. but the system is more than just consulting databases. it also tries to predict risk with clever algorithms and statistics. good news for the taxpayer: the system is self-sufficient, as applicants pay eur 7 for their electronic application. in most cases, applicants receive a decision within ten minutes. the system is fully automated and only checks the application manually in the event of possible identity confusion or a hit. a permit is valid for three years. the system should support border guards who have to make the decision on the spot at the border whether to let someone in or not. ethias allows them to see if an admission has been delivered and if additional checks are needed. the n-va is very pleased that police services and immigration authorities will also be able to consult the system within their mandate. we are closing a major gap in the protection of our external borders. europe is getting a little safer.
on behalf of the alde group. mr president, commissioner, i would like to begin by addressing mr viotti. very good work, mr viotti, your original text was an excellent one and you were clever enough to incorporate the best of the amendments from the political groups, including mine, which makes it even better. you know, i am well aware, the priorities of my group, but i want to remind you of them. first, we want, like you, more growth for more jobs, which means in concrete terms stimulating investment, in particular the 2020 programme, stimulating and supporting smes, in particular through the cosme programme, vigorously supporting - the eu-us privacy shield plays an important role in protecting the data of our european citizens. over 3,000 companies signed up to the privacy shield. these companies promised to protect the transfer of data between the eu and the us more strictly than the us law requires. i cannot therefore support the proposal to declare the s.h.i.e.l.d. inadequate and possibly suspend it. without the privacy shield, our european citizens and smes are worse off. they will be the ultimate victims of such a drastic proposal. citizens will have fewer rights of appeal because the ombudsman will disappear. europe will have a harder time checking that data transfers are being carried out correctly. there is therefore a greater risk of misuse of european data. the commission will also be able to provide the necessary information to the member states in order to enable them to assess the impact of the measures. the us implementation of the shield can indeed be improved, but at present our citizens and smes are better off with the protection of the shield than without it.
mr president, most countries only tax within their territory, but the united states also does so on the basis of nationality. in other words, americans living abroad must file an annual u.s. tax return. some eu citizens were given american citizenship without asking for it, because they happened to be born in america. the facta legislation on automatic exchange of banking data with the us has led to them losing access to basic financial services in the eu, which cannot be the intention. every now and then, i get a request for help in my mailbox from these "coincidental americans". a letter was sent to the finance ministry, but unfortunately, no reply was received. that is why i ask the council: can you jointly take new diplomatic or legislative measures to safeguard the interests of eu citizens?
the european parliament has also called for the european union to take action to combat racism and xenophobia, and for the european union to take action to combat racism and xenophobia. there are about 80 million people with disabilities in the eu, but why do you see so few of them in political office? people with disabilities still do not have equal opportunities. in most member states, people with disabilities lose their right to vote when they are declared incapable of acting. fifteen eu member states automatically deny people with disabilities who are in guardianship their right to vote. there are also major obstacles to voting, as polling stations are often simply not accessible. only in 12 of the 28 member states are there accessibility requirements for all polling stations and where there are already accessibility conditions, these tend to focus only on physical accessibility. the information available to disabled people is also often very limited. election programmes, debates and meetings are still barely accessible. in a democratic society, everyone should have the right to make an informed choice. make no mistake, there is a strong legal framework that obliges the eu and the member states to ensure that people with disabilities can participate in political life on an equal basis with any other citizen. unfortunately, the reality is just disappointing. it is high time that this was finally changed.
