in writing. it is clear that the comprehensive assistance programmes of european cohesion policy have borne fruit in northern ireland over the last decade. the european contribution of over eur 1 billion between 2014 and 2020 through various funds has so far provided, inter alia: sustained economic growth with the lowest unemployment rates in ten years; intensive support for the transformation of heavy industry into a more service-oriented economy; sustainable economic activities the eu also plays a key role in the peace talks between ireland and northern ireland. i believe that whatever the outcome of the brexit negotiations, european support for northern ireland must remain secure. northern ireland has been involved in the ambitious european project for a considerable period of time, and intensive cooperation - particularly on peace projects - should therefore be pursued in my view.
i have to say that i am not in favour of this. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. we all remember the year 2015, which was very painful, especially for our greek friends. in the context of the severe financial and economic crisis in greece, the country was no longer able to guarantee and guarantee the necessary co-financing shares for structural fund projects. there simply were no public funds. this was very concrete: many very important infrastructure projects in greece that had already started were at risk of being abandoned, and even worse: if these projects had not been completed within the 2007-2013 funding period, greece would have had to repay significant funds to the eu. so we were faced with an absolute worst-case scenario. the situation threatened to get even worse if these payments had to be made to the eu. it was therefore necessary to act very quickly, very wisely and very purposefully. the eu commission then proposed an amendment to the general regulation on the structural funds: in order to allow the country to use the available funds, the eu did not use the otherwise mandatory national co-financing rate in the previous funding period. this allowed greece to recover investment funds that would otherwise have simply been lost, as described above. in addition, greece was granted an advance of 1 billion for the new period of assistance, which has resulted in a sum of aid of some 2 billion euros, a sum which this parliament has had to monitor closely. as a special rapporteur, i was responsible for this monitoring on behalf of parliament and i went on several occasions to the greek side to speak to the important people there about these projects. we have repeatedly stressed in this context that this aid cannot be a blank cheque. they should be committed to the implementation and completion of these important projects, including in the context of the structural reforms that greece was urgently required to implement. that is what we wanted to ensure with this report. we have therefore carefully checked that the funds, these two billion euros from the eu, are being used and used in a meaningful way. and what is the result? the result is that greece has delivered. the country has drawn up and worked out a priority list of important projects. these were therefore completed in time and without any loss of funds. but not only that: this report also shows the impact that regional policy can have on regions and countries. we have seen that regional policy projects have helped to bring about tangible economic growth in the countryside, to combat high unemployment and to create prospects for people. this was a real success for greece. in times of massive crisis, the structural funds sometimes accounted for more than half of total public investment. the commission notes that the greek authorities have not provided any evidence that the greek authorities have not provided sufficient evidence to support their claim that the measures in question constitute state aid. 131 priority projects were completed during the whole period of this assistance. these are clearly measurable figures. such figures should therefore give cause for reflection to critics of regional policy. regional policy has created a very concrete added value here. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. ladies and gentlemen, the greek people have been helped effectively by these special measures. yes, these relief measures were a sign of solidarity. we in the committee agreed by a large majority that this was and is a promotion of the european idea and regional policy. in future, we must ensure that investments are sustainable, that jobs are safeguarded and that the quality of life is improved. in conclusion, i am convinced that the eu's decision to grant this special aid to greece was the right one. this is a pretty good illustration of what would have happened if we had not done this. but it is also clear that this aid measure is and remains an exceptional case. we must all ensure that this does not happen again, neither to greece nor to any other member state. i would like to thank the european commission and all the members of this house who have worked with me on this report and who have accepted these conclusions. thank you very much.
member of the commission. – mr president, i would like to thank the house for this opportunity to look back. and, looking back at what we decided together three years ago, i think we can see there is overall unanimity on the fact that the revision of the common provisions regulation and the measures specific to greece have achieved their goal. they have allowed greece to address a severe liquidity problem that had occurred at that time, to inject cash and to relaunch investments in the real economy – and all this in a relatively short period of time. as a matter of fact, greece was the first state to reach a 100% absorption rate of available funds under the 2007—2013 programming period and it is also among the best-performing member states in terms of implementation in the 2014—2020 programming period. the good progress so far should be the driver for greece to continue, and increase even further, efforts to put in place a solid pipeline of new mature projects, which could generate expenditure and ensure a good performance in the years to come. the commission has been providing – and will continue to provide – hands—on technical support to the greek authorities in facilitating the management and implementation of the funds. more generally, i think the specific measures in favour of greece embody the very essence of cohesion policy, namely championing solidarity between member states so that regions can grow together, supporting growth enhancing investments to boost the economy. this is exactly what the commission has tried to reinforce in its post—2020 proposal for cohesion policy by securing a policy for all regions and by making sure that resources are efficiently channelled to where they are the most needed. on the other issue that was raised, i can assure you that we are standing by the greek people and the greek authorities – as well as the italians – and we are working together to integrate the migrants. that is another issue and i am at your disposal if you would like to talk about it separately, but it is not the subject of this debate.
i have to say that i am not in favour of this. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. i'll be very brief. i have two minutes, but i'm not gonna use them. i shall not comment on the comments made by the extreme left and the extreme right, because i do not share their views at all. in addition, their comments were not about the special measures or regional policy, so i do not have to comment on that. i'll summarize what's important, though. there was a situation. we have responded with a special measure, and with great success, with great measurable success. i would therefore like to thank all those who have helped to make this happen. we have not been talking in these difficult times for greece, we have not been criticizing for the sake of criticism, we have been acting. it was right and important. and so my thanks go to all those who have contributed to this: thank you very much!
i would like to congratulate the rapporteur on her draft report. it is very detailed and contains references to sign languages, the preferred language of deaf people in europe. our society is digitalising very rapidly and it is important to preserve our linguistic wealth. this is especially true for sign languages, which are threatened by the displacement of some smaller eu sign languages due to size and lack of financial resources. so we need to find ways to allow all deaf people in the eu to participate and benefit from the digital single market in their own sign language. that is why i fully support the report on language equality in the digital age. all languages, whether spoken or sign, must be treated equally.
mr president, deforestation, degradation and illegal exploitation of forests can also be motivated by trade-related factors. the european union's trade policy therefore has a key role to play. the european union is one of the main exporters and importers of goods. it is therefore able to act in accordance with international standards, contrary to what our colleague on the radical right has said. but it is more than a will, it is an obligation. the european court of justice has said in its opinion on singapore that the european union must integrate the objectives of preserving and improving the quality of the environment and sustainable resource management in its trade policy. so what do you do? first, to establish a regulatory framework to apply transparency obligations throughout the supply of forest products at risk; second, to strengthen the sustainable development chapter with sanction mechanisms. so, to fight climate change with voluntary measures is simply irresponsible. that is why i call on the sense of responsibility of each of us in this parliament not to support the separate votes of certain members of this parliament, who would be committing suicide in relation to respect for the climate.

president, mr juncker, i found one thing particularly positive about your state of the union address, namely that we can at least be sure that it will be your last. an ambitious and comprehensive programme, i read everywhere about your admirers, but in fact it is just a shopping list of what remains of the old europe, the old european union. what we urgently need is a new european union, because the present one has a wrong design, a wrong basic plan and a wrong blueprint. if you think progress means that more powers should go to the eu, you are wrong. more and more european citizens want a new european union, in which the member states and the peoples do not disappear but are restored to their dignity. in your ivory tower, you have so beautifully divided the world into cosmopolitan, open-minded, traveling, good people on the one hand and populist, narrow-minded, xenophobic, nationalist, bad people on the other. you fanatical, extremist supporters of diversity, i therefore call upon you: accept the diversity of opinions and visions, put away your shopping list and make way for the new age!
madam president, it is not enough to say that the first pillar of pensions is necessary. the first pillar of pensions must first be strong, i.e. with a replacement rate that does not plunge pensioners into poverty. it must be non-discriminatory, i.e. it must compensate for situations where there are pension gaps, such as for women. it must be modernized, of course, not only in connection with the ageing of the population but also in the light of new forms of contract. today we see employment reforms, zero-rate contributions, which are gifts to businesses, but are deadly for the pension system. and so to say that pensions are not a matter for the european union is wrong, because the european semester system today requires countries to make savings in their social security. and social security is the pension system. today, social security is not being approached in terms of the well-being of individuals, but in terms of the savings we can make. it is a short-term management of social security. so transferring the collective solution of solidarity pensions by distribution to an individual capitalisation system is not the solution. the pepp (pan-european personal pension product) is perhaps a financial savings product, which we probably need to improve. but the pepp is simply a plus for those who already have, but it is nothing at all for those who do not have today, and so our concern must go to those who today are not covered by a fair pension system that allows them to live in dignity.
mr president, a debate on the future of pensions cannot be held without mentioning the concerns of the next generation. i don't think i'm the first to stress this this afternoon: will there be a pension for us, for my generation? aging is a fact. figures show that the population will continue to grow. but the active population will continue to decline at the same time. that's fewer workers to support more people. this means increasing costs for public pensions, which will become increasingly difficult to finance. the president-in-office of the european council, mr van rompuy, has said: "with the evolution from four active workers to two for every one retired in 50 years, it is no longer a question of left or right, but of pure arithmetic". the real solution to controlling pension costs, in my view, lies in three things: first, we need to get the public budgets in the eurozone in order, so that there is room to absorb the growth in pension costs. secondly, we must increase the degree of effectiveness. the more people in work, the more contributions to pensions. thirdly, measures are needed to reform the pension system itself and to keep people in work longer. finally, part of the solution is also for people to invest more in supplementary pensions themselves. we have known for a long time that we cannot survive on public money alone in the long term. the second pillar is the european union's policy on the environment. all too often it's the bottom of society that doesn't save money for later. it is wrong, indeed unjust, that the very parties which are supposed to be defending these people should be put on the brakes for ideological reasons. the private market can provide solutions. but then we must all have the courage to sit down at the table and create a framework for this too. for example, after retirement people have the security of a guaranteed supplementary income, on top of the pension, for the rest of their lives. so let us work together to develop a global vision to tackle these problems. let's show for once that we as politicians can also think long term.
the rapporteur. - mr president, a few days ago a major campaign to clean up the ocean began in the united states. the images went around the world. the ocean cleanup wants to use plastic traps to help clean up the plastic soup in the pacific ocean. i hope that the boyhood dream of the young dutchman boyan slat will be a success. it is noble to clean up the rubbish of the past, but of course we must first of all ensure that we prevent plastic waste from ending up in the sea. so we need to be more sensible about plastic. that is the focus of our debate here today. plastic is an important and valuable material, and it's hard to imagine it being eliminated from our society and our economy. the problem is the way we produce and use plastic today. it's untenable. plastic is designed to last forever, but all too often we only use it once. sometimes it disappears after a few seconds or minutes in the garbage bag, or worse, in the environment. we're very sloppy with the material. we produce almost 26 million tonnes of plastic waste in the european union every year, but only 30% of it is collected for recycling. all this has disastrous consequences for the environment, the climate and the economy. our ecosystems are suffering from pollution. not least our seas and oceans, which are turning into a plastic soup. we're missing out on opportunities to reduce our carbon footprint. we are literally leaking the euros out of our economy. 95% of the value of the material is lost. the recent chinese ban on the import of plastic waste from the european union is also a slap in the face. for years, in europe, we have outsourced our plastic waste problem for convenience. we must therefore take the chinese ban as an opportunity to invest ourselves in recycling capacity in the european union and to change our policy. the momentum is there, not just at the political level, but also among many citizens, where the support for action is growing. the point is to turn this public concern into public responsibility, into shared responsibility. so my report is not a plea against plastic, but a plea for a circular artificial economy, where we deal with plastic in a sustainable and responsible way, so that we can stop its harmful effects and preserve its value in the chain. to succeed, we need to use the strategy as a lever for circular production and consumption models. we have to provide customized solutions, since there are no one-size-fits-all solutions. and we need to work together across the entire value chain. better and more plastic recycling is the start. a few months ago, we passed ambitious new waste legislation here. full and rapid implementation of these measures is essential. but more is needed: the commission shall be empowered to adopt delegated acts in accordance with article 264 concerning the establishment of a european data protection supervisor. the packaging directive is a way of working towards a truly circular product design. companies can make a difference by linking their brand and marketing to circular production and consumption models. the creation of a genuine internal market for recycled plastics is also fundamental. today, the share of recycled plastic is estimated to be only 6%. to do this, we need to strengthen market confidence through quality standards and verification. minimum requirements for recycled content, a lower vat rate and a circular purchasing policy can also further encourage the use of recycled plastics. more and better recycling is necessary, but not sufficient in itself to move towards a true circular economy. i'll keep that in mind. the prevention of plastic waste remains crucial. one way to do this is by taking smart action on single-use plastics. the commission has now put forward a legislative proposal on this point, and we are working on it in the committee on the environment, together with my colleague frédérique ries and others. my report also calls for a deliberate use of bio-plastics. on the basis of clear standards and criteria, biodegradable plastic can play a supporting role, but it is not the panacea for marine litter. bio-based plastics can help reduce our dependence on third countries by partially differentiating the raw materials. but research and development are of great importance here to give innovation a chance. we call for a ban on oxo-plastics and microplastics in cosmetics and other products by 2020. finally, we call for further efforts to promote research and innovation, also in the next multiannual financial framework. the eu must also be a global player and, of course, set a good example in its own institutions. i would like to thank the shadow rapporteurs for their very constructive cooperation. i hope that tomorrow we will say 'yes' by a large majority to a new circular plastic economy that offers opportunities for the environment, climate and economy.
mr president, in the mid-1950s, plastic was a revolution, an invention that changed everything: products, quantities, possibilities, practices and our behaviour as well. but today we have to face the fact that we are drowning in waste and our oceans are dying. for half of them, it's these plates, these cotton buds, these straws, these cups, these bottles -- all these everyday objects that you use once, you throw away, and then it takes 500 years, maybe 1,000 years to decompose. so, as rapporteur for the european parliament on this directive on single-use plastics, i naturally support marc demesmaeker's ambition to promote the dynamics of the transition to a circular economy for plastics. that said, recycling is also, in some ways, encouraging production. and then it can be very complicated, especially when plastics contain problematic chemicals. clearly, the sustainable solution is to reduce the production, the consumption of plastic. every year, 500 000 tonnes of plastic waste are dumped into the seas of the european union, and this is no longer sustainable.
report by mr pompidou, drawn up on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission. president, i would like to thank mrs kadenbach for her report and her hard work. i appreciate that in her report she has also taken account of the itre opinion on antimicrobial resistance. as rapporteur for itre, i have tried to find sustainable solutions. these sustainable solutions, to me, lie in the following elements. it is of immense importance that research and innovation be continuously stimulated. the search for new antibiotics is crucial, but epidemiology and immunology also offer opportunities. this search will be undertaken mainly by pharmaceutical companies and research institutes. so there can be no single economic model, but there must be multiple drivers for the development of new antibiotics. we are talking here in the report about the pull and push incentives. in addition, it is also important that the patient be given a prompt and accurate diagnosis. is it infected with a bacterium or a virus, and which bacterium is it? the technology of that new rapid diagnosis should be available and accessible in all member states. antimicrobial resistance is not just a european problem, it is a global problem. we as the european union must put our backs to it. we must look for possible solutions. we must take our responsibility here by seeking european and international cooperation and thus resolving a world problem together.
mr president, clean, competitive and connected mobility is what we are all about. mobility accounts for a quarter of all emissions in the european union and remains the main cause of air pollution problems in our cities. the health of our citizens suffers and it is for them first of all that we must work towards cleaner mobility. we will also need to show greater ambition to achieve our climate goals. to this end, policy makers need to frame and facilitate modal shift, new technologies and principles such as mobility as a service. in conclusion, may i also stress that we still need a strong and competitive european automobile industry? i am concerned to hear that the market share of our european sector has fallen sharply over the last ten years. the car manufacturers can only secure their position if they are encouraged to innovate. the car was invented in europe! let us make sure that we can continue to pioneer in the future.
as always, mr president, good legislation stands or falls with good implementation. the report clearly shows the need for better implementation, enforcement and monitoring of existing legislation on plant protection products. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. the nva delegation supports this report. we are particularly keen to improve the use of good agricultural practices, such as integrated pest management. we certainly also support the call for greater transparency in the procedures. that can only serve to strengthen trust. the call for research and innovation, including in the field of biological pesticides, can also count on our support. finally, i would like to stress that we are and will remain committed to a scientifically based, objective and evidence-based policy.
mr president, the anxiety of europeans is growing with the crises and with the resignations. the glyphosate saga, as has been said, with this new five-year renewal, the crisis of eggs contaminated with fipronil it was last summer the soap opera of neonicotinoids, insecticides that kill bees that finally led to the ban, last may, of three of them. one thing is clear: pesticide legislation is not being properly applied by the member states. the abusive use of emergency authorisations, in particular, must cease. in belgium, it is the use of this procedure, provided for in article 53, which justifies the continued use of a substance as dangerous to the respiratory tract as chloropicrin on strawberry plants in the open field. this brings us back to the application of the precautionary principle, which, i would remind you, is a principle of action and not an encouragement to sit still, to do nothing. our parliament has an urgent duty to do so with regard to pesticides, as with other products. it must put the health of europeans first, as proposed in the excellent report by our colleague pavel poc, which i of course support.

the committee of the regions thank you, mr president. with the new legislation on health technology assessment, we are taking a step in the right direction for three reasons. first, we are concerned with every patient, no matter where in the european union. this european collaboration will increase the chances of patients with a rare cancer or disease having access to a medicine that works. subject to the necessary protection of patient and company privacy, member states will have access to larger-scale research data that they could not have collected separately. secondly, this european clinical assessment is a good development for the manufacturer. the pharmaceutical industry only has to undergo one clinical trial at european level, not 28. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union is able to provide the necessary information to the member states. the clinical evaluation of new health technologies ensures that member states are kept informed of market developments and whether these products perform better than those already on the market. by putting more emphasis on innovative developments, we are boosting our health sector at european level. thirdly, i am very much in favour of maintaining flexibility, both for the member states and for the coordination group which is drawing up the evaluation. it is up to the member states themselves to decide whether to introduce a reimbursement system for the new technology. some member states can extend their voluntary cooperation even further, as is already the case with eunethta or beneluxa. the coordination group shall keep track of which new technologies are ground-breaking. if we want to fully develop research and innovation in europe, with the patient at the centre, then a european health technology assessment is the way forward together with the member states. first, ms gesine meissner would like to thank the rapporteur, soledad cabezón ruiz, for her good work and all the shadow rapporteurs for their collaboration. this is a very important file that will accelerate access to medicines for patients in the eu. by simplifying procedures by doing them once for the eu market instead of once in every eu member state, it will indeed reduce the workload of the administrative bodies in the member states the scientists, but also the manufacturers. however, we are aware of member states' reservations regarding their competences. that is why it was one of my key priorities to draw a clear distinction between eu and national competences. we have managed to negotiate overall compromises on all the paragraphs which alde supported in the committee on the environment, public health and food safety (envi) vote and which it will support in the plenary vote as well. one of my key concerns was the inclusion of high-risk medical devices in the scope of an eu hta. with the new medical devices regulation we have just adopted new standards on the safety and performance of medical devices. regardless of their class, medical devices must be proven to be safe and their performance must be verified. a new requirement of the clinical benefits, which is the added value of a medical device, will also have to be demonstrated. i propose to focus on strong legislation for medical products and look at the medical devices at the second stage. although my approach did not make it entirely into the final compromises, i was very pleased to see that the rapporteur agreed to further narrow the scope on medical devices. this is a good starting point for further negotiation. generally speaking, i think we have reached a good and solid agreement which gives a good basis for the negotiations with the council and the commission.

president, ladies and gentlemen, we must stop illegal immigration into europe. the only way to do this is to build on the australian model. it is incomprehensible that ngo boats pick up migrants as far as just off the coast of north africa and then sail towards europe instead of to a safe port in the region. saving lives is noble, but supporting illegal migration to europe is not. the european summit in june decided to examine whether regional disembarkation platforms could be an alternative. these centres in third countries would then take in the drowning. that practice is fully in line with international law. it was decided in one breath that all boats operating in the mediterranean should not interfere with the libyan coast guard. so it is not when libya, like those platforms, stops boats heading for europe. the rescue operation, which no longer automatically results in an asylum application in europe, will have a strong deterrent effect. as in australia, that will bring the tragic drowning to zero. this is how we can bring migration back under control, which increases the support of the citizens for the really needy and the smugglers lose their source of income. i would like to ask the commission and all the relevant ministers in the european union, as do the european heads of government, to adopt the australian model. i hope that the european summit will finally reach concrete agreements to tackle illegal migration in a coordinated way.

the rapporteur. - mr president, commissioner vella, ladies and gentlemen, this is a good story. in the mid-1950s, plastic, invented a century earlier, was a revolution. an invention that changed everything, i must say, that changed products, that changed quantities and that changed our behaviour, with the advent of the disposable. today, this story is turning into a nightmare: 8 billion tonnes produced in less than 65 years for 6 billion tonnes of waste. the figures are telling: over 400 000 tonnes a year for europe alone. everywhere, the oceans have become our ultimate garbage and they're dying from it. images are being circulated in all the media: our beaches disfigured by garbage, cormorants suffocated by plastic bags, sea turtles whose stomachs are colonized by pieces of plastic ... these images are unbearable. the commission is therefore proposing to cover the ten single-use plastic items most commonly found on our beaches, plus fishing gear, which together account for 70% of marine litter. and the 51 members of the committee on the environment, public health and food safety who supported my report in the vote, have a realistic objective - i want to stress this - which is to ban this single-use plastic where a sustainable alternative exists, such as paper, bamboo, glass, cornstarch, etc. at the same time, this will stimulate innovation and create jobs in europe. the commission goes so far as to speak of 50 000 jobs. it also involves a series of progressive measures, an obligation, especially for producers, to take responsibility, the labelling of certain products that are difficult to recycle, awareness campaigns and, of course, ambitious measures to reduce the consumption of certain articles. as rapporteur, i wanted to raise the level of ambition of the commission's proposal with the fictitious rapporteurs, whom i will not name here but whom i would like to thank very sincerely for the excellence of our cooperation. to this end, we have added three products to the original list of prohibited products - this one is known - cotton buds, straws, plates, cutlery, and towels. we add in this case expanded polystyrene packaging, which is found in fast food, oxidized bags, which break down under the influence of light and therefore contribute to marine pollution, and ultralight bags, i.e. those bags of less than 15 microns, while maintaining a derogation mainly for hygiene reasons. i would like to make a small comment on this: i clearly do not share the view of some of my colleagues who, by deleting and derogating from the text, attack the very heart of our text and try to save the plates and cutlery made in southern italy or the german balloons. i would like to make it clear that the text does not in any way provide for the banning of balloons and the attack, as i read today, on the enjoyment of children. this type of argument is euroscepticism. that is not in the text and i shall return to that text. where we have not banned, we propose a robust legal framework, a timetable to reduce the consumption of food containers and cups; the ppe tabled an amendment in plenary to reduce consumption of these two products by 25% immediately, which i fully support. the signal sent to the member states is the right one. i am opposed to amendment no 122 which completely exempts smes until 2023 and opens the way, of course, to all legal abuse and optimisation. as regards plastic bottles, we are adding to the separate collection target of 90% a target of 35% recycled content collection by 2025, and some of the industry's leaders are already there. for fishing gear, nets, ropes, etc., which are responsible for 27% of marine pollution, this is a priority. i am reinforcing the commission's text by drawing on the best practices we have from norway and iceland, experts if there are any. the committee on the environment, public health and food safety has also supported my wish to include the fight against environmental damage from tobacco in this report. i'm obviously referring to the plastic filters in cigarettes, which are cellulose acetate. an ambitious reduction target: minus 50% by 2025, minus 80% by 2030, and finally we confirm the responsibility of producers under the polluter-pays principle. in conclusion, mr president, the people expect us to adopt the most ambitious legislation against single-use plastics on wednesday. it is now, ladies and gentlemen, that we must make this choice for the environment, for the generations that follow us and also for the millions, the tens of millions of europeans who are mobilized, who are watching us and who are ready to consume differently.

on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, the first time that i went to your country, president iohannis, to bucharest, was in december 1989 in one of those old tarom airplanes. i have to tell you, it was torture. with no leg-room, it was a little bit like flying with ryanair today. for me it was the first time that i went to a communist country, because before the fall of the berlin wall, we liberals were not allowed to go. in bucharest – i remember it very well – i met the historical leader of the romanian liberals, radu-anton câmpeanu, in an empty building, in an empty office with one table and one chair. he had just returned from exile in paris in december 1989. when i came back here, what i did was immediately to send a truck full of office furniture and computers. a few months later these capitalist gifts were destroyed by a furious mob of miners, the henchmen of iliescu. so i had to send a second truck to romania to help mr câmpeanu build up his liberal and democratic movement. not only did i send office furniture and computers, i also sent material for a radio station that became one of the first private radio stations in your country – radio contact – with the rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on foreign affairs. mr president, commissioner, as we have heard, the schengen information system is at the heart of security cooperation within the european union. the changes we are going to propose today are actually much needed to increase our security in the fight against terrorism. hence the use of facial recognition and fingerprinting. they're all necessary adjustments. but today i am particularly pleased with what my colleague, mr gérard deprez, has achieved. the preventive warnings for children at risk of parental abduction, child marriage or genital mutilation. this reform was badly needed. just like the reform we're implementing for the children in migration, for the children on the run. finally, it will be possible to use dna profiles to identify and track them more quickly. ladies and gentlemen, this third reform of the schengen information system will undoubtedly increase our security in europe.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the schengen information system is fully operational. this will increase the efficiency of the sis database in the areas of border surveillance, the follow-up of illegal returns and police cooperation. this database is also crucial for our security in the light of the free movement of persons within the schengen area. however, an evaluation revealed shortcomings ranging from quality problems, lack of training of police personnel to insufficient information on signalling and delays in the follow-up of hits. these proposals now address these shortcomings, but they go beyond mere cosmetic adjustments or technical improvements. now, individuals can only be searched in the database by their name or date of birth, for example. but in the future, criminals can be traced by their fingerprints. so much for identity fraud. it is also important that fingerprints found at the crime scene can be entered into the system. this is our way of increasing the chances of catching criminals on the run. a more efficient and user-friendly database is absolutely necessary to enable our police and border guards to do their jobs properly. together with the interoperability of databases by linking all kinds of databases, we are taking a new step forward in the fight against crime, human trafficking and terrorism. i therefore call on all the groups to vote in favour of these very necessary amendments. after all, safety is a matter for all of us.
on behalf of the alde group. mr president, commissioners, ladies and gentlemen, many european citizens and i are happy to enjoy freedom of movement in the european area. but what the public knows less about and what our two excellent rapporteurs, carlos coelho and jeroen lenaers, know perfectly well - and i would like to congratulate them - is the central role played by the schengen information system (sis) in guaranteeing this freedom. the necessary corollary of freedom of movement is effective control of external borders and increased cooperation between police forces within the european common space. commissioners avramopoulos and king, as well as jeroen lenaers, recalled the indisputable figures on the current effectiveness of the schengen information system. but this efficiency, however great it was, still had too many of the shortcomings we had pointed out. it therefore needed to be improved. that is what is proposed to our assembly in these three reports. firstly, member states will now have to enter an alert in the sis for any person involved in criminal or terrorist offences. europol will be informed immediately, allowing the rapid initiation of cooperation between european police forces. secondly, from now on, member states will have to enter an alert in the sis for any person who has been banned from residing in the european union. this is not the case at present and this considerably hampers the implementation of decisions to leave the territory. thirdly, member states will now be able to enter an alert in sis for missing children, children at risk of abduction, girls at risk of forced marriage or female genital mutilation. with these changes, ladies and gentlemen, the sis will no longer be just a repressive tool, which is still necessary, but will also become an effective means of protecting the most vulnerable people, which is something we should all be happy about. for the greens/efa group mr president, ladies and gentlemen, mr tusk, i do not know whether you are interested in this, but we can always talk. the treaty on european union makes the european council the body which gives the necessary impetus to the development of the union and defines its general political priorities. the citizens of the union are therefore entitled to expect a certain political leadership from the 28 heads of state or government who make up the union. between climate change and growing inequalities, conflict and persecution at our borders, the threat of terrorism, the impact of the digital revolution and the exorbitant power of multinationals, it is hard to say that our union is facing existential challenges in a world where its friends are becoming scarcer. this is probably why the european council in october 2017 adopted a work programme, somewhat pompously called 'the leaders' agenda'. one year later, where are we? we welcome the adoption last spring of the declaration on the european pillar of social rights. although it is very much a statement of intent, it is worth recalling that human well-being is not limited to maximising the profit of a few. but apart from that, the leadership of the european council is still being sought and its meeting last week provided, once again, a sad example of this. on the question of brexit, the 27 heads of state and government cannot be held responsible for the failure, we are faced here with a british government caught in its own contradictions and a theresa may torn between the members of her majority and her party. until she cuts this gordian knot, no deal can be made. but on the other cases, no excuses for the leaders! asylum and immigration are two issues that have been on the agenda of all european council meetings for more than three years - with one exception - but the heads of state of the member states are still unable to agree on a common approach to the dignified reception of asylum seekers on our continent. the only common ground at the moment is to discharge their responsibilities to the neighbouring states of the european union. it is a year since this parliament found the political courage and creativity to propose common solutions on the same issue, in line with the values of the european union, and this is proof that a political agreement on this issue is indeed possible if there is political will. on the question of monetary union, the heads of state or government have not made any progress. how many times must we repeat that 'economic and monetary union is not viable' - i repeat, not viable - without budgetary union and therefore fiscal union and social union. here again, as tensions are being rekindled by the budgetary choices of the italian government, the 'leaders' remain in denial of reality. as an environmentalist, i would be tempted to make fun of co2 emissions and the brussels deadlock caused by yet another inconsequential european summit. but this lack of leadership is not without consequences. it is daily weakening the european union's ability to meet the challenges of our time and undermining its credibility. it seems that the conservatives, the christian democrats, the social democrats and the liberals who make up the council are proving less and less able to understand these challenges and are taking refuge in a choreography that has no impact on reality. is it any wonder that more and more of our citizens are choosing to look elsewhere for answers to their questions? of course, with nationalists of all stripes, but also, and i am glad to say, increasingly with environmentalists, as the recent elections in bavaria, belgium and luxembourg have shown, and who knows, perhaps in a few days in hesse.

mr president, we are all concerned about the use of veterinary medicines, since they have a direct impact on our diet, the environment and our health. i am therefore pleased that we are voting in favour of reducing the use of antibiotics on farms to limit the presence of resistant bacteria in food. in this respect, it is essential that veterinary medicinal products are not used in any way to improve the performance of farms and to compensate for non-compliance with good farming practice. preventive administration of antibiotics without clinical signs of infection should be strictly limited. owners and keepers of farmed animals should use breeding animals with appropriate genetic diversity in proportions that do not increase the risk of disease transmission and isolate diseased animals from the rest of the group. finally, i am fully satisfied that the centralised marketing authorisation for veterinary medicinal products is being separated from that for medicinal products for human use.

on behalf of the s&d group. – madam president, as has been mentioned, tomorrow we will be voting on three very important reports on the clean energy package. this package represents the most important work done in the field of energy in this term, and it defines the way forward for our energy landscape for the next decade. it has been a tough battle for parliament to succeed, as we all know and as has been mentioned, on the energy efficiency and the renewable goals. in the first reading in this parliament we asked for 35% for both. we were not able to maintain that, but we can be proud of this result as we had to overcome the resistance of the council and, unfortunately, also a rather weak basis to work on from the commission. this again is proof that this parliament is of utmost importance to keep ambitions very high, and in that sense the regulation on governance is very important, especially given the absence of national binding targets on energy efficiency and renewable energy. the governance regulation provides the commission with the tool to integrate and evaluate the national energy and climate plan. each member state has to come forward by the end of this year. if the sum of the pledges in these plans do not add up to the union’s collective goals, the commission can make a member state step up that ambition. it is extremely important to step them up because, as we know, the proposals that are in front of us are a first step but are not enough to meet our climate objectives to keep the temperature well below the 2 degrees – and, you know, that should be 1.5 degrees. this package deals with the goals towards the 2030 targets, and in that context of climate and energy targets, that means tomorrow. therefore, however important, this is not an end, and this is not a stop. these reports are a crucial step of many to come. reaching our climate and energy goals, as well as each of the sdgs, in a socially just manner, is the top priority of the s&d group, and we shall put these challenges not just here, but in all the files that we negotiate: for instance, the multiannual financial framework is the next step to come. we are also looking to the long-term decarbonisation strategy for 2050, mr commissioner. we expect the commission to take into account the most recent developments and challenges ahead of us – like, for instance, the again alarming results of the ipcc report and parliament’s request stated in the resolution on cop24. the last but very crucial point for our group is the requirement of combating energy poverty that we managed to get into the energy efficiency and into the government’s report. it’s the first time – and very important – that very specific obligations aimed at people living in energy poverty have been enshrined in european legislation. and that, i can say, is a true victory for my political group.
president. - ladies and gentlemen, tomorrow we will take a major step towards more renewable energy and greater energy efficiency in the european union. we have negotiated this legislation long and hard and it is now time to cut the knots. i am counting on an overwhelming support in this parliament. everyone's had to add water to the wine, including myself as a shadow reporter. it is clear that we all want to achieve the paris objectives, but we must not forget that we must also involve our citizens, because it is they, our citizens and our companies, who will pay for all these measures. in countries where progress has been made, such as germany with the energiewende, we see that the hoped-for results for the environment will not be achieved in the first decades, unless we want to burden the germans with a huge bill. so we have to be ambitious, but we have to be realistic. we also need to continue to convince our trading partners to join us in an eco-realistic story. the eu accounts for barely 10% of global emissions. so we cannot compete with ourselves and we must also advocate the green revolution in other parts of the world.
the committee on the environment, public health and food safety has adopted a report on the proposal for a council directive on the approximation of the laws of the member states relating to the protection of the health of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work. mr president, i would like to begin by thanking the rapporteurs for this report, which is ambitious. it is realistic, however, and it focuses on investments which create real added value and which really show the citizens of europe that europe can make a difference. two examples: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the life+ programme, for which parliament is requesting a doubling of the funds. i think that's really necessary. and secondly: just energy transition fund, i think that is really necessary, also to prove to people that europe is also there to protect the citizens. but of course there are also wishes from our committee, explicit questions on the health programme, health programme, to make it a full-fledged programme, given the revolutionary changes, digital e-health, cross-border developments in health care that are about to come. in conclusion: those who say that a europe of 27 member states can be achieved with a smaller budget are mistaken when you consider the challenges we face. you cannot shout that europe must protect its people and then start cutting budgets. it is therefore essential that we also make use of these own resources and make them a breaking point.
in writing. our union is facing greater challenges than ever before, such as migration, climate change, disruptive technologies and unstable regions on the union's borders. the union must have the necessary manpower to cope with these challenges, including in the budgetary field. we therefore call for a budget of at least 1.3% of europe's gross national income and the possibility of financing at least 60% of the budget from own resources. but it is equally important to ensure that this money is also spent properly. the key guidelines for this are our commitments under the paris agreement and the sustainable development goals (sdgs). the social democrats in parliament have succeeded in getting the other groups to agree to a 30% specific allocation of expenditure to achieving climate objectives. moreover, in the interim report we were able to emphasise the view that the entire mff should be geared towards achieving the sdgs. this means that all investments which are not in line with these objectives, or which undermine them, must be stopped. we want a european budget in which all investments contribute to a social and sustainable europe, which puts the citizen at the centre.

the temptation for investors is great. the bitcoin, the ether, the litecoin, the ripple, they all promise huge returns that you can never achieve with traditional savings products. but to this day, anyone who invests in bitcoins and other virtual currencies is riding a roller coaster without wearing a seat belt. as an investment, the risk is enormous: the course really does go all out; there is no intervention by banks or countries; there are no rules, and there is no oversight. if something goes wrong, like your digital wallet getting hacked, there's nowhere to turn. that intervention is necessary is an understatement. we all agree on that. in addition to the need for consumer protection, there is another problem: a potential jackpot combined with anonymity obviously attracts investors, but unfortunately also criminal organizations. exact figures are lacking, but rough estimates suggest that more than €7 billion of criminal crypto-currency is involved annually. the key question is this one: can we use these coins to connect the online user to the real identity behind them? bitcoin can do that, but dash, for example, can't. we have to keep that in mind when we regulate. one last question . who should regulate? well, the players in this game are unfortunately not bound to our european borders alone. a g-20 initiative on a global framework for the regulation and control of virtual currencies is therefore necessary. but let us not stop that, dear commissioner. time is money, especially in this case. literally, i mean. only by taking swift and consistent action will we be able to close the loopholes in the network systematically.
the belgian government has already signed the marrakesh political declaration in may of this year, with the minister of the interior, mr jambon, and the state secretary for migration, mr francken, in the lead. this time to sign this global compact for migration. this text is as disastrous as the geneva convention. international agreements, conventions and treaties all have one thing in common: they are the product of foreign diplomats and politicians. they are written over the heads of the people and the consequences are forced on them behind their backs. for this reason, migration is only an exceptional issue where free nations can delegate their powers. under active pressure from my party, the flemish interest, the belgian decision was postponed. i call on the belgian government not to sign this un migration pact.
the european parliament adopted the telecoms package today. the new rules will end excessive consumer charges, provide faster internet and help facilitate the roll-out of a 5g network in europe. the abolition of roaming has already made it much cheaper to use telephony and the internet abroad, but we are still stuck with loose ends. it is still very expensive to call a friend or colleague abroad. that is now being solved by setting maximum rates, and i am very happy about that. we must not faint: a lot of international communication today is already happening online, through whatsapp for example. however, this measure still affects 9% of european users, especially those who are not very good at digital. this package is a step towards a better internal market with greater consumer protection. the physical barriers have been removed, and so too have the barriers to communication. it is also important that there should be a european-wide warning system in the event of a disaster or an attack. the reverse 112 will allow you and me as citizens to be notified if there is a serious incident in the area. that too can only be an advantage.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve the quality of the road transport services. the nva delegation will vote in favour of ambitious co2 standards and support sales targets for low and zero emission trucks. as much as a quarter of co2 emissions in road transport come from trucks. if we're going to effectively meet our climate commitments and reduce our carbon emissions by 40 percent by 2030, we just need those standards. the technology is available, by the way! we must not forget that greening the fleet is also essential for improving air quality. our citizens have a right to clean air. the standards also save fuel, which is a win-win for businesses and consumers. ambitious product standardisation is also needed to attract innovation and investment. third countries such as the united states and china are already investing heavily in this. the european union must not fall behind. the transport sector is also a party to the dispute. so why would we doubt? i'd like to put the next footnote. we support the introduction of a bonus-malus system, but we think it is a bad idea to include electric city buses in the achievement of the green truck targets. we are giving an unfair advantage to those manufacturers who build both buses and lorries and we are threatening to undermine the ambition of the lorries themselves. so let's think about it.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, everyone has already said: the next european elections will be extremely important for our future. voters will have to choose between parties which are pro-european, which are pro-european cooperation or parties which want to turn back the clock and actually hide behind national borders. i think we all know that there are forces, powers, that actually benefit from europe not becoming a strong player in the international market. so these forces will not hesitate to manipulate the elections to their advantage. the romans already knew: divide and rule. there are two ways of doing this, commissioner, either by falsifying the votes or by influencing the electorate through false information. hence my appeal: we must be vigilant in both fields. firstly, we need to set up networks in which member states exchange best practice to protect voting procedures. many colleagues have already advocated this. the second point is even more important to me. we need to make sure that we're arming citizens against fake news, against disinformation campaigns. i would like to make a very specific proposal, commissioner. i would like to make a plea here today for the appointment of a real special representative against disinformation, against fake news, against cybercrime. i think we really need that: a special representative who can immediately investigate whether we are dealing with fake news, who can immediately intervene with campaigns to correct that fake news, so that we can make europe great.
delays, cancellations and incomplete information are also faced in belgium, more than we would like. the current european rules do not provide sufficient guarantees for the quality of service. if we want to motivate more people to take public transport and if the railway companies themselves want to be ready to compete with new players in the market soon, then we need to do better. if these companies are not getting the necessary structural reforms today, it is because there are too many exemptions and preferential measures. the european parliament is already pointing the way. increased compensation for delays, which means additional financial pressure to drive faster, the right of passengers to always take their bicycles on the train and stronger ombudsmen services, that is the railway of tomorrow. but a word of caution: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the railway companies are not forced to pay for the services they provide. furthermore, additional efforts are needed to improve accessibility for disabled people. we must now move towards a modern, attractive and competitive railway market.
on behalf of the alde group. mr president, commissioner, the courage of people with lyme disease is admirable, because it is a real wall that they often encounter in their journey of suffering. first, there is the misunderstanding of the large number of lay doctors who simply do not have the tools to detect this disease in time, even though it already affects one million europeans, a number that is constantly increasing, especially because of climate change. another obstacle that patients face is the conservatism of medical academies. in many cases, they still claim that the elisa test is 100% reliable, when all the scientific publications show the opposite. in our text we denounce this hostage-taking of patients, who have the right to be tested in a totally reliable way. it is therefore essential to take up existing best practice, together with good care for patients with chronic lyme disease, as is the case in germany and the grand duchy of luxembourg. this is clearly what all the european associations, of which there are hundreds on the ground, are asking us to do. in my home country of wallonia, i think particularly of sonia boulanger, who coordinates the asbl's time for lyme. there is hope - we have already spoken about this in this debate - doors are opening. the european union has just added lyme disease to the list of communicable diseases, which is essential for networking researchers. who recently changed the codes for lyme disease in the international classification of diseases, recognising the dementia associated with it and transmission from mother to child. in conclusion, we have said that funding for research into lyme disease is essential because of the complexity of its diagnosis and the variety of its clinical forms. in the ten seconds i have left, i will remind you of this: a gesture, a habit, a card, a tick clip for parents and for children could already, in the first place, avoid many complications.
