but anyway they are saying that they are not so stupid as to leave the european union. let’s reform the european union: that is the message of the danish people as i see it in the eurobarometer. and i hear this not only in denmark but in many countries of the european union when we are talking about the future. what we have to do now, prime minister – and this is in opposition to mr vistisen who wants everything as a conservative, so nothing will change for him, otherwise he is not a conservative – what we have to do now is to take the lesson of brexit seriously. not say ‘oh brexit, it’s only the uk going out and let’s just go on the same way’. no, take the lessons from brexit, because the uk leaving the european union is not an enormous success, it’s a failure of the european union. so we have to reform the european union. i want to mention two things. first, the so-called european army, as angela merkel called it. i think that mr weber has given all the arguments about why we need a foreign policy, why we need a european army – and mr vistisen, it has nothing to do with killing nato – for me a european army is the european pillar of the north atlantic treaty organisation, and it is absolutely not in opposition to that. but we have to stop the waste of money on defence in europe: we are spending 40% of what the americans spend on their army but we are incapable of doing 10% what the american army does. we are spending three times more than the russians on military defence in europe – three times more! but i’m not sure, prime minister, that if the russians come over our borders we will be capable of stopping them without american help. my second point concerns the single market. we need the single market to complete it. you are totally right. we have a single market for belgian chocolates. very good. we have a single market for french champagne. good also. we have a single market for german cars. fine. we have a german market for danish design. i’m not talking about ikea now, i am talking about real design, especially in denmark. but we do not have a single market for the services of the future. we don’t have one for telecom, or for digital, or for the capital markets. that can only be realised when we have a single regulator in europe. at one time we had one standard for mobile, you might remember, the gsm standard, a european standard. we had champions in europe, nokia, eriksson... and now we don’t have european standards, and among the 200 biggest high-tech companies there are only eight europeans. none of the 20 biggest high-tech companies in the world are european. so i want to support you in your endeavour to create this digital single market as quickly as possible and to do it with one regulator. and finally, on the values, also there denmark has to play a role, manfred – we cannot go forward with illiberal tendencies in the european union, and there also denmark will play a key role.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, i would like to welcome the prime minister to the european parliament. when denmark comes into the conversation, many good things spring to mind: the global champion of renewable energy, a world leader in development aid, a country that has the guts to confront russia’s power plays by saying ‘no’ to nord stream 2 (and for that thank you), and a country where the social partners are strong and which invented flexicurity as a way to give security to workers and flexibility to employers, and not the other way round. your country also gave us margrethe vestager, the most impressive competition commissioner for a very long time, a real liberal fighting rent-extraction, even when it gets in the way of sweetheart deals given by governments to multinationals. denmark, as a small country like my own, has a lot to offer to the european union. you had four points. i have only three, but at least we have two in common. let me start with the ecological and just transition. we share your view that it’s vital for europe to be a world leader in that transition. we need to ensure that all societies operate within the boundaries that our planet has set for them. we also need to do it in a way that is good for everyone and not just for the happy few like we can see in france. it is an environmental imperative, a social necessity, a geopolitical aspiration, but also – and you mentioned this – an economic opportunity not to be missed. one could expect denmark to share in that aspiration towards leadership – and listening to you, i tend to believe this – and we praise you for pushing europe to the most ambitious standards for instance on co2 emissions for cars. thank you for that as well. but your country – and this is a bit surprising – ranks fifth in europe in terms of carbon footprint by household. if you don’t change political course, the non-renewable part of your energy mix is actually expected to increase, and thus your co2 emissions would rise and not fall. on the second point, migration, we believe that the european union should assume its part of the responsibility in facing the asylum and migration challenge. we agree with you when you say that this is a place where more european action is needed. but, under your party’s leadership, denmark is now joining hands with those who want to make europe a fortress, slamming its door shut to those in need. when you speak about tackling the root causes of migration, you are only focusing on the smugglers. not only is your country reducing its development budget, because you claim – and rightly so – that it is 0.7% of gdp, but it used to be 1% of gdp. but it is now increasingly using this development budget to force beneficiary countries to lock their borders and take back migrants who might have escaped. prime minister, when people forcibly leave their country, it is because they are fleeing wars, persecution, climate change, natural resource exhaustion or their social and economic conditions. if they turn to smugglers, it is for a lack of safe and legal ways to access safe countries such as ours. nowadays, the key words of eu asylum and migration policy are pushback and containment. we believe that we should welcome our fair share, while working on the real root causes. of course europe cannot solve all the world’s problems. but we can do much more to fight climate change, to have a fairer global economic system, and to promote democracy and the rule of law instead of supporting tyrannies, notably by selling them weapons and buying natural resources extracted with little respect for the local communities. let me conclude with a point that you didn’t tackle: the euro. denmark is in the unenviable position of actually not being part of the euro, but having its currency pegged to it. we are disappointed that your country is now joining the camp of the naysayers who deny reality by claiming that the euro is safe and viable just by applying the current rules. on the contrary, we are convinced that no monetary union is viable without strong financial solidarity, both in the private and public sectors. that means a fiscal union where common taxes fund a common budget and, possibly, basic elements of a common social safety net. it also means a full banking union that eliminates the doom loop between states and their banking systems. and of course financial solidarity comes with responsibility enshrined in rules. but these rules need to be based on sound economic science, which is not always the case – far from it – and protect the general, rather than particular, interests. indeed, mutual trust is badly affected when these rules are breached. speaking of which, mr prime minister, such breaches sometimes come from unexpected quarters, like when danske bank is exposed as one of the worst money launderers in europe. mr prime minister, we share your european ambition. i’m not sure that we agree on everything, but i’m glad to see denmark engaging. i’m sure that we are going to have lively debates because in this – we could hear this, for instance, from mr vistisen – we have strikingly different visions about the future of europe. but you know what? i am confident that next year, during the european elections, those who are committed to discourse in our continent, to keep it as a beacon of peace, human rights, liberty and freedoms will prevail.
prime minister of denmark. i am not sure that this is the case. i am grateful for the many comments and questions, many of them from danish members of the european parliament who are committed to the cause. i'm going to try to answer as many as i can. in relation to the green transition, bendt bendtsen is in favour of - and i think it is wise - that we should also reach out for the low-hanging fruit, and they are there to be found. and there is no doubt that greater efforts to improve energy efficiency and renewable energy are part of that. there is a lot that could be done if all europeans washed at 10 degrees below what we normally do, that is equivalent to the total capacity of all german nuclear power stations - that is, if we washed at 30 degrees instead of 40 degrees - and similar calculations could be made on energy renewal. i think it just goes to show that in the green transition we must use all the instruments, and what you are pointing to, bendt, is one of them. i would like to say to mr grapini, who is creating the impression that i should be against the idea that people who work in denmark, for example, do not have the same rights as danes who work in denmark. that's not what i'm saying! it is clear that if you come to denmark and you bring your children with you, you must be able to work and live under the same conditions as everyone else. i am talking about the situation where you do not bring your children with you and where the danish child benefit, which is supposed to cover the costs of having children in danish society, is sent to other countries where the cost of living is much lower and the money therefore goes further. this is the situation i'm talking about. then grapini asked me about my views on the mff, and it could be a very long talk. i would just say very briefly that under a reasonable framework, 1% of gnp, which would be an increasing budget, given that there is growth in europe, our priorities will be to invest in new areas. it is, among other things, development, research, innovation, but it is also - to comment on the very last question from the finnish member of parliament - a more holistic approach to the whole challenge of migration in all its forms, and many have been on board: margrethe auken has been on board, jens rohde is suggesting it, many others are on board, and i think that the time has come when we need to talk about these things in a context and be a little clear when we talk about these things. there is an extreme difference between a young african who, quite naturally, so to speak, seeks happiness in a prosperous europe in order to improve his living conditions, and the individual persecuted who is actually on the run, versus the internal movement in europe on a labour market. and there is a tendency in the debate - perhaps not here, but in other places where i participate in the debate - to conflate it all into one big pear-shaped mess. i am a strong supporter of free movement in europe. denmark will not be able to function unless we have the privilege of drawing on labour from elsewhere. sometimes, too, problems arise. i am a strong supporter of our efforts to deal with and combat, deal with and secure people who are on the run. that is why i am proud to come from a country where we, as only a handful of countries in the world, are increasing the un target of 0.7 in development aid. that is why we need - and mr jens rohde asks what we are going to do in the council - to develop a comprehensive strategy which is not just about protecting the external borders, which is not just about providing development aid in africa, which is not just about supporting a genuinely free market economy in africa which has the potential, in the image of europe, to develop an african internal market. but it can't be just that. it has to be all at once, and it's the only way we're gonna get everyone on board. that is also why i am of the opinion, in relation to mr morten helveg and others who have been involved in the question of reallocation and solidarity, that we must work from a real point of view, and the real point is that it is not possible to reform the dublin system on the basis of mandatory reallocation. it is not enough to say that, it is going to divide europe. and then, with that start, you have to pragmatically discuss what you are going to put in its place. what we can do instead is to create a different kind of committed solidarity, which is not just about taking in refugees, but about making a different contribution in a different way in a different place. because if we do not get on with the development of this concept, which is both about protecting europe and reaching out to africa, and which is also about ensuring that people who are temporarily fleeing something have a temporary place, an opportunity to return, then we will not be able to deal with the overall challenge that is huge, huge, and perhaps in fact the biggest challenge we face. i must say to ronja, on the subject of referendums and democracy: danish participation in the european union is based not on just one referendum but on several. after the danish people voted no to maastricht, a national compromise was reached in denmark, which was also put to a referendum, and that is where our reservations lie. and it goes without saying that if the conditions to which the danes have agreed in the maastricht treaty are to be changed, then of course a referendum is needed. but this cannot be reduced to the view that any adjustment of cooperation which is not covered by the danish reservations requires a referendum. i do not subscribe to the view that we should have this year's referendum to decide whether we still want to be involved. i say this also as a comment on hekel and the considerations of a new deal, if and if the british house of commons does not reach a decision to support the agreement that has been reached, and denmark here, in the light of our history, should invite us in and say that we will just find another solution. in my reading of it, the agreement that theresa may has made is, of course, to be compared with what we did in denmark when the danes back around maastricht said no to maastricht, namely that we defined our national bid on the circumstances under which we can then have a relationship. we went to a referendum on this and won, and it will not be a viable path if it is to be renegotiated in stages. so i think i have to say that i agree with juncker and all my colleagues in the council who have said that now there has been a very complicated process, where every comma in a very, very complicated agreement has been reversed. it is like the construction on the table, and it is therefore the one you have to decide on. i would like to conclude by saying that when the lithuanian member of parliament asks me how i would view things, for example from a lithuanian perspective, in the light of the differences in living standards. and it is also a reply to christel schaldemose and others who have touched on the question of free movement and fairness of things - jens rohde too, mr morten lykkegaard too. the basic idea is that, as a precondition for freedom of movement, when you move to another place, you have to be able to operate under the same conditions as those you had before - that is, in terms of pay and employment. if you look at denmark as a showcase here, you have to say that where we are challenged is not where people move into denmark for a shorter or longer period of time and say: 'i'll take a job here'. if we look at their level of coverage in terms of collective agreements, it is almost on a par with the danes in general, and that is not where the problems arise. the problems arise in relation to service, the problems arise in relation to transport. and the problems we have seen recently are precisely linked to that. we must do something about it, and we must tackle it in a very concrete way, and parliament has a job to do here too. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. it is a question of setting some requirements as to the wage conditions on which one can drive around. it is about making sure that we do not get a lot of exemptions from the general services directive in relation to the transport sector. it is a very practical piece of work that must be done and we must work together on it if we are to secure the support of the people. for my own reading of the danish people is that they are not against free movement, they are against situations where you find that under the guise of free movement you are in fact offering people wages and conditions that we would not accept to take on our own bodies. that is what this is about, and it is a piece of practical work that needs to be done. i think i will just end by thanking jens rohde for his kind words. and then perhaps a single thing about the un document, the global compact on migration, which is not a treaty, and which is not a marrakesh treaty, but which is a un document to be discussed in marrakesh. and i just want to try and put it into context to conclude on. it is not useful to introduce international cooperation where the standard is that you can only participate if everyone thinks the same as you. and that is the kind of shadow that i think we see cast on the european landscape, at least in a trend, and which is also felt in a few of the speeches here today. we want it the way we want it, or we don't want to be in it. it is against the whole concept, and i am of course speaking in favour of a danish debate, but i am also speaking in favour of a european debate. the european union is a dream, but it is also a reality, something we must deal with in practice. and it is no use dividing ourselves into camps where you have to either praise the whole project with all its glories, because otherwise you are probably not a true european, or else you have to criticise everything. we must have a balanced approach to this, that when we talk about crossborder problems and about raising european prosperity, even in a global competition that is getting sharper and sharper with an increasingly self-conscious china, with a united states that is turning its back on itself to some extent, then there must be cooperation! and we all have an obligation to start that dialogue with our citizens, where we are told - because i think we all understand this - that if everything has to be our own solution, there will be nothing to cooperate on. that is the difficult task, and that is also where i think - and i would therefore like to thank you for allowing me to come here today - that the whole idea of trying to launch a european debate with europeans at national level and anchored here in parliament is something. we are not done with that, we must also deliver on the concrete content, but we have to start where we are told that there is no alternative to cooperation, other than turning inwards and sliding backwards in terms of prosperity, etc. and with those words i would like to say a big thank you, mr president, for allowing me to be here today.
vice-president of the commission. – madam president, under this mandate we focused primarily on what needs to be done for the clean energy transition between now and 2030, and by the end of it, also thanks to the great cooperation we had with this house, we will have delivered on all our commitments. the policies put in place will go a long way even after 2030, but they will only bring us to emission reductions of around 60% by 2050, and this is not sufficient for the eu to contribute to the paris agreement temperature goals. climate change is already impacting on the european union. we had extreme heat waves in four of the last five years and this summer temperatures in the arctic circle were five degrees higher than normal. central europe was so dry that the rhine was in places too shallow for inland navigation. and this is just the beginning: a planet with a climate that’s out of control would not be a safe and prosperous home, and we have to act. today we are therefore looking further ahead towards 2050 and towards a climate-neutral europe, not only to contribute but to lead on the paris goals. the commission is responding to a call by this parliament from last october which was renewed last month. in march the european council also called on the commission to present a long-term strategy by the first quarter of 2019, taking into account national plans. parliament called for an ambitious mid-century zero emission strategy for the eu, providing a cost-efficient pathway towards a net zero carbon economy in the union by 2050 at the latest. the commission has taken up this challenge and we are starting from a good basis: we have reduced emissions by 22% while our economy grew by 58% since 1990 and this was accomplished by investing in clean energies, energy efficiency, innovation and cleaner transport. we have seen that this not only produces benefits for the environment and therefore for the citizens, but that it also creates growth and jobs. four million green jobs already exist in the european union. under this commission we have learned how to better integrate climate, energy, transport and other policies into the energy union and we are the world leader in designing coherent policies that drive investment, and investment that delivers results. the strategy we are presenting today builds on this comprehensive approach. the strategy looks at all the key economic sectors, at innovation, finance, agriculture, the circular economy, trade... in short, how to make climate neutrality a cornerstone of a modern european economy fit for the 21st century that will leave the planet clean. climate neutrality in 2050 will require additional investment – eur 175 to 290 billion a year – but it will drastically reduce our bill for fossil fuel imports, which today is eur 266 billion a year. the mathematics are clear, instead of paying to burn fuels that pollute our climate and our air, we can invest in cleaner and modern european industries that provide good local jobs. i’m particularly proud of one aspect. it’s a strategy that makes sure that we do not leave any europeans or any regions behind. it analyses which sectors will benefit, which will transform and which will lose out, and it looks into how to mitigate these losses, especially through upskilling for higher quality jobs, and it has european solidarity at its core. honourable members, there are many strong reasons to put forward the long-term strategy now: global commitments, economic rationale, but also a key political reason. more and more people are asking in the run-up to the european elections about the future of europe. there is widespread public support for climate action and actually widespread optimism: 85% of europeans agree that fighting climate change and using energy more efficiently can create economic growth and jobs in europe, and i am one of them.
member of the commission. – madam president, the long-term strategy adopted by the commission today spells out a clear vision for the european union climate and energy policy, one that can lead to net-zero greenhouse gas emissions by 2050, through a socially fair transition in a cost-efficient manner. the strategy is meant to set the direction of travel for european union climate efforts and to frame the european union contribution to achieving the paris agreement goals. in paris, the world committed to keeping the global temperature rise to well below 2°c and to pursue efforts to limit it to 1.5°c, and thanks to the support of the european parliament, the european union has adopted all the legislation needed to meet our paris commitment, and in fact in doing so we have de facto increased the level of ambition to some -45%, and the role of the parliament was instrumental in this process. we should now follow through and start preparing the next step, looking at the 2050 perspective. the difference between 1.5°c and 2°c is considerable, and the ipcc special report presented this year provides a clear signal. if we are to avoid the more disastrous impact of climate change we should seek to limit it to 1.5°c. the ipcc report also told us what we have to do collectively to limit climate change. the world will have to go to net zero greenhouse emissions in the second half of the century, but if the aim is to pursue 1.5° this goal needs to be reached at global level by 2070. the european union should lead in this transformation, demonstrating to others that the transition to net zero greenhouse gas emissions is possible and opens up many opportunities, and this is what the commission is doing today. with this strategy, the european union would become the first major economy to be climate neutral. if we succeed, others will follow. if we do not lead, nobody else will, and if nobody else acts unrestrained climate change will severely impact europe and rest of the world. the commission proposal is based on a detailed assessment looking at the possibilities for economic and social transformation that deliver such decarbonisation pathways. some pathways are looking at reductions going to 80% by 2050, while others look into pathways leading to net zero greenhouse gas emissions by 2050. the results clearly point to a comprehensive vision with seven key strategic building blocks that will be required to achieve a net zero greenhouse gas economy by 2050. first, energy efficiency plays a central role. in the future our economy will grow but we need to prepare for reducing energy consumption by as much as a half compared to 2005. energy efficiency is central to continue reducing emissions from industry but equally in the housing sectors. second, we need to advance the deployment of renewables. by 2050 more than 80% of electricity will be coming from renewable energy sources and these will have beneficial effects on our security of energy supply and deliver cumulative savings on our import bill of between eur 2 and 3 trillion over the period 2031-2050. there will also be additional benefits due to the reduced fugitive emissions of fossil fuels used in the energy sector and in this context, the commission is looking into policy options for addressing methane emissions. a central tenet here is the electrification of our energy system, at least doubling its share in final energy demand by 2050 and with electricity generated by renewable sources, spreading to more uses such as heating and transport. thirdly, we have to embrace a clean mobility system. the automotive industry already invested in this new future and electrification is a promising option but some transport modes will not be able to electrify. for them alternatives in the form of advanced biofuels and e-fuels would need to be applied. and it’s not only the vehicle or the fuel that can change. with 75% of our population living in urban areas, cities are breeding grounds for new solutions and we already see movement towards smart urban planning and traffic management. these, together with clean public transport, have the potential to reduce emissions and improve the quality of life. our industry is already one of the most efficient globally, but the transformation will converge with the need for modernising industrial assets. steel, cement and chemicals dominate industrial emissions and in the next 10 to 15 years technologies already known in these sectors will need to be demonstrated at a scale to facilitate investment in clean and competitive industrial installations. electrification, hydrogen, biomass and synthetic fuels are part of the solution. the transformation will require an adequate smart network infrastructure and interconnections and we need also to harmonise the potential of the bio-economy while preserving our carbon sinks. european union agriculture and forestry will continue to provide food, feed and fibres as well reduce emissions. continued efficiency improvements, better fertiliser application and nutrient management, and healthy animal stocks will benefit both farmers and the climate. agriculture and forestry will contribute also to decarbonising other sectors and eu forests will play a central role in providing biomass. the transition of our economy will always have to be careful of how to make best use of scarce land and other natural resources. and finally, we need to look into how to tackle with carbon capture and storage those co2 emissions which we cannot otherwise reduce. thanks to the rapid deployment of renewable energy this is no longer the silver bullet to decarbonising. still, ccs has a crucial role to close the circle for a net-zero economy, notably for energy intensive industries. if combined with sustainable biomass it could create negative emissions, helping to compensate for remaining emissions in our economy. while by no means easy, these options, if deployed together, can make our path towards net-zero greenhouse gas emissions a reality. but to achieve this vision we will need additional investment of around 0.8% of our gdp. but this can also benefit our gdp by up to 2% by 2050. this transformation will also save on health costs, with estimated reductions in premature death of more than 40% and health damage reduced by around eur 200 billion per year. the european union climate policy relies on global cooperation and next week’s conference of the parties – cop 24 – is decisive for the future of the paris agreement. in katowice the european union has a good story to tell. we have adopted all the legislation needed for implementing our paris contribution and our 2030 targets are projected to reduce greenhouse emissions by around 45% by 2030, which is the starting point for long-term planning. with the strategic vision that we adopted today we can also show our partners that we have started preparing for a climate-neutral economy fully in line with the paris agreement. it demonstrates our determination to shape together the best future for our planet, and this should inspire others to follow. in the months ahead our proposals and different pathways should be studied in detail to understand how the european union can best achieve a net-zero greenhouse gas emission economy in 2050 in a cost-effective way. we look forward to engaging with you, the european parliament, member states, national parliaments, business, ngos, cities, communities and our citizens on the eu’s long-term ambition and its contribution to the paris agreement. whatever the outcome of this debate, the european union objective needs broad and deep endorsement, not only by our decision-makers, by our legislators, but also by all stakeholders and citizens. these debates and discussions should allow the european union to adopt and submit an ambitious and broadly supported long-term strategy by 2020 to the unfccc. i look forward to continuing this essential work with you in the months to come.
i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in this. this plan is indeed timely, on the eve of the climate conference in katowice. we can now send a clear signal to the world that we in europe are taking our responsibility to put the paris agreement into practice. our group fully supports this plan. we support all the investments you envisage in research and development. they are, of course, crucial. we support your proposals, mr šefčovič, to create a battery alliance in europe to develop and produce batteries in europe itself. we also support your proposals to achieve carbon storage. you have referred to the chemical, steel and cement sectors, where this is of course essential. there are a number of areas where we can be even more ambitious. i refer to the transport. i am referring to aviation and shipping. aviation will be one of the biggest threats to our climate in the coming years. this sector is once again in danger of escaping and avoiding responsibility. the corsia negotiations (on the carbon offsetting and reduction scheme for international aviation) are in danger of being boycotted and eroded. we are counting on you, mr šefčovič and mr cañete, to stop this. finally, a word on the accompanying social measures. they are only realistic if we maintain the social support. workers whose jobs are threatened in the automotive industry or in coalfields should be assured that we will guide them in the transition to a low-carbon economy. we must keep a close eye on this dual aspect of this transition. i wish you the best of luck in katowice next week.
on behalf of the s&d group. – madam president, we can say ‘yes we can’. yes, we can take the right decision to go to a fully climate-neutral economy. this is one of the real ‘tinas’ – there is no alternative to taking this decision. but let me also be very clear. there are different pathways to get there and we need to change that as well. it is not just about greening the economy. it is about making a just transition to do so. we’ve witnessed the gilets jaunes in belgium, and more in france. we should not say these are climate sceptics. we should not say that. we need to make sure that moving in that direction – and we really need to do that – is done in such a way that takes everybody on board. i’m looking to my good colleague, ivo belet. i liked your speech and i hope that next time, if we vote on co2 car emissions or energy efficiency that we have your support to do so. that is really very clear because it is not good enough just to give speeches. we need to get results, and one of the results that we have to work on – and we need your support, commissioners – is: we need to change the governance in europe. we need to change the european semester, take ecological and social indicators into account and change the way we govern the european union. i sincerely hope that we will have the support of the epp on that as well.
over the past decades, the wto has helped to provide a stable and predictable framework for businesses around the world. the wto has helped to bring about significant growth in many economies. but the world is changing and unfortunately the wto has not always evolved with it. i support the mandate given to the commission by the european council in june this year to step up the pace and help the wto move into the 21st century. i am interested in two things. firstly, the role of the wto as a mediator in trade disputes must be strengthened. this can be done by adapting the rules of trade to the technological and economic developments of the last 20 years. there is also still too little emphasis on sustainability and social challenges. more flexibility may be part of the answer. in addition, we must ensure a level playing field for market players by tackling market-distorting subsidies. finally, i would like to stress that the current political trends in various parts of the world, notably the us and the situation in the uk, underline again and more than ever the need for a well-functioning and effective wto.
(parliament's brexit coordinator) on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, first of all, i would like to welcome the withdrawal agreement and the political declaration. i think that we can say easily today that both have been built around the principles of the european union – that means the integrity of the single market, that means the four freedoms which underpin that single market and that means the ability, the capacity of the european union to decide in an autonomous way. but i have to tell you, and i think it’s important in these debates, that also two other elements have been taken into account. the first are naturally the positions – maybe i have to say the red lines – of the uk government following the decision to end the membership of the european union. then, a second important point that underpins this agreement or these agreements, and that is something often underplayed and not fully understood, is the 1998 good friday agreement, though the european union is in fact not a direct party in this. the 1998 agreement was only possible among eu member states, between eu member states, and it shows, in my opinion, that the european union is about that, about reconciliation, about the end of violence, about peace. the result of all this is the withdrawal agreement, and i think the only and best agreement possible, considering these three elements that i have just described. in achieving this, i would like to thank, naturally, and congratulate – it’s not the first time that i’m doing this – michel barnier personally. he has done a tremendous job in close cooperation with the european parliament and with the brexit steering group, and this also gives me the possibility to congratulate all the members of the brexit steering group for the good cooperation beyond the lines of the political parties. i think that it worked very well. now, the result, michel, is an agreement that, in my opinion, does three things. the first is to minimise the disruption for both sides, in particular for the economy, and to allow for an orderly withdrawal and allow for a timeframe between two and four years to conclude the negotiations on the future relationship. the second result is to avoid a hard border on the island of ireland, to safeguard the good friday agreement and to secure peace. then the third thing – and i think this was the first priority of the european parliament – is to safeguard the rights of all citizens. here the european parliament has, with the help and with the support of our negotiators, significantly improved the agreement and the text. moreover, we are also committed – and i want to tell you this today – to ensuring that, before we start the consent procedure in the european parliament, we receive from our british counterparts the necessary guarantees that all these citizens’ rights will be fully implemented. i believe that this framework – withdrawal agreement and political declaration – provides for the possibility to establish in the future a really close relationship between the european union and the united kingdom and the basis, therefore, as you have seen in the agreement, in the political declaration, is an association agreement, as the european parliament has proposed from day one. why an association agreement, on which also the british government has agreed? well, it will be applicable, once concluded, in a provisional way after approval at the european level. secondly, it will be ratified in one round of ratification by the member states, so as to avoid – if i may say so – the complexity of, for example, our relationship with switzerland, which is based not on one association agreement, but on a patchwork of agreements. the brexit steering group, along with the whole european parliament, will further scrutinise the deal, the withdrawal agreement and the political declaration on the future relationship, as part of the consent procedure.
on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, parliament will, in this respect, look very carefully into how the so—called joint committee will work. we want to ensure that the decisions that are taken by the joint committee are legitimate and, where necessary, also require the approval of the parliament. i hope that, before we start the consent procedure, we can have an agreement with the council. let’s be clear that there will be no winners in this. i think that the departure of the uk from the union is a failure for everybody – for the uk and for the eu. last sunday – we have to tell our colleagues this – was a sad day for both. so let us make the best of it in the knowledge that in the near future there will be a new generation in britain who will – and i’m pretty sure about this – decide to come back into the great political european family. (applause)

the rapporteur. madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, in the european union, the main cause of work-related death is cancer. each year, 100 000 people die as a result of exposure to carcinogens in the course of their working lives. silent deaths, deaths that nobody talks about, diseases that are not the subject of large prevention campaigns. that is the importance of the second revision of the carcinogens and mutagens directive, which we are dealing with today. it is unacceptable that thousands of workers should lose their lives in order to gain it. i would like to thank the commissioner for having the courage to initiate the revision of this directive, which has been awaited for more than ten years, and also to thank the fictitious rapporteurs and the council, the presidency, which has been attentive to our work. a first phase has already been completed. tomorrow, with your approval, we will conclude a second stage which, together with the first, will help to save 100 000 lives in the union over the next 50 years. this text sets exposure limit values for six carcinogens. we wanted to be ambitious. with the mandate given to me by parliament, i have advocated the inclusion of diesel emissions, which affect millions of workers. i cannot list all the sectors concerned, there are so many: from construction, to road and rail transport, to mining and tunnelling. but what is important today is that we protect those who work better and better. the subject was sensitive, we negotiated at length, but here is the result. together we have succeeded in meeting this challenge: tomorrow, workers will be better protected, because we have included the fumes from diesel engines and we have set an exposure limit value that will protect workers. the protection of workers must be stepped up. science and technology are constantly evolving. we have undertaken a third revision of the directive, with the aim of completing it before the end of the parliamentary term. but beyond that, i am pleading again here in this house for the next commission to continue to work with the constant addition of substances to be controlled. we need a permanent review of this directive. europe cannot stop on such a good path. the health of many workers depends on it. in this report i also wanted to give the social partners back their fundamental role. to encourage the field to be proactive, the commission will publish the agreements on the osha agency for health and safety at work website. this is an opportunity for the social partners to establish a real culture of prevention in companies, ahead of legislative measures. i am also pleased to note that in october the chemical industry and the european trade unions, in a joint statement, asked the commission to include toxic substances for reproduction in this directive. this shows the willingness of the actors to move forward. as you will have understood, ladies and gentlemen, all these measures are not only essential to safeguard the health of workers wherever they may be in europe, but they are also beneficial for companies which have the same concern - ours - to protect those who run them every day. by supporting this text tomorrow, you will be protecting the health of millions of workers. by voting tomorrow, you're going to save thousands of lives.
member of the commission. – madam president, honourable members, once more i would like to thank the co-legislators for finding an agreement on a commission proposal that will help save many workers’ lives. however, this is not the end of our initiatives to fight occupational cancer. the third amendment to the directive, addressing five additional important substances and groups of substances, is on the way, and in that regard, i congratulate parliament on the adoption of ms agea’s report on the third batch. the interinstitutional negotiations can start now, which should make it possible for this further modification of the directive to be adopted before the end of the current term. as i said before, i am convinced that the three amendments which i was allowed to initiate in this commission have now set on track a regular process of updating the carcinogens and mutagens directive. that is a great achievement, for which i particularly wish to thank the three rapporteurs, claude rolin, marita ulvskog and laura agea, as well as the shadow rapporteurs. madam president, honourable members, before i conclude let me also tell you that my staff have started preparatory work on the inclusion of occupational exposure limit values for three additional priority substances, and, of course, we are continuing to develop this legislation on the basis of scientific evidence and in close cooperation with the social partners. i am sure you will agree with me that this is europe at its best – a europe that protects. thank you for all your work.
the rapporteur. madam president, the unanimous view that we have heard here is a strong political message, which shows the importance for the european parliament of the health and safety of workers in europe. many things have been said, but for me, when it comes to health and safety, the alpha and omega is prevention, information and awareness. this is also the role of occupational doctors, who play a vital role in the company and who must be aware of the gendered nature of cancer, as women and men are not affected in the same way. monitoring is essential, and so is monitoring, as we have already discussed in part 1, the cmd1. the involvement of the actors on the ground, in particular the social partners, must be continued. we have been able to do this work thanks to the commission, thanks to the council's attitude, but above all thanks to the fact that together with laura and marita, with the fictitious rapporteurs, we have been able to form a team, a team that is committed to this fundamental issue, and it works when it can be done in this way. madam commissioner, it is clear that we will have to continue to work to obtain this permanent revision of this directive, to ensure that protection is actually the most concrete. i am indeed, i was and i am still a trade unionist at heart and quite a few of my trade unionist friends asked me, when i put myself on a list for the european elections: claude what are you going to do there? well, i'm going to continue my job to defend the wage workers, to defend life in the corporate world, and i think that this, what we're voting on tomorrow, makes perfect sense. what if one day my grandchildren ask me: what did you do in the european parliament? well, i can tell them that together with you i have helped to give concrete meaning to a europe that protects, that protects health and that saves lives.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on agriculture, fisheries and rural development, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0173/97). the whole glyphosate debate has made one thing clear: the importance of greater transparency, the importance of independent research. we have stressed this from the outset. we are for empirically based policy based on objective science. the risk assessments of the european food safety authority, efsa, are crucial in this regard. i would like to stress that we must give the efsa the necessary tools to play the powerful role we expect from this authority. this proposal provides answers to these challenges and deserves our support. but at the same time i do recognize that the concern about, among other things, the timing of publication, that this concern is legitimate, that it must be answered. if we are not to slow down innovation in the food sector, we need to strike the right balance and make workable choices.
mr president, the pursuit of greater transparency is of crucial importance in modern political decision-making. we also see this in the development of plant protection products and foodstuffs. people want to know what's on their plate and where it ends up. they know that whatever considerations we base on make decisions that directly affect them in their daily lives. that is why i very much welcome the proposal from the european commission to make european risk assessment in the food chain more transparent. i do not need to remind you of the many citizens' initiatives on the lack of plant protection products, for example, to illustrate the deep concern among citizens about this issue. then we must listen to this concern. i think that's obvious. as policymakers, we have more than one responsibility. while full transparency is desirable in some cases, there are also certain procedures where total transparency can undoubtedly also entail risks. and this is one of them. because, colleagues, let's not kid each other. in global industry, there are always hijackers on the coast. competitors are lurking, and information is more powerful than ever. so if we want to support our own european companies and safeguard the jobs they provide, then i think the last thing we should do is to oblige them to immediately disclose all their secrets. moreover, they would achieve the opposite of what the citizens want, namely that companies no longer invest their revenues in research and development for safer products, with all the negative consequences that this entails. if we really want europe to be the world's leading innovator, we cannot do that to our companies in this field.
in writing. the risk assessments carried out by the european food safety authority (efsa) when evaluating potentially harmful substances often raise a lot of questions and are met with resistance from the public. more transparency on the procedures followed and the results of the investigation is absolutely necessary. and more transparency need not conflict with respect for the intellectual property rights of producers. that is why we support the proposals of the rapporteur, mr sommer. they're ambitious and yet balanced. we are in favour of this amendment because, as i said, there is an urgent need for a transparent investigation method, respecting the intellectual property and r & d efforts of the companies submitting dossiers to the efsa.

president, is the eurozone resilient to storms? can we handle another crisis? the european economy has been doing well for some time and the crisis seems to have been absorbed, although the fat also seems to be a bit of the soup. we're not going to hit the growth rate of 2017 this year. the threat of a trade war, the chaos of brexit. it's autumn, but the proverbial winter is upon us. remember, commissioners, when you said we'd fix the roof in the summer. well, we're running out of time. after the past crises with ireland, greece and others, there is now italy, which is trying to resist the agreed budgetary rules like a bad boy. as europe whistles back at italy, it will ultimately be the financial markets that will put the knife to italy's throat and force it back into the fold. despite all the well-intentioned political rhetoric, at the end of the day it is always the financial markets that determine the course and we as politicians are left to pursue. it is a matter of the credibility of the commission and the confidence of the citizens, and it is high time that we changed course on this matter. firstly, a monetary union can only work with clear budgetary rules. let's get that straight. the simpler they can be, the clearer they will be. secondly, countries in difficulty must be supported on financial markets by a credible european crisis mechanism. the esm must therefore become a genuine european monetary fund, in my view, a transparent mechanism with democratic control from the national parliaments and the european parliament. in this way, mr president, we will take matters into our own hands and the cockpit of eurozone policy will once again be in the hands of those who have been elected to it.
i would like to point out, mr president, that in the north and south of europe there is a very different view of this reform. in the north, they are happy that there is no common eurozone budget, because that will of course only lead to transfers. but in the south of france i hear ministers cheering that the first steps are being taken. what is it now? i see a europe that is sliding ever further towards a transfer union, a belgium xl, where we are always paying for the debts of the south and that does not work. each country is individually responsible for a budget that must be in order. each country must control its debt and keep its economy competitive. that is the only recipe for a sustainable european project. "blue card" is the answer. there is clearly no public support for more and more european rules, more and more european budgets, more and more european taxes. the european project is thus being further and further undermined. i am very glad that the northern countries, and particularly the netherlands, have been able to put the brakes on this matter, and thus prevent this vision, this direction, from being taken.
on behalf of the s&d group. – mr president, on behalf of the s&d group, i would like to request to refer the file back to the committee and start interinstitutional negotiations. (applause)
the rapporteur. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, it is now more than two and a half years since we were confronted in brussels with the terrible bombings. the bloody terrorist attacks in paris were still fresh in our memory. the images of untold human suffering and the testimonies of survivors have forever changed our view of the world. this report has become a visionary text with advice on all aspects of counter-terrorism. i would have liked to have presented some of them to you. the report proposes several new solutions. hate preachers and foreign funding of mosques are a problem. that is why i am proposing that a european blacklist be drawn up for hate preachers so that they can no longer roam europe. the financing of mosques and organisations by third countries which do not respect democracy, the rule of law and human rights should also be prohibited. several terrorist attacks in the eu were committed by individuals known to the judiciary. therefore, car rental companies should be able to exchange information such as booking or reservation data with security services for cross-checks with police databases. the commission should therefore examine how to set up a system which allows the verification of the identity of persons hiring vehicles, aircraft and boats. existing loopholes are also being sued. for example, there are no methodologies to measure the effectiveness of radicalisation projects. however, the eu budget until 2020 provides eur 314 million for such programmes. the commission is therefore asked to set up a service within the european commission to launch pilot projects and then to measure their effectiveness by scientific methods. the commission has also proposed to establish a list of all funds for deradicalization programmes. the problem of the sensitive treatment of victims of terrorism is also a painful one. the way in which they have had to beg for the financial interventions to which they were entitled is unacceptable. that is why we propose three things. first, a standard form for claiming compensation. secondly, clear obligations with deadlines for insurers, and thirdly, automatic pre-financing by the government shortly after the attack to cover immediate costs. i also drew inspiration from conversations with experts from home and abroad, including our own police and intelligence agencies. they pointed out to me a number of good practices in belgium which are also convincing at european level. this is the case, for example, of the local integrated security cells which jan jambon, as belgian minister of the interior, wanted to roll out throughout belgium. pilot projects have shown that these cells can be a successful tool to address potential violent radicalisation at municipal level through human-centred counselling. as far as brexit is concerned. the eu and the uk are highly interdependent in terms of security. therefore, arrangements should be made with the eu to ensure that the uk and the eu can continue to collect, share and analyse vital information on the fight against serious crime. i am very satisfied with the present text of this report. i therefore call on all members to vote in favour of this report. finally, i would like to thank the co-rapporteur, shadow rapporteurs, colleagues and experts for their contribution and assistance in the preparation of this report. i would also like to thank the secretariat of the special committee on terrorism for its unstinting commitment. last but not least, i would like to thank in particular the victims of the attacks for their courage to come forward and for their important input. i commend this report to them and their families. the report is the result of a titanic effort, made possible only by the cooperation of all of you. thanks again. remember, making europe safer is a team effort and a responsibility for all of us.
blue-card question. – i will speak flemish, so maybe you can listen to the translation. mevrouw, ik heb u horen zeggen dat u eigenlijk tegen het delen van informatie bent, dat u tegen informatie-uitwisseling bent omdat u vreest dat dan meer mensen gevolgd worden. zo kwam het over. ik wil u hier toch wel zeggen dat, als we alle aanslagen in europa bekijken, een of meerdere daders van elke aanslag in een of meerdere databanken zaten. dus als we effectief een echte europese inlichtingendienst zouden hebben en veel meer europese gegevens zouden uitwisselen, dan zouden we veel sterker staan. ik vraag mij dus af wat uw argumentatie is om te pleiten tegen meer gegevensuitwisseling.
i think we can say, mr president, that europe has indeed taken concrete measures against terrorism in recent months and years, but that, unfortunately, the work is not finished. we will have to remain very alert and work more closely together to protect people effectively and well. the key to this is - as arnaud danjean has just said - the exchange of information. that is essential. information must be exchanged automatically, continuously and immediately between national police services. it happens, but it can always be better. we must also build a better bridge between the national police services on the one hand and the national counter-terrorism services, the national intelligence services, on the other. secondly, as the commissioner has said, we must also combat radicalisation on the internet and also tackle terrorist propaganda there. europol is indeed the hub and the most appropriate instrument. it should be developed into that central european unit for the fight against digital terrorism, as the report says. finally, the commissioner also said we need to further tighten controls at the eu's external borders and the european border and coast guard must have direct access to all the information in the european databases. that is the only effective way to deal effectively with this scourge. if there is one area, ladies and gentlemen, in which we can prove that europe has added value, it is in this area, and it is our duty to put the measures contained in this report into action quickly and efficiently.
i would also like to thank all the members for their contribution to this debate. it's very important. it is not just the words of my colleague mrs hohlmeier that i will repeat, but i would like to join her in her words. i would like to comment briefly on what some colleagues have already said about the small member states, which do not have the capacity and the means to fight terrorism as successfully as large member states. that is why it is so important to cooperate at european level between all the member states. that's so important, because together we can do more than any of us can do alone. i really want to emphasize this. cooperation between national authorities and europol and eurojust. we already have those instruments. please let us use these instruments better, apply them more efficiently and improve where necessary. that is why we have included a great many recommendations in our report and i call on all countries to read that report carefully and to continue to develop. the ball is in your court now. we've done our job, but it's not finished yet. together we have the responsibility to fight the fight against terrorism, that fight that we all have to fight together at all different levels. i hope no one is evading responsibility. as regards the comments on the balance between security on the one hand and fundamental rights on the other. this is not a discussion for me. it's not an "either/or" story. it's a story of one and one. of course, there has to be a proper balance between security and human rights, and i think that in our report we have really tried to keep that balance very close. thank you for your attention. i call on you to support our report in the interests of the future, to prevent terrorist attacks in the future.
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, tomorrow we are going to vote on the largest bilateral trade agreement the eu has ever negotiated, the eu-japan economic partnership agreement. a trade area of up to 600 million consumers will be created, representing 40% of world trade. europe and japan believe in the power and prosperity of specialization and free trade, unlike us president trump, who prefers to sit on his island and worship protectionism. we choose to cooperate in more than 40 fields: the commission has already taken a number of initiatives in this area. why would you do that? because we think that in this globalized world, we need to work together more. because we are like-minded partners with the same values and standards, but also because we want to engage in dialogue on issues where we may not agree. we also call on japan to abolish the death penalty. we call on japan to ban whaling and to ban animal testing of cosmetics. this is a good agreement, ladies and gentlemen, good for our companies, good for our agriculture.
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, three years ago, the vast majority of you in this house, and you too, commissioner, were in favour of a mechanism called isds in trade agreements. yet today, you overwhelmingly agree that these isds were toxic and therefore no longer needed. what is the current state of sustainable development, social and environmental rights? once again, today in this house, a majority of you are convinced that these social and environmental rights should be optional in free trade agreements. i hope that you, madam commissioner, and you, my colleagues, will one day change your mind. why is it that in the eu-japan agreement all chapters are binding with sanctions, except for social and environmental rights? why is the union agreeing to ratify an agreement with a partner that is the main player in the illegal clearing of forests in south-east asia in the fight against deforestation? why are the ilo conventions on trade union rights not a prerequisite for these agreements? no, i don't think this deal is a new generation deal. i think this is a missed opportunity to integrate trade into a strategy that respects social and environmental law.
president, ladies and gentlemen, mrs mogherini, i have been following developments in ukraine for several years now, before, during and after the euromaidan, the revolution of dignity. you know what's always impressed me the most? these are my meetings - and you referred to them, mrs mogherini - with the very active and very conscious civil society. that middle ground was the engine of the revolution and still is. five years ago, since the euromaidan revolution began at the end of 2013, society itself made an important choice. a choice for a democratic model with respect for the rule of law. a choice for a european future. a choice for an association agreement with the eu. our great neighbour russia has immediately responded to this independent choice with a hybrid and open aggression, with annexation and occupation. and it's not stopping, with a direct confrontation in the sea of azov at this moment. despite this difficult context, the country has embarked on a path of difficult and far-reaching reforms. the eu has invested heavily in this: thank you very much, mrs mogherini. is it going to be thorough and fast enough to really change the face of the country, to eliminate all forms of corruption? i'm sure you didn't. it is always possible to do better, it is always possible to do it faster, but here again it is the civil society that is keeping its politicians in line and keeping alive the spirit of the euromaidan revolution. they are our greatest allies. it is important for them that the message be sent that we will never abandon them, that they will continue to enjoy our support, and i hope they will hear this. i'm going to the ukrainian slava!
in writing. the importance of this agreement has been further highlighted in recent weeks by the rising geopolitical tension with russia, but ukraine is on our radar for another reason. our poultry industry has been warning for some time about unfair competition. it's high time we took action. the trade part of the agreement with ukraine includes a customs tariff line which allows for the export of chicken breasts with skin and wing to europe. by processing that winged breast in dutch and slovak establishments into 'european' chicken breasts, the ukrainian poultry sector is circumventing the quotas that do exist for pure chicken fillets. it is simply a structural error in the agreement and it must be removed. the political pressure must be kept up to force the commission to revise the arrangement with the ukrainians with convincing arguments.
for the greens/efa group mr president, i note that this budget ceremony featured four men in dark suits, and i think that on budgetary matters, a little gender diversity would not hurt. this was not the main subject of my speech. mr president, pursuant to rule 190 (4) of our rules of procedure, i propose that the house postpone the vote on the two reports by mr pedro silva pereira on the ratification of the trade agreement with japan. you know, ladies and gentlemen, that free trade agreements are one of the fixing points of growing anti-european sentiment in europe and i think we could make the treaty with japan the prototype of free trade agreements of a new type: free trade agreements which would respect public services, avoid financial deregulation, safeguard the precautionary principle and above all make social and environmental standards mandatory and binding. if we are to make the eu-japan free trade agreement such a treaty, further discussions are needed with japan, and i therefore propose that you postpone this vote.
representing the s&d group. – madam president, it’s a little bit of an awkward situation, i have to say, and i think we’re all a little bit taken aback by what’s happened, so we can have a good but respectful debate today on a topic that is clear. in the sense, we asked for this topic before the so—called gilets jaunes, but of course it is in connection with that. last weekend, we had 75 000 people walking the streets of brussels to ask for more ambition with regard to climate change. and on the other hand, you have the gilets jaunes in france but also in parts of belgium. this could sound like a contradiction, two different manifestations, but actually it isn’t. it’s actually rather logical and understandable. people do understand the necessity and the urgency of fighting climate change, and they do demand urgent action of europe and the member states. but they cannot, they will not and they should not accept the fact that they are the only ones that will have to pay the bill for that: bad air quality, health risk, diminishing purchase power, job losses, unaffordable bills when it comes to energy, bad housing conditions. think about our polluting cars and, at the same time, the new electric cars unaffordable for so many people. these people are not climate sceptics, but maybe they will become climate sceptics if we do not make sure that we make a just transition. yes, we need a transition, but it will only be a good one when it is a just one. and for us, the s&d group, it is not just a necessity or a purely economic process of changing business sectors and securing competition. no, for us the social dimension is absolutely clear. yes, coal mines: they need to be shut down. and yes, we will have to get rid of the combustion engine. but not while at the same time making all these things unaffordable for families and putting territories or families into uncertainty or even poverty. and in that sense, i think we need coordinated action to make that a just transition. let me give you a few examples. first of all, just transition should be a key concept in all policy processes at all levels. second, we need a radical change in the governance of the european union. broadening the european semester with social and ecological indicators which have the same weight as the economic, the fiscal and the debt indicators –that is also key. investing in clean and sustainable industries and technologies and keeping these investments off the balance sheet, because otherwise we will not have enough room to make these investments. creating new specific funds for just transition and dedicating money in existing funds and programmes within the multiannual financial framework. and also making sure no investments go against these just transition principles. and to make a just transition successful, we need a profound social dialogue in this case. this should involve governments at all different levels, workers and their unions, employers, businesses and ngos to work and cooperate together. we have no alternative. we have to make this transition, but by making sure we do this in a just way, putting people and social values at the core of our sustainable agenda, we will be able to get everybody on board and turn this transition into a successful europe. the two most important challenges – and that’s my final word – that we are all facing are climate change and rising inequalities. let’s pull them together and make sure that we come into a concept of sustainable equality.
for the greens/efa group madam president, ladies and gentlemen, how can we dare to divide society into those who are successful and those who are nothing? many of you have written to me, especially from france, since the beginning of the yellow vest movement. in words that were often poignant, you told me of your anger at being treated as nothing, your disgust with our democratic institutions, your revolt against injustice in all its forms, especially tax, which these institutions perpetuate. you can't be taken for fools anymore. no, making the rich rich sorry, don't throw stones at the first in line doesn't benefit everyone. no, strengthening the power of multinationals - sorry, defending our great national champions - is not defending the public interest. none of you endorses the violence of the burglars, but all of you are speechless in the face of repression. and while the fuel tax is the spark of the revolt, i know you care about the need for our societies to respect the limits that nature has set for them. the ipcc is urging us to undertake a profound ecological transition without delay: we need our planet to be or remain habitable for all. but this transition can only be a solidarity transition. each one should do his share of the work according to his ability and each one should help according to his need. now, we know that half of the emissions are produced by the richest 10 percent. this ecological and solidarity-based transition must also be fully democratic, because no one has, on his own, the blueprint for the ideal, just and solidarity-based society, nor the gps to achieve it. finding the path requires us to mobilize our collective intelligence and creativity. so i would like to say that those who want to oppose citizens mobilized against injustice to those who mobilize to save our planet have absolutely understood nothing. between the end of the month and the end of the world, we do not have to choose, these two struggles are two sides of the same coin, that of the struggle for human dignity, that of all human beings present and future.
president. - ladies and gentlemen, it is not possible for the internet giants, often american giants, to come here to europe and make huge profits from our data without paying adequate taxes. so i agree that now is the time to modernize our tax rules, to adapt them to the digital age. but this can be done on the largest possible scale. if europe wants to tackle this on its own, our competitiveness may suffer and that is why we must look to the oecd first. now, if an international solution proves to be really impossible, then we must make joint arrangements in europe. in these reports we are voting on today there are some good ideas, but also some things that we simply do not agree with or that need more research to see what the impact will be on our economy. let me be very clear in closing. i do not want this issue to be used to fund the european union itself further. i do not want the european union to collect its own taxes. it is the member states that are responsible for their fiscal policies.
president, colleagues, google, amazon, microsoft, facebook: this is the first time that the european union has been involved in a global climate change conference. they all have a market value of over 400 billion dollars. apple even just passed the $1 trillion mark this summer. a trillion dollars, that's almost twice the gdp of my country, belgium. digital companies, colleagues, provide information services to the public. they create huge revenues without having to employ a lot of people. walmart, for example, employs 100 times more people than facebook, but is worth only half that. and who creates these huge profits? those innovative companies, sure, but at least we all do. we provide these companies with their most valuable production factor, which is data. in our completely outdated tax system, none of this is taken into account. it is therefore only logical, ladies and gentlemen, that we should tax them properly, and the only way to do that is if we do it together. how long are we going to let europeans play us off? if we can be united against the british, why can't we be united against google, for example? are our smes so worthless that we tax them on average almost twice as much as those multinationals? i'm not being naive. i also understand the considerations of many countries, including my own, but i firmly believe that if we really want to respond to all those people who are coming out to demand their share of the cake, injustices like this must be eliminated, and today rather than tomorrow.
the european parliament has a duty to ensure that the european union is able to take action in the fight against corruption and that the european union is able to take action in the fight against corruption. citizens want an active fight against corruption. citizens want a transparent government with full independence. citizens want a government free of conflicts of interest. citizens do not want their political leaders to see the european union as an atm machine, as a cash machine where money can be taken out, where business and political friends can be enriched. that, ladies and gentlemen, is the context in which this debate is taking place. how do we protect the financial interests of the european union in the czech republic? president, ladies and gentlemen, the annual report of the european ombudsman for 2017 is always an interesting read. this is how you find out what the bottlenecks are at european level. topics such as transparency, accountability and public access to information and documents, the culture of service provision and respect for procedural rights are at the forefront. other issues raised included ethical issues, public participation in the eu decision-making process, the proper use of discretion including infringement procedures, sound financial management of eu procurement, grants and contracts, recruitment and good management of issues relating to eu staff. in particular, i would like to congratulate the ombudsman on her inquiry into the treatment of persons with disabilities under the commission's common health insurance scheme and on the accessibility of the commission's webpages and online tools for persons with disabilities. the ombudsman should of course do all he can to ensure that the un convention on the rights of persons with disabilities is fully and consistently implemented by the eu administration. i also fully support the idea that citizens should be able to participate more directly in the democratic order of the eu, follow the decision-making process in the eu institutions in detail and have access to all relevant information in order to be able to fully exercise their democratic rights. that is the essence of democracy.
president, ladies and gentlemen, i fully support the recommendations in the report that the commission should exercise its powers properly in its role as guardian of the treaties, since that role is crucial to the functioning of the eu for its citizens. the logic is that infringement proceedings should be opened in an objective and neutral manner where necessary to put an end to situations in which eu law is not respected. i also thank the committee on petitions for having given its opinion in various contributions to parliamentary reports on various subjects such as the european accessibility act, the marrakesh convention to promote access to published works for the blind and visually impaired and the european disability strategy. the annual report also shows that access to public transport and the built environment and discrimination in the labour market pose the greatest problems for people with disabilities. this highlights the importance of the recently adopted european accessibility act, which member states now have to transpose into national law. hopefully, that will make a real difference in the field. furthermore, it seems to me that the member states are not doing enough to implement the 2000 employment equality directive. given that work is an important lever for the inclusion of people with disabilities in society, member states must urgently take action in this area.
